Foreword

Colombia presents its third Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC 3.0) to the Paris Agreement as a declaration of State and society. It reaffirms that climate action is a structural pillar for the country's present and future, as well as an ethical and political imperative toward future generations.

This commitment is grounded in the ecological and cultural character of the Political Constitution of 1991, which enshrines the protection of biological and cultural diversity as an obligation of the State and a duty of all society, especially through the recognition and strengthening of Indigenous Territories.

NDC 3.0 reaffirms that Colombia's climate action is guided by clear and orienting principles: organizing the territory around water; advancing a just energy and socioecological transition; protecting the Amazon and other strategic ecosystems; promoting just climate finance; strengthening environmental democracy and effective participation; integrating the agrarian reform and rural transformation agenda; and aligning with international frameworks for biodiversity, the Sustainable Development Goals and human rights.

Colombia undertakes this effort with full awareness of its national circumstances, but also of its responsibilities in the global response. In a context marked by the first Global Stocktake, our country presents this NDC as a progressive, ambitious and coherent contribution, which seeks to align with the 1.5°C target while asserting that the structural transformations required must be supported by a just, adequate and predictable international climate finance.

With this NDC, Colombia reiterates to the international community that climate action is a State policy and a national commitment. Advancing toward carbon neutrality and resilience by 2050 means protecting life, guaranteeing the dignity of people and affirming Colombia's place as a Megadiverse country that protects its natural heritage for humanity.

Irene Vélez Torres – Minister of Environment and Sustainable Development (i)

Introduction

Colombia presents its third Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC 3.0) under the Paris Agreement, as an expression of a State and societal commitment oriented toward consolidating a more just, ambitious and coherent climate action consistent with its national circumstances. This new cycle integrates the updating of mitigation and adaptation commitments, reaffirms long-term objectives and expands the scope of climate action to include loss and damage, and the means of implementation.

In accordance with Article 4 of the Paris Agreement, NDC 3.0 represents a step forward compared to Colombia's Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC 2.0) 2020–2030 (Republic of Colombia, 2020). Colombia ratifies its 2030 mitigation target and establishes more ambitious targets for 2035, aligned with a long-term trajectory toward net-zero emissions by 2050.

In a pioneering manner, this NDC includes an initial narrative on loss and damage, recognizing the urgency of addressing increasingly severe impacts. The preparation of this NDC is underpinned by a robust legal and institutional framework for climate action—Law 1931 of 2018, which established a legal basis for the National Climate Change System (SISCLIMA)—as well as a participatory process that involved indigenous peoples, black communities, peasantry, youth, civil society, the private sector, academia and different levels of government.

The presentation of NDC 3.0 occurs at a key moment, marked by the first Global Stocktake (GST). Colombia incorporates its recommendations and lessons learned to guide climate action toward the global target of limiting the temperature increase to 1.5°C, and to advance toward an equitable, resilient and sustainable development model.

Together, NDC 3.0 constitutes a more ambitious, progressive and coherent contribution to the structural transformation that Colombia must undertake to transition toward a resilient development model compatible with the global targets of the Paris Agreement.

Country Vision

The 1991 Constitution recognized the environment as an autonomous legal good, whose protection commits the State and society as a whole. The Constitutional Court has indicated that this protection requires transforming the relationship between human beings, societies and nature, moving beyond instrumental visions toward a model of mutual care and respect.

The Colombian State has made climate change a national priority. In 2017 it approved the National Climate Change Policy, and in 2018 Law 1931, which structured the National Climate Change System (SISCLIMA) as the hub for coordinating the actions needed to address this phenomenon. The ratification of the Paris Agreement in 2018 reinforced this commitment at the international level.

In this vision, the country recognizes the Indigenous peoples' Own Knowledge Systems as a principal category for guiding climate action, valuing their contribution to the construction of mitigation, adaptation and resilience strategies from an intercultural and territorial perspective, in full respect of their autonomy and right to self-determination.

Colombia's long-term climate objectives are:

Achieving carbon neutrality by 2050, reducing emissions by 90% compared to 2015 and offsetting the remaining 10% through carbon removals.

Reducing the territorial vulnerability of municipalities, departments and Indigenous Territories by decreasing loss and damage resulting from climate change through a comprehensive and multisectoral approach.

The Strategy proposes nine (9) transformative priorities and 195 ambition benchmarks, which synergistically integrate mitigation and adaptation, guiding the transition toward a more just, inclusive, resilient and low-carbon society.

Within this framework, three axes guide Colombia's vision toward mid-century:

Climate justice

Colombia reaffirms that its climate action is guided by climate justice as an ethical, political and social framework that orients the vision toward 2050. This axis recognizes that the benefits and costs of the transition must be distributed equitably across generations, genders, sectors and territories, ensuring that the most vulnerable people and communities are not left behind and that their rights, autonomy and perspectives are central to all climate decisions.

In this framework, climate justice implies reducing structural inequalities, protecting communities' livelihoods, and ensuring that climate action contributes to the protection of human rights, inclusion and social cohesion. It likewise recognizes the central role of women and youth; of indigenous peoples, black, Afro-Colombian, Raizal and Palenquero communities; of the peasantry; and of local communities and persons with disabilities, acknowledging their contributions, their specific vulnerabilities and their rights in the face of the climate crisis.

Climate justice frames climate action within the commitments of the Paris Agreement and the orientations of the Global Stocktake (GST), reaffirming that NDC 3.0 constitutes not only a technical commitment, but a political commitment to intergenerational and territorial equity.

Transición Justa

The just transition is the process of socio-ecological, energy and economic transformation that carries the principles of climate justice into the structural transformations the country needs to meet its climate and development targets. This process guides changes in the economy, the world of work, production, consumption and the relationship with the territory and nature.

This axis contemplates differentiated labor transitions: for those who are leaving sectors, for those who remain in transforming sectors, and for new generations entering sustainable sectors, promoting skills certification, social mobility and access to decent employment with social protection.

Black, Afro-Colombian, Raizal and Palenquero peoples; and the peasantry; and local communities, elderly persons and populations that have historically faced structural inequalities and that today present greater vulnerability to climate impacts.

It is conceived as a territorialized strategy, which recognizes regional vocations and strengthens economic diversification, reindustrialization and solidarity and popular economies. It also links with peacebuilding, territorial equity and social cohesion, recognizing the limitations of a single template and the need to adapt processes to local realities.

International Agenda Alignment

Colombia projects its NDC 3.0 as an instrument that consolidates coherence between climate action, biodiversity conservation and sustainable development, within the framework of the triple planetary crisis of climate change, biodiversity loss and pollution.

In this sense, Colombia reaffirms its commitment to a structural synergy between climate action, the fight against deforestation and biodiversity conservation, in coherence with the Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD), the Kunming-Montreal Global Biodiversity Framework and the Escazú Agreement.

Colombia also underscores that this synergy requires a coherent international financial system that supports it effectively, avoids double counting and does not impose restrictions on investments in biodiversity that generate direct climate benefits. Such a system is expected to recognize the multiple benefits of the country's conservation actions and ensure that the finance provided is additional and transformative.

NDC 3.0 is conceived equally as an instrument that contributes directly to the 2030 Agenda, particularly to the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) of an environmental character (2, 6, 7, 13, 14 and 15). This alignment ensures that climate policies are conceived as part of a broader framework for sustainable development, in which environmental, social and economic dimensions advance in an integrated and synergistic manner.

Likewise, NDC 3.0 is inscribed within the framework of international obligations on human rights, in accordance with Advisory Opinion OC-32/23 of the Inter-American Court of Human Rights, which identifies the progressive elimination of fossil fuels as part of mitigation duties. In this context, Colombia promotes fossil fuel phase-out as an ethical and political commitment, articulating this principle with its long-term energy transition strategy.

In this way, international agenda alignment constitutes an axis of Colombia's vision toward 2050, ensuring that climate action, biodiversity and sustainable development advance jointly and inseparably, for the benefit of present and future generations.

1. Principles and Approaches

1.1 Guiding Principles

Water as the Axis of Life and Environmental Justice

Colombia recognizes that water constitutes the structural axis of its territorial planning and a pillar for achieving environmental justice. Advancing toward territorial organization around water means recognizing ecological limits and the centrality of the hydrological cycle in sustaining life, biodiversity and the socioeconomic processes that the country needs.

The National Development Plan incorporates this principle by stating that 'if the territory is not organized around water, human activity will not be sustainable.' The hydrosocial cycle, as the foundation of biodiversity, of the processes of settlement and occupation of the territory and of productive systems, must be central to all climate decisions.

Water is a common good that articulates biodiversity and cultures. Around this principle, governance schemes are consolidated that are expressed in bodies such as Territorial Councils, which enable the identification and management of socio-environmental conflicts, and promote nature-based solutions, planning based on rights and watershed management under the water-territory principle.

Thus, the fundamental role of Indigenous Governments in relation to the care and protection of water is recognized, as are the processes of indigenous environmental territorial planning based on the rights of autonomy and self-determination—recognized in Colombia's legal order through ILO Convention 169 and Law 21 of 1991, as well as through Law 1955 of 2019 and Decree-Law 0488 of 2025.

Organizing the territory around water also invites going beyond the limits and barriers of existing instruments, and analyzing the territory in a comprehensive manner, incorporating the management practices implemented by indigenous peoples; Black, Afro-Colombian,

Raizal and Palenquero communities; the Peasantry; and Local Communities. This principle guides prospective and consensual planning that responds to the country's ecosystemic and biocultural characteristics, generating equity and contributing to overcoming historical inequalities in access, quality and management of water and hydro-resources.

Just Energy Transition: A Path Without Fossil Fuels

Colombia assumes the just energy transition as a guiding principle of its climate action. The country has made historic decisions, including the suspension of new hydrocarbon exploration contracts, the progressive reduction planning of fossil fuel use, the construction of scenarios for an energy transition with social justice, and the expansion of renewable energy generation, in a democratic and territorially distributed manner.

These steps must be accompanied by a transition that goes beyond the labor and productive sphere, and drives a cultural and civilizational transformation that reimagines energy as a common good, profoundly connected with land, water, wind, sun and human labor. This presupposes overcoming a model centered on fossil fuel extraction and advancing toward a model based on life, in which energy sovereignty and access to clean energy are rights of the territories and communities.

As part of its commitment to global climate justice, Colombia articulates this principle with the orientations of the first Global Stocktake (GST) and with international work programs on just transition, and reaffirms that the progressive abandonment of fossil fuels is not only a climate imperative, but a political and ethical commitment aligned with its national development model.

Amazon: Heart of Climate Action

Colombia recognizes that the Amazon is the heart of its climate action and an essential heritage for humanity and for its inhabitants, especially indigenous peoples, including those in voluntary isolation or initial contact. The protection of the Amazon rainforest constitutes a global priority that Colombia assumes with full conviction, recognizing that its conservation is inseparable from climate objectives and the rights of its peoples.

This principle guides the country toward ways of inhabiting the forest that transcend extractivism, recognizing collective rights, life plans and the autonomy of peoples, and integrating the cultural and political concepts developed by Amazonian indigenous peoples, as well as the concept of the Rights of Nature, which recognizes non-human living beings as subjects of rights.

The Amazon is also a cultural and political pillar: a reference for international cooperation and for the construction of alternatives to the civilizational crisis. Protecting it implies transforming the logics of investment and territorial planning toward perspectives that recognize its intrinsic value, its function as a climate regulator and its role as a global common good.

Within this framework, the importance of ensuring comprehensive protection of the territories of indigenous peoples in Voluntary Isolation and Initial Contact, and their role as forest protectors, is recognized in order to achieve effective environmental justice.

With this principle, the country ratifies that the ecological and cultural integrity of the Amazon is decisive for fulfilling its NDC, advancing toward carbon neutrality and contributing to the global target of limiting the temperature increase below 1.5°C, preserving the biological and cultural wealth of this territory for present and future generations.

Just Climate Finance

The country understands just climate finance to be that which is decentralized, transparent and accessible in a timely manner to territories and communities, especially indigenous peoples, black, Afro-Colombian, Raizal and Palenquero communities, the peasantry and local communities, as well as women, youth and vulnerable populations. This implies moving beyond traditional instruments and advancing toward models of climate governance that recognize the self-governance capacity of the territories.

In line with such decentralization, NDC 3.0 highlights Indigenous Territories as territorial entities recognized in the country's legal order, which allows them—pursuant to their own governance structures and autonomy, in implementation of Decree-Law 0488 of 2025—to participate effectively in climate finance planning, programming, execution and accountability.

Colombia has positioned justice in financing as a priority, underscoring that countries with greater historical responsibilities must provide concessional resources and in the form of grants. At the same time, the country promotes the mobilization of international and national financial resources, including domestic public finance, private finance and innovative financial instruments, under principles of additionality, transparency and predictability.

The country reaffirms that international cooperation in climate matters must recognize national priorities and sovereignty over the management of resources and common goods. Just financing implies strengthening national and territorial capacities to administer and ensure that these resources reach the territories that need them most, under principles of transparency, accountability and cultural adequacy.

With this principle, NDC 3.0 projects that justice in financing is a pillar for ensuring equity in climate action, strengthening the country's resilience and contributing to a more balanced global effort in the face of the climate crisis.

Environmental Democracy and Escazú

Colombia recognizes environmental democracy and effective participation as substantive principles of its climate action. NDC 3.0 affirms that facing the climate crisis requires decisions built with the territories, respecting the autonomy of peoples and the historical exercises of territorial governance carried out by indigenous peoples, black communities, peasantry and local communities.

This principle is expressed in the respect and strengthening of rights of participation, access to information and environmental justice; in the comprehensive protection of environmental leaders and defending communities; and in the guarantee of Free, Prior and Informed Consent (FPIC), prior consultation and autonomous participation in the design, implementation and monitoring of climate measures. Indigenous peoples and ethnic communities. Environmental democracy implies transparent climate action,1

In this framework, the Escazú Agreement constitutes a fundamental instrument that reinforces the principles of environmental democracy, by consolidating binding standards on access to information, public participation and environmental justice. Its implementation contributes to strengthening climate governance, ensuring accountability and protecting the rights of environmental defenders.

With this principle, NDC 3.0 projects that environmental democracy and effective and participatory engagement are necessary conditions for consolidating the just transition, territorializing climate measures and ensuring that climate action is built from and with the territories, in harmony with diverse knowledge systems and the rights of peoples.

Territorial Justice and Rural Transformation for Climate Action

Colombia recognizes that Agrarian Reform, the Comprehensive Rural Reform and the strengthening of the peasant, family, ethnic and community economy (ACFEC) constitute essential pillars for climate action, the just transition and the construction of territorial peace. These structural transformations recognize that sustainable land management, food sovereignty and care for ecosystems are interdependent and must advance together.

The peasantry, recognized as subjects of special constitutional protection (Rulings SU-075 of 2018 and T-210 of 2025, Legislative Act 01 of 2023 and Article 64 of the Constitution) and as a central actor in Agrarian Reform and the Comprehensive Rural Reform in the Peace Agreement, plays a fundamental role in food production, territorial care and the preservation of ancestral knowledge related to climate change management.

In this sense, the environmental dimension of the peasantry and its productive practices is explicitly recognized.

This principle is grounded in the promotion of agroecology as a fundamental strategy for climate mitigation and adaptation, in the protection of functional biodiversity and in the consolidation of productive systems that articulate scientific knowledge with local and traditional knowledge. It also promotes the development of alternative sustainable economies that value the contribution of the peasantry to the care of nature.

The peasant, family, ethnic and community economy is conceived as an engine of climate action, by strengthening local capacities and the communities' and peoples' own and traditional productive practices to generate sustainable alternatives for production, consumption and marketing that protect ecosystems and reduce vulnerability to climate change.

With this principle, Colombia reaffirms that the productive, social and environmental strengthening of rural territories—in connection with the challenges of peace and social and agrarian justice—constitutes an effective path for facing the climate crisis and advancing toward a horizon based on life, equity and cultural diversity.

Food Sovereignty and Soil Protection: The Basis for the Regeneration of

life

Colombia recognizes food sovereignty as a fundamental right and an essential pillar of its climate action, aimed at guaranteeing access, production and consumption of healthy, sustainable and culturally appropriate foods. To secure this right for present and future generations, the country promotes the transition toward productive systems that are diverse, resilient, agroecological and compatible with ecosystem conservation.

This principle is grounded in the sustainable management and defense of protection areas for food production, promoting territorial planning policies that prioritize food use, prevent their degradation and avoid their conversion to activities incompatible with food security and sovereignty.

As an integral part of this principle, sovereignty over genetic resources and a fair and equitable sharing of the benefits derived from their use are guaranteed, ensuring national autonomy in the conservation, access and utilization of biological diversity, in accordance with the International Treaty on Plant Genetic Resources for Food and Agriculture and the Convention on Biological Diversity.

In this way, Colombia reaffirms that protecting agricultural land, strengthening food sovereignty and conserving genetic resources are strategic and convergent actions that sustain the regeneration of territories and the guarantee of the right to a dignified life and collective well-being.

Common but Differentiated Responsibilities and Respective Capabilities

Colombia reaffirms its commitment to the principle of common but differentiated responsibilities and respective capabilities (CBDR-RC), which constitutes the foundation of the international climate regime and a pillar of equity in climate action. This principle recognizes that industrialized countries have a greater historical responsibility in the accumulation of greenhouse gas emissions and, therefore, must lead the effort and provide greater support to developing countries.

This guiding principle implies that climate action is built from a comprehensive perspective, in which national responsibilities are accompanied by an urgent call for just, sufficient, predictable and differentiated climate finance, that guarantees the adaptation of the most vulnerable territories, the transfer of technologies and the development of capacities in developing countries.

In this way, NDC 3.0 represents not only a national commitment, but also a legitimate claim for global climate justice, where economies with greater historical responsibility and greater capabilities must lead the effort, freeing space for countries of the Global South to advance in their development and just transition.

1.2 Cross-Cutting Approaches

Colombia's NDC 3.0 integrates cross-cutting approaches that recognize that the climate crisis is an environmental, social, cultural and rights challenge. These approaches make historical inequalities visible, value the diversity of subjects and territories, and guide decisions toward transformations that are just, inclusive and respectful of rights.

Together, the gender approach, the intergenerational approach, the differentiated ethnic approach, the peasant sociocultural approach, the human rights approach and the approach to peacebuilding with nature provide conceptual frameworks and guiding criteria for the design of mitigation and adaptation measures, the implementation of climate finance and the monitoring of commitments.

1.2.1 Ethnic Differential Approach

Indigenous Peoples of Colombia

Indigenous peoples constitute an essential part of the Colombian social, diverse and plural State, and participate in the construction of a just and intercultural climate action. They are recognized as collective rights holders, holders of special jurisdiction, authority and own governance, in accordance with the international law on human rights, the Colombian legal order, ILO Convention 169 and the constitutional mandates that recognize their territorial autonomy and own knowledge systems.

The historical struggles of indigenous peoples have given rise to legal and political achievements that are today pillars of the State: Law 21 of 1991, which ratified ILO Convention 169, guarantees the right to Free, Prior and Informed Consultation (FPIC) and to effective participation in decisions affecting them. Law 1381 of 2010 protects native languages. Legislative Acts 01 of 2003 and 02 of 2015 strengthened indigenous territorial entities and their participation in governance. And Decree-Law 0488 of 2025 recognized Indigenous Territories as territorial entities for the purpose of channeling climate finance.

Indigenous peoples inhabit diverse territories and territorialities: legally constituted resguardos, untitled ancestral territories and urban spaces with significant presence. Titled resguardos represent 29.7% of the country's continental area, with a strong presence in the Amazon, where their collective management constitutes an essential climate solution.

In Colombia there are 115 indigenous peoples who, despite their significant contributions to conservation, figure among the most vulnerable to climate change impacts. This situation reinforces the urgency of strengthening their capacities and guaranteeing their full participation in the construction of public policies, as subjects of rights and not merely consultation objects.

In the same way, indigenous peoples contribute from their Territories in a differential and effective manner in climate change mitigation, adaptation and resilience, and for this reason they require a greater commitment from the International Community, the Colombian State and society to strengthen their territorial governance and their own strategies.

Black, Afro-Colombian, Raizal and Palenquero Communities

Black, Afro-Colombian, Raizal and Palenquero communities in Colombia constitute a differentiated ethnic group with their own cultures, customs and forms of organization based on an ancestral worldview that guides their relationship with the territory. For these communities, the territory is not merely a physical space, but a living being, a cultural, spiritual and collective heritage that sustains their identity and their forms of life.

The achievements of these communities have been decisive for the recognition of their rights. Law 70 of 1993, the fruit of historical mobilization and resistance, established the right to collective ownership of their territories. Decree 1745 of 1995 regulated this law and recognized Community Councils as the highest form of collective organization and territorial governance. Additionally, Decree-Law 4635 of 2011 established specific measures for victims of the armed conflict belonging to these communities.

The country recognizes, however, that these communities figure among the most vulnerable to climate change impacts. Despite their key role in conservation and climate action, they face structural exclusions that limit their participation in decisions and their access to climate finance.

NDC 3.0, built jointly with Black, Afro-Colombian, Raizal and Palenquero Communities, assumes that commitment and recognizes that their ancestral knowledge and cultural practices are essential for facing the climate crisis and biodiversity loss. This commitment is expressed in the integration of their perspectives in the NDC's objectives and measures.

i) valuing their ancestral knowledge in mitigation and adaptation; ii) consolidating collective territories as strategic spaces for conservation and climate resilience; and iii) promoting an approach that integrates culture, territory and biodiversity, from autonomous and owned-rights-based planning and governance.

Furthermore, following the principle of common but differentiated responsibilities, developed countries must contribute proportionally by assuming the costs of adaptation for African and Afro-descendant countries and peoples.

1.2.2 Peasant Socio-Cultural Approach

In Colombia, the peasantry is recognized as subjects of special constitutional protection and holders of collective rights. Their role transcends food production: through their practices and knowledge transmitted across generations, they exercise fundamental guardianship of ecosystems, contributing to biodiversity conservation, water regulation, soil care and the maintenance of essential environmental services for the country.

This approach and recognition of their territorialities guides climate action toward the protection of territories as spaces of life, where mitigation, adaptation and access to climate finance are articulated with the guarantee of collective rights and with environmental justice. Incorporating the peasant sociocultural approach in NDC 3.0 implies recognizing and strengthening their own knowledge, ancestral practices and forms of territorial governance as essential contributions to climate action.

The country has advanced along this path through instruments such as the creation of Peasant Reserve Zones (Law 160 of 1994), and with the explicit recognition of the peasant economy in the National Development Plan 2022–2026. These advances demonstrate that Colombia has bases, still being consolidated, for a climate action that recognizes and strengthens the peasantry as essential agents of the territorial and ecological transition.

The incorporation of this approach in NDC 3.0 represents a significant step toward recognizing the peasantry as an essential actor of climate action. It implies promoting their full, effective and binding participation in the formulation, implementation and monitoring of mitigation and adaptation measures, as well as in the management of climate finance, under principles of autonomy, equity and cultural adequacy.

1.2.3 Intergenerational Approach

Children, Adolescents and Youth (NNAJ)

Colombia recognizes that children, adolescents and youth (CAY) are rights holders and key actors in climate action. This approach is grounded in the Convention on the Rights of the Child, General Comment 26 on children's rights and climate change, and the Paris Agreement, which recognizes the role of youth in climate action.

The CAY approach guides climate action toward the protection and realization of rights, recognizing that mitigation and adaptation measures must respond to their specific vulnerabilities, but also enhance their capacities and their power of influence. Incorporating this perspective in NDC 3.0 means designing more effective climate measures, with genuine intergenerational equity and accounting for the needs and contributions of younger generations.

Colombia has advanced along this path through an interinstitutional process led by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development and co-led by UNICEF, Fondo Acción, LCOY, La Plataforma and Save the Children, with the support of sectoral allies and the participation of CAY. This process resulted in specific recommendations for the design, implementation and monitoring of NDC 3.0 measures.

With this, NDC 3.0 more clearly integrates the contributions of CAY in its objectives and measures, consolidating transparency and accountability mechanisms such as public dashboards with disaggregated data, child-sensitive indicators and participation and protection protocols for CAY in coherence with international standards on children's rights.

Furthermore, the country recognizes the differential right to indigenous peoples' own education, implemented through the Indigenous Own Education System – SEIP, incorporated in the Colombian legal order (Decree-Law 0481 of 2025). This recognition ensures that training processes are appropriate to the cultural and territorial realities of indigenous peoples, including climate dimensions in their own educational processes.

1.2.4 Gender Approach

Colombia recognizes that the climate crisis is not gender neutral. The historical and structural inequalities that have particularly affected women and diverse persons are also expressed in vulnerability to climate change and in limitations of access to resources, participation in decision-making, justice and adaptation opportunities.

The gender approach in climate action is assumed as:

An analytical tool that makes visible discriminations and inequalities in specific contexts.

A transformative framework that guides toward more equitable and democratic social relations.

A principle of legitimacy for public decisions on mitigation, adaptation and resilience.

Integrating this perspective ensures that climate measures respond to diverse realities, promote social justice and strengthen the resilience of communities and territories. Colombia has advanced in the institutionalization of this approach through instruments such as the Gender Action Plan and the Guide for incorporating the gender approach in sectoral and territorial climate management plans.

NDC 3.0 reaffirms this commitment and advances in the full and effective inclusion of women in all their diversity—rural, indigenous, peasant, black, Afro-Colombian, Raizal and Palenquero women—as well as persons with diverse sexual orientations, expressions and gender identities, in the processes of design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of climate measures.

It also seeks to strengthen equality in access to resources, opportunities and benefits derived from climate action.

With this, Colombia demonstrates that equity and sustainability can and must advance jointly, projecting the country as a regional reference in the integration of gender and climate action, and contributing to consolidating climate justice as a guiding principle of the transitions underway.

1.2.5 Human Rights Approach

The human rights (HR) approach in NDC 3.0 is conceived as a cross-cutting pillar that guides climate action. It recognizes that the climate crisis is not only an environmental challenge, but a direct threat to life, health, water, food, housing and the self-determination of peoples.

This approach guides climate action toward inclusion, equity and transparency, recognizing that mitigation and adaptation measures have differential impacts and must contribute both to the guarantee of collective and individual rights. By mainstreaming principles such as universality, equality, progressive realization and non-regression, climate measures are better designed and more legitimate.

With this approach, NDC 3.0 reinforces the articulation between entities with competencies in climate change and human rights, recognizes communities as rights holders and as co-managers of sustainability, and projects climate action in terms of justice and dignity. This consolidates it as a process that goes beyond technical management and commits the State to protecting human beings and nature.

1.2.6 Peacebuilding with Nature Approach

Colombia understands that peacebuilding and climate action are inseparable processes. After decades of armed conflict, the country recognizes that violence disproportionately affected the most vulnerable communities and also nature: rivers, forests, páramos and entire ecosystems were victims of the conflict. Rebuilding peace, therefore, implies repairing and caring for both human and natural life.

This approach projects peace as reconciliation between communities and territories, and as a pact of care with the nature that sustains life. It recognizes the essential role of indigenous peoples, black, Afro-Colombian, Raizal and Palenquero communities, the peasantry, women, youth and other historically excluded actors as agents of the territorial peace and ecological transition.

Colombia's NDC 3.0 incorporates this approach through a Peace and Climate Action methodology that mainstreams principles of human rights, just transition and environmental justice in all its dimensions. This methodology is articulated with the Final Peace Agreement (2016) and the Total Peace Policy (Law 2272 of 2022), ensuring that climate measures contribute to the construction of a stable and lasting peace, and relies on metrics and indicators that permit monitoring progress.2

Colombia's NDC 3.0 constitutes the first national contribution to systematically integrate a peace framework in its design under the UNFCCC, through a Peace and Climate Action methodology that allows this approach to be mainstreamed in all its dimensions. This framework constitutes a robust proposal for other countries emerging from or managing complex socio-environmental conflicts.

2. National Circumstances

2.1 National Context

Colombia, located in northwestern South America, has a territory of 2.07 million km² — 1.14 million km² continental and 0.93 million km² maritime — extending between the Pacific Ocean and the Caribbean Sea (as part of the Atlantic Ocean). Its geography includes three Andean mountain ranges, broad plains, two coastlines and one of the greatest water availabilities on the planet, which sustains one of the world's highest levels of biodiversity. This physical-biotic configuration, at the same time that it confers great natural wealth, exposes the country to a wide diversity of climate risks.

With an estimated population of 52.7 million inhabitants for 2024 and an urbanization rate exceeding 76%, Colombia is a multi-ethnic and multicultural country, with a significant presence of indigenous peoples, Black, Afro-Colombian, Raizal and Palenquero communities, and peasantry. It is organized as a unitary and decentralized State, with articulation among the national government, departments, municipalities and Indigenous Territorial Entities. The Colombian economy combines sectors intensive in natural resources — particularly hydrocarbons, mining and agro-industry — with manufacturing and services sectors.

The base information and official figures on geography, biodiversity, demography, infrastructure and climate risks are developed in Colombia's First Biennial Transparency Report (BTR1, 2024), submitted to the UNFCCC and available at: https://unfccc.int/documents/645256.

2.2 Institutional Arrangements

According to Colombia's BTR1 (2024, section 2.1.3), the institutional and legal arrangements supporting the implementation and monitoring of the NDC are grouped into five categories: normative, planning, management, economic/financial and information instruments. This comprehensive framework articulates public policy, regulation and institutions around climate action in the country.

In the normative sphere, Colombia's climate architecture rests on the Political Constitution of 1991 — recognized as an 'Ecological Constitution' — and is consolidated through specific legal developments. Among these, Law 1931 of 2018 stands out, which elevated to legal status the National Climate Change System (SISCLIMA), the Intersectoral Climate Change Commission (CICC) and the Regional Climate Change Nodes (NRCC), entities initially created by Decree 298 of 2016. This law also established the National Climate Change Council as a plural consultation space. Additionally, the National Development Plan 2022–2026 (Law 2294 of 2023) integrates climate change management across all sectors, including the development of a National Climate Finance Strategy (ENFC).

From an institutional standpoint, SISCLIMA constitutes the basis of the country's climate governance. At the national level, it is coordinated by the Intersectoral Climate Change Commission (CICC), supported by advisory committees and technical groups. At the regional level, the Regional Climate Change Nodes (NRCC) promote climate action in the territories. Law 1931 also created the National Climate Change Council as a plural consultation space that articulates the State, the private sector, academia, civil society and ethnic peoples. The Ministries of Environment and Sustainable Development, Finance and Public Credit, and National Planning, together with IDEAM, UNGRD and sectoral ministries, lead implementation.

These arrangements are complemented by planning instruments such as the National Climate Change Policy (2017), the Long-Term Climate Strategy E2050 (2021), and the Comprehensive Territorial and Sectoral Climate Change Management Plans (PIGCCT and PIGCCS). These instruments enable the alignment of climate action with development plans, territorial planning instruments, Life Plans and Indigenous Territorial Environmental Planning Instruments or their equivalents, strengthening territorial governance.

In the area of information management, the National Climate Change Information System (SNICC), regulated by Resolution 1383 of 2023, stands out; its objective is to provide transparent and consistent data and information over time for decision-making in climate change management in Colombia. The SNICC is integrated into the Environmental Information System for Colombia (SIAC) and constitutes a pillar for fulfilling transparency commitments under the Paris Agreement.3

Institutional Arrangements for Adaptation

The normative and policy architecture supporting adaptation in Colombia has been described in BTR1 (2024, section 3.1.6), where it is noted that climate management in the country is comprehensive, multi-level and decentralized. Within this framework, Decree 298 of 2016 establishes that one of the main policy instruments through which SISCLIMA acts is the National Climate Change Adaptation Plan (PNACC), which constitutes the central axis of adaptation planning in Colombia. According to BTR1 (2024), the Adaptation Plan is reviewed and updated periodically to adjust to new climate scenarios and challenges.

As a complement to this planning framework, Colombia has the Information System for Risk Management and Climate Change Adaptation (SIIVRA), which forms part of the National Climate Change Information System (SNICC). Within this framework, SIIVRA constitutes a key component of the adaptation monitoring and evaluation system, by contributing to the availability and consistency of information required for monitoring adaptation progress and reporting to the UNFCCC.

In summary, Colombia's institutional architecture combines a robust legal framework, a multi-level governance system (SISCLIMA) and a set of planning, management, information and financing instruments. This scaffolding constitutes the basis for implementing NDC 3.0, while at the same time highlighting the importance of continuing to advance in regulation, inter-institutional coordination and the strengthening of territorial capacities for effective climate action.

2.3 Emissions Inventory

Greenhouse Gas Emissions

BTR1 (2024) of Colombia included the National Inventory of Greenhouse Gas Emissions and Removals (1990–2021), as well as estimates of criteria pollutants and black carbon (2010–2021), available in the technical document annexed to BTR1.

Additionally, the country submitted the Common Tabular Formats (CTF) as an integral part of the First Biennial Transparency Report (BTR1, 2024), available on the official UNFCCC website: https://unfccc.int/first-biennial-transparency-reports.

The estimates are prepared based on the 2006 Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) Guidelines, their 2019 refinements and the Guide for the Estimation of Air Pollutant Emissions Inventory (EMEP/EEA, 2019). Likewise, reporting is adjusted to the Modalities, Procedures and Guidelines (MPG) of the Enhanced Transparency Framework (ETF) of the Paris Agreement, in accordance with Decision 18/CMA.1 and Decision 5/CMA.3.

Colombia does not report indirect N₂O and CO₂ emissions from sources other than the agriculture and forestry, land use and land-use change (LULUCF) sectors.

These inventories make it possible to assess the impact of human activities on the atmosphere, identifying sectors, sources and activities that emit and absorb greenhouse gases (GHG), criteria pollutants and black carbon. This aims to guide effective mitigation policies, strategies and measures, as well as to promote the adoption of better technologies, practices and processes, based on scientific data for decision-making.

In 2021, net emissions were 280,101.98 kt CO₂eq, compared to 222,664.72 kt CO₂eq in 1990, representing an increase of 25.80% (see Figure 1).

Carbon dioxide (CO₂): net emissions grew 9.90% between 1990 and 2021, from 169,698.39 kt CO₂eq to 186,498.10 kt CO₂eq, mainly due to deforestation, burning of fossil fuels and biomass, and industrial development. Methane (CH₄): Increased 70.62%, from 44,398.45 kt CO₂eq in 1990 to 75,752.29 kt CO₂eq in 2021, associated with enteric fermentation, manure management and wastewater treatment.

Nitrous oxide (N₂O): Grew 57.55%, from 8,526.24 kt CO₂eq in 1990 to 13,433.41 kt CO₂eq in 2021, due to land management practices such as the use of nitrogenous fertilizers, urine from grazing animals and crop residues.

Fluorinated gases (HFC, PFC, SF₆): In 1990 only SF₆ was reported (41.70 kt CO₂eq). Since 2001, HFCs and PFCs have been incorporated, reaching 4,418.16 kt CO₂eq in 2021. These emissions are related to the use of substances that substitute ozone-depleting substances (ODS), mainly as refrigerants in the industrial, commercial and residential sectors.

Figure 1. GHG Emission Trends by Sector (1990–2021)

Total greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, excluding those associated with forestry, land use and land-use change (LULUCF), increased 81.91% between 1990 and 2021, from 100,875.87 kt CO₂eq to 183,498.92 kt CO₂eq.

Net emissions, which incorporate the LULUCF balance, recorded growth of 25.80%, from 222,664.78 kt CO₂eq to 280,101.96 kt CO₂eq over the same period.

The following figure shows the evolution of the country's per capita GHG emissions during the period 1990–2021.

Figure 2. Per Capita GHG Emissions (1990–2021)

The average total per capita emissions in Colombia during the 1990–2021 period were 6.0 t CO₂eq, while in 2021 they stood at 5.9 t CO₂eq. In terms of net emissions, the average is 5.7 t CO₂eq, with a value of 5.5 t CO₂eq for 2021. Since 1990, a decreasing trend in per capita emissions is observed, with a cumulative reduction of 18.13%.

The causes of this behavior are detailed in 'Annex 1: Document of the National Inventory of Atmospheric Emissions and Removals of Colombia. Greenhouse Gases (1990–2021). Criteria Pollutants and Black Carbon (2010–2021). First Biennial Transparency Report (BTR 1), submitted to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change.' In sectoral terms, LULUCF accounted for 34.49% in 2021, followed by energy (32.71%) and agriculture (20.69%) (see Figure 3).

In LULUCF, the main sources are associated with deforestation and degradation of natural forest. In energy, emissions from road transport and domestic aviation, electricity and heat generation, solid fuel production and oil refining predominate. In agriculture, livestock management and agricultural soil management stand out.

Although the industrial processes and product use (IPPU) and waste sectors represent a smaller proportion (12.11% in 2021), their contributions are relevant.

Figure 3. Share by IPCC Module in Net 2021 Emissions

2.4 Risks and Vulnerabilities

The National Climate Change Communications presented by Colombia to the UNFCCC have demonstrated that climate transformations have the potential to significantly alter ecosystems and modify established relationships between society and nature. These changes have direct effects on socioeconomic systems, as evidenced by multiple authors (Pérez et al., 1998; Pabón, 2003; Costa, 2007; Bedoya et al., 2010; Pabón, 2012; Ochoa and Poveda, 2008; among others).

The Third National Climate Change Communication (2017) conducted a vulnerability and risk analysis for all Colombian territory, using 113 indicators organized around the components of threat, sensitivity and adaptive capacity. These indicators were distributed across six territorial dimensions: food security, water resources, biodiversity and ecosystem services, health, human habitat and infrastructure.

While the construction of an aggregate vulnerability index was useful for obtaining a comparative metric between territories, this methodology presented two main limitations for national adaptation planning. Initially, the consolidation of results into a single composite index obscured the visibility of individual results by dimension; followed by the lack of ownership by sectors, as they did not see themselves clearly and separately reflected in the index with their specific competencies and responsibilities.

The threat component of the TCNCC showed the potential effects of climate change based on temperature and precipitation projections for the national reference scenario, with behavior close to RCP 6.0 for the period 2011–2040. From this information, the levels of climate change risk were calculated for the entire country, results that constitute a key input for national and sectoral planning on adaptation.

Based on those experiences, and to respond to new information needs, Colombia presented to the Green Climate Fund a capacity-building proposal that led to the design of the Integrated Information System on Vulnerability, Risk and Climate Change Adaptation (SIIVRA).4.

This system, led by IDEAM in coordination with the National Planning Department (DNP), the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development and the National Unit for Disaster Risk Management (UNGRD), seeks to consolidate and harmonize information on vulnerability and risk, and to facilitate the evaluation of adaptation in the country. SIIVRA structures its analysis around eight territorial dimensions (2025) that reflect the main vulnerability and resilience factors of the country. These dimensions encompass social, economic and environmental aspects.5

  • Climate change risk module, to identify threats and vulnerabilities.

Figure 4. Adaptation Monitoring Levels – SIIVRA Conceptual Framework

Source: Adaptation of the SIIVRA Conceptual Framework, 2021

In October 2024, Colombia presented the new climate change scenarios SSP — Shared Socioeconomic Pathways, within the framework of the Fourth National Communication of Colombia (IDEAM, 2024). These scenarios are fundamental not only for academic research and institutional climate change management, but also as a tool to strengthen social and political understanding of the risks that territories face. They are also an input to the threat component of SIIVRA.

The analysis is based on the reference period 1981–2010 and projects possible changes into the future. For the period 2021–2040, moderate temperature changes are anticipated; however, toward the end of the century (2081–2100) scenario differences intensify, showing what implications global socioeconomic trajectories may have on our country's climate (BTR1, 2024).

BTR1 (2024) presented the first advances on vulnerability and climate change risk for three dimensions: Biodiversity and Ecosystem Services, Human Health and Water Resources, which are already reported in SIIVRA. Progressively, by 2026 the analysis of all eight dimensions will be completed — including Disaster Risk and Cultural Heritage — as well as a specific study for coastal and island areas, within the framework of the Fourth National Communication of Colombia.

Adaptation Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E)

Regarding the Monitoring and Evaluation module, progress has been made in calculating the deltas — change or difference between one cut-off year and another — in sensitivity, adaptive capacity and vulnerability. Cut-offs will be made every 4 years, taking 2015 as the baseline and continuing with 2019, 2023, and so forth. Preliminary results are currently available in the dimensions of Human Health, Water Resources and Food Sovereignty and Agricultural Production for the period 2015–2019.

  • Human Health: 82% of municipalities reduced their vulnerability, while the remaining 18% recorded increases, especially in Vichada, Tolima, Guainía, Vaupés, Risaralda and Nariño. These increases are related to higher rates of multidimensional poverty and unmet basic needs, as well as the incidence of diseases such as dengue and Leishmaniasis.
  • Water Resources: 63% of municipalities reduced their vulnerability, while 35% increased it. In the latter, the main factors were deforestation, high susceptibility to supply shortages during dry seasons and low remanence index values (proportion of conserved natural cover).
  • Food Sovereignty and Agricultural Production: 70% of municipalities showed reduction in vulnerability, while 29.4% recorded increases, largely associated with soil salinization, restrictions on water access for agricultural production and high levels of multidimensional poverty activities. The most affected departments are La Guajira, Vichada, Huila, Tolima and Vaupés.

SIIVRA makes available control panels that allow visualization of how different indicators contribute to the degree of vulnerability of each municipality, linking social, economic and environmental dimensions. In this way, it constitutes an input to guide NDC targets and strengthen decision-making from an environmental and territorial justice perspective.6

National Monitoring Mechanism for Indigenous Peoples

The Indigenous Community Environmental Monitoring Route (MACI), agreed between the National Government and the MPC, is a key tool in the face of the climate crisis that articulates Indigenous Knowledge Systems with technical methods to generate situated and continuous information across three monitoring axes: i) forests, deforestation and ecosystems, ii) climate change (droughts, floods and bioindicators) and iii) water resources and water clarity. Through the characterization of existing initiatives, this route consolidates and systematizes experiences of indigenous environmental monitoring that contribute to understanding climate change from the territories.

2.5 State of Climate Change Adaptation

Colombia has been consolidating an institutional and policy architecture for adaptation that integrates planning, information, monitoring and feedback. The National Climate Change System (SISCLIMA) articulates public and private entities at all territorial levels through the Intersectoral Climate Change Commission (CICC) and the Regional Climate Change Nodes (NRCC). This framework seeks to align the NDC with the National Climate Change Policy (PNCC, 2017), with planning instruments such as the PIGCCT and PIGCCS, as well as with the SDGs and the Paris Agreement.

The First Biennial Transparency Report (BTR1, 2024) reaffirms the urgency of advancing toward resilient development. Climate projections warn of precipitation reductions of up to 44% in the Caribbean — with critical impacts in La Guajira —, increases of between 8–45% in the Amazon, and a sustained increase in mean and maximum temperatures throughout the territory. These transformations are already manifesting in concrete risks: according to SIIVRA, 464 municipalities identified as having the greatest climate vulnerability overlap with areas of greater food insecurity, a phenomenon that is exacerbated by the country's unequal development.

Despite the advances, BTR1 (2024) identifies four central obstacles that hinder the country's adaptive capacity:

  • Insufficient and dispersed information, which limits data traceability and quality.
  • Capacity and financing gaps in the territories, which hinder bringing policies into practice.
  • Weaknesses in monitoring, evaluation and learning, which restrict effective feedback.
  • Failures in means of implementation, in particular the lack of predictable financing and weak inter-institutional coordination.

These barriers, described in greater detail in BTR1 (2024), show that adaptation is not only a technical matter, but also a political one: it is about how resources are distributed, who participates in decision-making and how the rights of the most vulnerable communities are guaranteed.

In this regard, it is essential to recognize the contributions of indigenous peoples in the fight against the climate crisis, who through the own governance of Indigenous Territories and Territorialities exercise control of deforestation and management of forests and water sources, as recognized in CONPES document 4021 of 2020. Their way of conceiving the territory as collective property makes it possible to guarantee physical and cultural survival.

The balance presented in BTR1, together with the advances of SISCLIMA and the PNCC (2017), made it possible to extract key guidelines that today inform NDC 3.0: the organization of adaptation targets under the eight dimensions of SIIVRA, territorial and sectoral prioritization with differential approaches, the need for stable means of implementation and clear governance arrangements, and the consolidation of SIIVRA as the axis of adaptation monitoring and evaluation. These elements form the basis for the commitments and measures of NDC 3.0.

In addition to SIIVRA as the axis of adaptation monitoring and evaluation, indigenous peoples have implemented community monitoring mechanisms on climate change based on indigenous peoples' autonomy and Knowledge Systems, which are in the process of strengthening and unification based on the measures defined in this NDC.

2.6 Loss and Damage

Colombia faces increasingly severe and systematic impacts from the climate crisis, both from extreme weather events and from slow-onset processes. In recent years, there has been an increase in the frequency and intensity of landslides, floods and droughts, as well as the emergence of gradual phenomena including sea level rise, variability in precipitation patterns and a sustained increase in temperature.

These dynamics generate significant economic and social losses, affecting critical infrastructure, food security, water availability and the state of strategic ecosystems. Likewise, they impose growing pressure on public health systems due to the increase in climate-related diseases.

In addition to these situations, indigenous peoples have experienced cultural impacts derived from climate change, such as the modification of ecological-cultural calendars that transform their cultural and spiritual practices for environmental management, which in turn makes mitigation and adaptation actions more difficult from indigenous knowledge systems. The ecological or ancestral calendars of indigenous peoples are millennia-old and demonstrate the close relationship with the natural world and its cycles.

This situation highlights the urgency of strengthening climate risk management as a central component of fulfilling the Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) and the guidelines defined in the Long-Term Climate Strategy (E2050). Both instruments establish the need to integrate adaptation, vulnerability reduction and the protection of human and natural systems, ensuring that the country advances toward resilient and low-carbon development.

According to E2050 (2021), in an RCP 4.5 scenario, the Expected Annual Loss (EAL) amounts to 4.03 trillion pesos, a figure equivalent to 21.46% of the budget allocated in 2021 to sectors such as housing, agriculture, environment and transport. Added to this, the contingent liability from disaster risk represents 4.27% of projected GDP for 2025. These economic costs are accompanied by non-economic losses: ecosystem degradation, biodiversity reduction and displacement of human communities.

In this latter respect, human mobility for climate-related reasons has become a growing phenomenon in the country. In 2023 alone, 351,000 new internal displacements due to disasters were recorded (mainly storms, floods, droughts and forest fires). Forced internal displacement generates profound uprooting and affects multiple fundamental rights, such as a dignified life, freedom of movement, housing and the minimum vital standard, constituting loss and damage of social and cultural nature.

Facing this situation, the implementation of a solid package of adaptation measures against floods, hurricanes and droughts could reduce the EAL by 50%, with an investment close to 7.8 trillion pesos annually. An investment of this scale would not only reduce future impacts, but would also strengthen the country's resilience and its capacity to respond to climate emergencies.

Projections confirm a sustained increase in mean, maximum and minimum temperatures throughout the territory, which would intensify toward the final decades of the 21st century. Between 2061–2080 and 2081–2100, increases of at least 2°C compared to the historical reference period (1981–2010) are expected, with possibilities of exceeding 3°C toward the end of the century. Regarding precipitation, increases of between 20% and 50% are anticipated in the Andean region during the second half of the century, while significant reductions are projected in the Caribbean.

This situation confirms that losses and damages are not a future risk, but a present reality that limits sustainable development, increases social and economic costs, and deepens social and territorial inequalities, especially for indigenous peoples; Black, Afro-Colombian, Raizal and Palenquero communities; peasantry and local communities. For this reason, Colombia demonstrates a strong commitment and ambition to address loss and damage from climate change, recognizing the urgent need for adequate means of implementation.

Finally, Colombia recognizes the need to include this dimension in its NDC 3.0, elevating the issue to the international agenda and underscoring the urgency of having support mechanisms that allow its impacts to be managed in a comprehensive manner.

3. National Climate Priorities

The National Development Plan 2022–2026, Colombia: A Global Power of Life, establishes as its central purpose the protection of life through a new social contract that transforms relationships with nature and lays the foundations for a sustainable productive transition. Within this framework, climate action becomes a structural axis: the fight against deforestation, the conservation of strategic ecosystems such as the Amazon, the Pacific and the Sierra Nevada, and the sustainable management of biodiversity are placed at the center of a project of transformation of the country.

In fulfillment of the Paris Agreement and in response to the 2023 Global Stocktake, NDC 3.0 presents a set of priorities that guide the country's climate action in mitigation, adaptation and loss and damage management. These priorities reflect the need to advance in a coherent and ambitious manner in greenhouse gas emission reductions, the strengthening of climate resilience and the attention to adverse climate change impacts, in accordance with national circumstances and cross-cutting approaches.

The priorities defined in NDC 3.0 are the result of a multi-level and multi-actor effort, built jointly among national government sectors, territorial entities, the private sector, communities and civil society. This collaborative character recognizes that climate action is not the responsibility of a single actor, but a common project that articulates diverse knowledge, capacities and visions. Within this framework, NDC 3.0 incorporates for the first time the priorities of indigenous peoples, giving expression to territorial and differentiated climate action.

Climate Ambition Signals of NDC 3.0

NDC 3.0 expresses a signal of greater climate ambition from the national government, consistent with the vision of life protection and productive transformation of the country, oriented toward gradually overcoming dependence on carbon-intensive activities, in particular those associated with the use and exploitation of fossil fuels. This ambition is reflected, among other things, in the strengthening of ecological restoration targets, in the consolidation of a cross-cutting decarbonization approach in sectors such as transport, industry and waste, and in the promotion of a just energy transition.

Within this framework, NDC 3.0 reinforces the need to advance toward more decisive actions in strategic sectors, reaffirming the country's commitment to a determined, coherent and verifiable climate action.

The participatory process developed for the NDC 3.0 update is documented in Annex 1 – Participatory pathway in the NDC 3.0 update, which brings together the methodologies employed, the dialogue spaces, the actors involved and the main contributions that underpinned its construction.

National climate priorities express four key elements:

  • Comprehensive vision. Each sector and actor recognizes that climate action requires addressing simultaneously — but in a differentiated manner — mitigation and adaptation, so that emission reductions contribute to the strengthening of resilience, security and sustainable development in the territories.
  • Quantifiable ambition. Concrete and measurable targets are incorporated — such as hectares under sustainable models, expanded coverage of wastewater treatment, expansion of electric mobility or reduction of deforestation — which reflect the ambition, seriousness and traceability of the commitments assumed by the country.
  • Productive and social transformation. Beyond emission reductions, climate priorities aim to transform productive systems, urban dynamics and territorial development models, advancing toward a just transition that promotes competitiveness, innovation, social well-being and the protection of ecosystems.
  • An initial prioritization framework and progressive expansion. The integration of strategic sectors, indigenous peoples, territories and the private sector constitutes a diversified base upon which Colombia will continue to broaden and deepen its climate action, ensuring that NDC 3.0 becomes increasingly comprehensive, just and inclusive.

INDIGENOUS PEOPLES' PRIORITIES

Colombia's NDC 3.0 marks a milestone by incorporating for the first time a national target and a set of 12 measures agreed upon with indigenous peoples, through the Permanent Consultation Table (MPC), the highest political dialogue body between Indigenous Government and the national government. This inclusion recognizes that contributions from Indigenous Territories and Territorialities, anchored in their own Knowledge Systems, territorial governance and the sacred relationship with nature, constitute an essential contribution to fulfilling global objectives in the face of the climate crisis.

The priorities presented here constitute a strategic vision that guides indigenous climate action within the framework of NDC 3.0. This vision starts from the recognition of indigenous peoples as fundamental actors in the protection of biocultural diversity, territorial governance and climate resilience, contributing practices and knowledge that strengthen mitigation and adaptation. It likewise values the diversity of their territories and ways of life, and underscores the importance of respecting their autonomy and self-determination in all climate decisions.

Salvaguarda

Nothing contained in this NDC 3.0 may be interpreted or applied in a manner that limits, undermines or suppresses the rights that indigenous peoples currently hold or may exercise and acquire in the future, including the right to autonomy, self-determination, self-government, territorial rights, their own planning mechanisms and the full exercise of environmental authority, as well as the administration, management and execution of resources.

Indigenous Peoples' Target

Colombia will report in its official reports the specific contribution that indigenous territories make — grounded in their knowledge systems — to the fulfillment of the country's mitigation and adaptation targets, priorities and accounting, through the implementation of their Life Plans or equivalents by indigenous governance structures, ratifying the importance of indigenous peoples and their territories as climate action.

Axis 1. Intercultural Climate Governance and Institutionality

This axis strengthens the institutional conditions necessary to ensure coordination, articulation and recognition between the National Government and Indigenous Governments in climate change management, as well as the reporting of indigenous territories' contributions in mitigation and adaptation. Its scope encompasses the adaptation of SISCLIMA and its institutional architecture, as well as the development — in a consensual manner — of a protocol for the National Climate Change Information System to incorporate indigenous peoples' own knowledge and monitoring systems.

Axis 2. Indigenous Territories and Territorialities

This axis recognizes Indigenous Territories and Territorialities as essential spaces for climate management and socioecological sustainability. It is grounded in the strengthening of own territorial governance, in coherence with indigenous planning and land-use instruments, and with the full safeguarding of the territorial rights of indigenous peoples.

Climate action in these territories is grounded in Indigenous Knowledge Systems, which guide the care, protection and healing of the territory; strengthen food sovereignty and autonomy; and contribute integral visions for climate change mitigation and adaptation. Their articulation with intercultural actions and with national processes of climate education, training and awareness-raising contributes to expanding Colombia's capacities to face climate challenges.

Axis 3. Energy Transition with a Rights-Based Approach

This axis establishes that the energy transition in Indigenous Territories and Territorialities must be developed under a rights-based approach and through processes of joint regulation between the National Government and Indigenous Governments. It seeks to ensure that policies and projects linked to the energy transition recognize indigenous territorial autonomy, respect their own systems of authority and decision-making, and contribute to the comprehensive protection of their territories.

Additionally, the environmental, social and cultural impacts of the exploitation of strategic minerals, as well as of alternative energy projects for the energy transition in indigenous territories and territorialities, will be assessed, and institutional adaptations will be undertaken to protect the integrity of the territories and guarantee the right to Free, Prior and Informed Consent and other rights of indigenous peoples.

Axis 4. Just and Direct Financing for Indigenous Climate Action

This axis is oriented toward promoting conditions that allow progress toward mechanisms of direct and just financing for indigenous climate action. Its approach seeks to ensure that such mechanisms are designed and implemented from, with and for indigenous peoples, in accordance with their own governance systems and territorial priorities.

The strengthening of strategies, instruments and capacities of Indigenous Governments is fundamental to favor the mobilization, allocation and execution of resources destined for climate action in indigenous territories, in a manner consistent with their forms of organization and own planning.

Axis 5. Indigenous Women: Participation and Care

This axis recognizes the fundamental role of indigenous women in the survival of Knowledge Systems, in the protection of the territory and in the construction of climate solutions. Their leadership, knowledge and practices are essential for strengthening climate change mitigation and adaptation from a biocultural and community perspective.

For indigenous climate action, it is a priority to promote conditions that facilitate the participation of indigenous women in spaces for decision-making and knowledge exchange at the global, regional, national and local levels, in harmony with the commitments established in international treaties and agreements on the rights of women and indigenous peoples.

Likewise, this axis promotes the design and implementation of community strategies for the care and protection of women, communities and indigenous territories in the face of impacts associated with climate change and climate variability, within the framework of a just transition that respects collective rights and strengthens territorial resilience.

Together, these priorities express an integral vision of indigenous peoples for climate action, based on their Knowledge Systems, their own forms of governance and the protection of their Territories and Territorialities. This vision reaffirms their leading role in building climate solutions, in conserving biological and cultural diversity, in guiding an energy transition respectful of their collective rights, in the strengthening of their autonomy and decision-making capacity, and in advancing toward a just climate finance that reaches their territories in a direct and adequate manner.

AGRICULTURAL SECTOR

Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development

The Colombian agricultural sector has consolidated a strategic vision for addressing climate change, articulated in five sectoral axes that guide its commitments within the framework of NDC 3.0.

This vision recognizes peasant, ethnic, family and community agriculture as a pillar of climate action and food sovereignty. It is grounded in the democratization of knowledge and technology, promoting rural communities as protagonists of innovation, productive conservation and the transformation of agri-food systems, inspiring them to see the countryside as a space of the future. This transition drives sustainable production with a territorial approach, which conserves biodiversity, reduces emissions and strengthens resilience.

Axis 1. Climate Change Information and Risk Management for Decision-Making

Agroclimatic technical roundtables, risk and vulnerability analysis, and the generation of climate information to guide productive and territorial adaptation decisions have been strengthened. These tools are progressively extended to rural territories, guaranteeing equitable access to information, public governance of agricultural data and climate technology as a public good, indispensable for local decision-making.

Axis 2. Sustainable Agricultural Practices

The sector promotes sustainable water management, soil management and conservation, sustainable use of ecosystems and diversification of productive systems. An example is the Sustainable Bovine Livestock NAMA, which drives silvopastoral systems, ecosystem restoration and traceability, with a target of 3.6 million hectares by 2030. Commercial forest plantations on degraded soils (1 million hectares by 2035), cacao under agroforestry systems and peasant, family, ethnic and community production models also stand out.

Likewise, agroecological practices and production models that integrate conservation and productivity are promoted, enabling conserving while producing and strengthening local value chains that sustain the rural bioeconomy.

Axis 3. Resilience as a Strategy Against Extreme Climate Events

Measures have been developed to face emergency situations and risk conditions, including the design of NAMAs for coffee and panela, and the use of biomass through biofuels and biogas, reducing emissions in agro-industry.

These actions are complemented by the strengthening of community capacities and the rescue of traditional adaptive practices, promoting climate adaptation based on local knowledge and the participation of youth as agents of change.

Axis 4. Investment and Policy for Resilient and Low-Carbon Rural Development

The sector works on formulating policies with a climate and differential approach, and on access to climate finance and inclusive markets. Progress is being made in implementing a national monitoring and verification system for the fulfillment of zero deforestation in the agri-food sector.

Complementarily, public and private investment in rural innovation, agricultural digitalization and technological inclusion is promoted, ensuring that the climate transition also reaches peasant and ethnic territories as part of a process of democratizing development.

Axis 5. Institutional Coordination, Research and Capacity Building

Inter-institutional cooperation, knowledge management and the strengthening of technical and community capacities for the effective implementation of measures are promoted. Within this framework, agricultural research and extension are understood as tools for territorial equity, aimed at bringing science, innovation and technology to those who produce food and steward ecosystems.

Complementarily, the sector recognizes that the transition toward a rural bioeconomy and greater agri-food circularity constitutes a strategic framework for strengthening climate adaptation. This approach guides the sustainable use of residual biomass, productive diversification and the development of bioinputs and bioproducts. In an articulated manner, the consolidation of sustainable, low-emission agri-food models more resilient to climate change is projected, consistent with the principles of the just transition and territorial equity.

With these commitments, the agricultural sector is consolidated as one of the main actors in climate management, promoting a just, inclusive and territorially diverse transition that guarantees food sovereignty, biodiversity conservation and well-being for present and future generations.

ENVIRONMENT SECTOR

Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development

The environment sector strengthens its climate contribution to NDC 3.0 around six strategic axes that condense its main commitments in terms of greenhouse gas emission reductions, forest conservation, promotion of the circular economy, improvement of air quality and climate resilience.
