These axes integrate sectoral commitments with national mitigation targets and are articulated with instruments such as the Comprehensive Sectoral Climate Change Management Plan (PIGCC), the REDD+ Strategy, CONPES 4021 — National Policy for Deforestation Control and Sustainable Forest Management in Colombia, the Biodiversity Action Plan, the Green Growth Policy and the National Circular Economy Strategy.

Axis 1: Comprehensive Protection and Restoration of the Colombian Amazon Biome for Climate, Hydrological and Biodiversity Stability

The environment sector will guide the protection, restoration and comprehensive management of the Colombian Amazon Biome as a strategic climate barrier for the country, given its essential function in continental water regulation, regional climate stabilization, the capture and storage of large volumes of carbon, and the conservation of ecosystem connectivity that links the Andes–Amazon–Orinoquia systems. Its river network — the most extensive on the planet —, its areas of high endemism, its role as a carbon reservoir and its contribution to the water cycle make it an irreplaceable asset for climate stability at the national and global level.

This axis is driven by the delimitation and declaration of the Renewable Natural Resources Reserve in the Colombian Amazon biome with definitive status. From this, the conservation of its ecological integrity, Andes–Amazon connectivity and the reduction of the risk of reaching critical thresholds of ecosystem degradation are prioritized, while promoting the transition toward forest, biodiversity and community-based economies, with the participation of indigenous peoples and local communities.

In coherence with the technical and scientific evidence on the biome's function in climate regulation, water stability and carbon capture, the country will guide the prevention of the opening of new mineral and hydrocarbon exploration and exploitation activities in this strategic region, as well as the restriction of the expansion of existing operations that could increase impacts on the biome's integrity, together with the containment of other factors that drive forest loss.

This axis is articulated with the deforestation reduction, ecological restoration, community forest management and sustainable forest management measures included in this NDC — among them the transition of Active Deforestation Nuclei (NAD) toward Forest and Biodiversity Development Nuclei (NDFyB), the National Restoration Strategy, and the Comprehensive Deforestation Containment Plan (PICD) —, recognizing that such actions contribute to securing the ecological functionality of the biome and its role in the global carbon cycle.

Axis 2: Deforestation Reduction and Forest Restoration

Deforestation reduction involves several sectors and is developed under the leadership and coordination of the environment sector. The established target is to reduce the deforestation rate to 50,000 ha/year in 2030 (change in natural forest area due to deforestation). To this end, policy instruments have been structured to halt deforestation and manage forests sustainably, addressing agents and drivers through the strengthening of forest governance, conservation incentives and community empowerment.

In addition to deforestation containment, the National Restoration Strategy sets a target of reaching one million hectares restored by 2030 (accumulated since 2015), with a proportion of 80% passive restoration and 20% active restoration. For the period 2031 to 2035, a minimum annual rate of 68,684 hectares restored will be maintained, guaranteeing continuity in processes, strengthening territorial resilience and contributing to carbon capture.

Axis 3: Circular Economy

This axis seeks to increase efficiency in the production and consumption of goods and services, promoting the closure of material cycles, technological innovation and new business models that reduce emissions. Among the most relevant measures are:

  • the implementation of urban thermal districts, with targets of at least 8 by 2030, 12 by 2035 and 15 by 2040, to advance toward centralized and more efficient systems.
  • the reduction in the use of ozone-depleting substances with high global warming potential, accompanied by a technological upgrade in domestic refrigeration and air conditioning.
  • the decrease in food losses and waste, strengthening recovery and redistribution within the framework of the national policy on the subject.

Axis 4: Bioeconomy and Productive Transformation

This axis drives the transition toward an economy sustained by biodiversity and the sustainable use of ecosystems, promoting new forms of production and consumption that reduce greenhouse gas emissions and strengthen climate resilience.

The bioeconomy is conceived as an engine for knowledge management, productive diversification and nature-based innovation. It articulates local and ancestral knowledge with the conservation of ecosystem services and ecological restoration, driving value chains based on products and services derived from biodiversity and strengthening local and community economies. It also promotes basic and applied research for the development of bioproducts that substitute fossil-derived or synthetic inputs.

Within this framework, communities play an essential role as custodians of knowledge and practices that make the transition possible, connecting biodiversity with productive opportunities that reinforce the identity and resilience of territories.

Sustainable productive transformation, a pillar of the bioeconomy, is driven from the territories through community models that integrate production and care of socioecological systems. These models redefine the relationship with nature, reduce dependence on extractive activities and strengthen a productive culture capable of adapting to the challenges of the climate crisis, contributing to a just transition that sustains a diverse and dynamic economy.

Axis 5: Air Quality

In order to integrate climate change management and air quality, and with the intention of advancing toward the reduction of black carbon emissions, the environment sector, together with the support of other sectors, has promoted actions aimed at the progressive adoption of stricter emission standards for diesel-cycle and off-road land mobile sources operating within the national territory, among others.

Axis 6: Biodiversity as the Basis of Climate Resilience

The Environment sector will guide its adaptation contribution through a strategic axis centered on the protection and restoration of biodiversity as the basis of climate resilience, articulating comprehensive water management and climate risk management in terrestrial, marine, coastal and island ecosystems. This axis prioritizes the reduction of socioecological vulnerabilities, the conservation of critical ecosystem services, including those associated with water resources, the prevention of loss and damage, and the improvement of adaptive capacity in territories at greatest risk.

Likewise, this axis is guided by the identification of risk and vulnerability levels established in the Fourth National Climate Change Communication, ensuring that adaptation measures strengthen territorial adaptive capacity and contribute to the reduction of climate risks, with sustainable environmental and social benefits.

COMMERCE, INDUSTRY AND TOURISM SECTOR

Ministry of Commerce, Industry and Tourism

The contribution of the Commerce, Industry and Tourism sector to the NDC 3.0 agenda is oriented toward consolidating a productive, innovative and resilient future.

Axis 1: Business Competitiveness for Decarbonization.

The central commitment is to transform the productive apparatus toward a low-carbon economy. To this end, energy management and energy efficiency, the transition to cleaner fuels and non-conventional renewable energy sources (FNCER) will continue to be strengthened. These actions are complemented by a focus on sustainable processes, good operational practices and the use of advanced technologies, promoting greenhouse gas reductions in key subsectors such as cement and fertilizer production, and sustainable tourism.

Axis 2: Sustainable Logistics and Trade.

It seeks to optimize value chains and goods mobility through innovation, advanced digitalization and low- and zero-emission technologies to reduce the sector's carbon footprint. It includes the development of a study for the design and validation of a zero-emission logistics corridor or hub, leveraged by anchor companies and strategic operators, that drives low-carbon freight transport and value networks in the industry.

This vision aligns with the National Development Plan, which promotes sustainable productive linkages, logistics efficiency and technological adoption, and with the 2050 Climate Strategy.

Axis 3: Adaptation for Competitiveness and Resilience.

The commitment is to strengthen the capacity of companies and tourist destinations to assess and manage their risks from extreme climate events. This will materialize through the design and implementation of strengthening and technical assistance programs, the development of tools for water risk management and others, and the promotion of nature-based solutions (NbS) and the bioeconomy. This axis links with the Sustainability Pact of the National Development Plan, the Circular Economy Policy and the national bioeconomy agenda.

In a cross-cutting manner, these commitments are integrated with key enabling elements, such as a science-based business competitiveness roadmap for adaptation and decarbonization, the creation of business plans integrating biodiversity and gender, the strengthening of public-private coordination, financial mechanisms and monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV) systems. These elements seek to drive actions and projects toward sustainability and sectoral progress.

MINING AND ENERGY SECTOR

Ministry of Mines and Energy

The mining-energy sector (MES) is strategic for Colombia's just transition, given its relevance in the country's greenhouse gas emissions dynamics, its role as an engine of competitiveness and energy security. Its contribution to NDC 3.0 is articulated around three mitigation axes and a comprehensive adaptation agenda, in coherence with the PIGCCme 2050, the just energy transition roadmap and international commitments on decarbonization.

Axis 1: Just Energy Transition

The priority is to accelerate the decarbonization of the electricity system through a progressive reduction in fossil fuel use in generation, both in the National Interconnected System (SIN) and in Non-Interconnected Zones (ZNI). To this end, the incorporation of renewable energy, large-scale storage solutions, network digitalization and regulatory modernization will be promoted.

At the same time, the projected growth in electricity demand must be managed in a clean, safe and affordable manner. To achieve this, measures such as time-of-use tariffs, demand response programs, distributed storage, advanced metering systems and source diversification will be promoted.

Likewise, enabling technologies such as green hydrogen, sustainable biomass and advanced digital systems will be incorporated, aimed at substituting fossil fuels, improving operational efficiency and capturing residual emissions. Their deployment will require closing regulatory gaps, attracting investment, strengthening national capacities and ensuring coherence with PIGCCme and NDC targets.

Axis 2: Emission Reductions in Oil and Gas

The target is to consolidate a more efficient and competitive hydrocarbon sector through the systematic implementation of leak detection and repair (LDAR) technologies, equipment modernization, operations electrification and the progressive elimination of routine venting and flaring. These advances require regulatory frameworks that strengthen emissions traceability, ensure access to international markets and increase the sector's resilience in the face of the structural reduction of fossil fuel demand.

Axis 3: Transition in Mining

It seeks to reduce the carbon footprint of the mining sector through energy efficiency measures, electrification, use of renewable energy and management of fugitive emissions from coal mines. The just transition of coal extraction in mining will be gradual and differentiated, accompanied by social and territorial dialogue to ensure a just, inclusive transition that generates new opportunities.

Axis 4. Adaptation to Climate Change

Design and implement adaptive pathways by subsector (energy, hydrocarbons and mining), based on the level of climate change risk, aimed at reducing the vulnerability of the value chain and the territories where the sector operates. These pathways are oriented toward establishing technical, regulatory and economic capacities through strategies to increase adaptive capacity.

The Ministry of Mines and Energy, as part of its commitment to decarbonizing the national energy matrix, will promote the construction of a Roadmap for Fossil Fuel Phase-Out and the Dismantling of Carbon-Intensive Subsidies, addressing legal, financial and technological challenges to reduce the participation of fossil sources and accelerate the penetration of FNCER in the country's energy matrix. Key milestones include: the non-issuance of new titles for coal exploitation; the non-issuance of new hydrocarbon exploitation contracts by 2035; as well as the dismantling of fossil fuel subsidies and the promotion of measures that increase revenue to mobilize financial resources for climate action.

In this way, the mining-energy sector reaffirms its commitment to advancing in a just, orderly and technically sound transition, which allows emissions to be progressively reduced, the energy matrix diversified and Colombia consolidated as a regional reference in the construction of a competitive, resilient and low-carbon sector.

TRANSPORT SECTOR

Ministry of Transport

The transport sector's contribution to NDC 3.0 is organized in three strategic axes: sustainable mobility, logistics and adaptation. Each one gathers recent advances and is articulated with official policies and instruments such as Law 1964 of 2019 for the promotion of electric vehicles, Law 2294 of 2022 which adopts the National Development Plan and includes targets for the transport sector on vehicle renewal and sustainable mobility, Decree 0402 of 2025 on the promotion program for zero-emission vehicles, the National Electric Mobility Strategy (ENME) and the National Active Mobility Strategy (ENMA).

Axis 1. Sustainable Mobility

It seeks to accelerate the decarbonization of the vehicle fleet and promote modal shift toward non-motorized and electric modes, ensuring access and equity, with a gender approach.

By 2026, the modernization of urban and inter-municipal public transport fleets will be prioritized through renewal primarily by low-emission electric buses in priority territories. Additionally, the renewal of combustion taxi fleets to electric vehicles in prioritized cities is sought through the Fund for the Promotion of Technological Upgrade — FOPAT.

Likewise, the target of incorporating 600,000 registered electric vehicles by the year 2030 is sought to be consolidated, in line with the ENME and the strengthening of FOPAT. Additionally, for that same year an increase of 5.5% in the modal share of active transport relative to the national baseline is projected, through the implementation of the ENMA, the National Program for the Promotion of Bicycle Use (PFUB) and the participation of at least 17 cities in the country.

Axis 2. Logistics

It seeks to improve the energy efficiency of cargo transport and shift relevant cargo volumes from road to lower carbon-intensity modes.

For the year 2030, the annual mobilization of 8 million tonnes by river transport and 4.2 million tonnes by rail transport is proposed, reducing the share of road transport in long-distance routes, articulated with the National Logistics Policy — CONPES 3982.

This requires prioritization of investment in intermodal nodes, modernization of rail infrastructure and waterways, which are coordinated with the renewal of road cargo vehicle fleets through FOPAT.

Axis 3. Adaptation and Resilience

The aim is to incorporate climate and risk criteria in the different phases of infrastructure, the design, operation and maintenance of transport infrastructure to ensure service continuity and life protection, while not neglecting the protection and management of biodiversity impact.

By 2030, the incorporation of climate resilience criteria in infrastructure will be promoted, improvement in information systems and the implementation of the sector's Risk and Climate Change Management Policy, reducing vulnerability to extreme weather events and ensuring service continuity. These axes are accompanied by monitoring mechanisms, financing schemes and measurement systems that allow commitments to be grounded in concrete projects. In this way, the transport sector becomes a driver of the just and low-carbon transition in Colombia.

HOUSING, WATER AND BASIC SANITATION SECTORS

Ministry of Housing, City and Territory

The Ministry of Housing, City and Territory assumes a strategic role in fulfilling NDC 3.0, consolidating advances and projecting commitments from the housing, water and basic sanitation sectors. With a territorial, inclusive and differential approach, the sector's actions contribute both to the mitigation of greenhouse gas emissions and to climate change adaptation, in coherence with the recommendations of the 2023 Global Stocktake.

The sectoral contribution is articulated in the following main strategic axes:

Axis 1. Comprehensive Solid Waste Management

Consolidation of circular economy measures through Mechanical Biological Treatment (MBT), recycling and biogas energy recovery, in coherence with the Zero Waste Program. The regulation of the biogas market will also be promoted to make sustainable projects viable and attract investment.

Axis 2. Domestic Wastewater Management (GARD)

Expansion of treatment coverage, and the use of biogas in Wastewater Treatment Plants (WWTP), articulating mitigation targets with improvements in sanitation, under a regulatory framework and technical guidelines that ensure urban and rural solutions in accordance with territorial planning.

Axis 3. Comprehensive Water Resource Management

Develop protection and management actions for priority water supply sources, as well as increase coverage and quality in the treatment of the most polluted watersheds and strengthen with climate change adaptation criteria. Similarly, the creation of enabling conditions that allow modification of the current regulations on the reuse of treated wastewater and generate incentives for service providers.

Axis 4. Risk Management for Climate Change Adaptation

Strengthen resilience and adaptive capacity to climate-related risks affecting the sector, through the implementation of structural and non-structural actions in municipalities prioritized by susceptibility to supply shortages during dry and rainy seasons.

Axis 5. Sustainable Construction

Mass implementation and strengthening of strategies that promote water and energy efficiency in new buildings in the country, in accordance with the provisions of Resolution 0194 of 2025, thereby achieving the development of projects aimed at improving the quality of life of their occupants and the exercise of actions with environmental and social responsibility.

Axis 6. Climate Change Adaptation for Territorial Development

Establishment of guidelines for the delivery, management and care of areas released due to non-mitigable risks, as well as for the adaptation of settlements located in areas of high mitigable risk in order to achieve intervention on the threat when possible, act on vulnerability for risk reduction and, in the long term, for climate change adaptation; consequently, with purposes coinciding with risk management. Hence the need for prioritization, innovation and learning about what works.

These axes are sustained by enabling instruments identified by sector actors, such as:

The waste MRV system under development, which will be coordinated at the sectoral level; the Implementation Plans (PdI) that ground responsibilities, schedules and financing sources; and financial viability mechanisms (incentives, financial closures and business schemes) that transform regulations into measurable, viable and sustainable projects.

In a cross-cutting manner, the sector will promote multi-level governance that integrates national and territorial perspectives of climate action. In this framework, it is worth highlighting that Colombia was among the first countries to endorse the declaration of the High Ambition Coalition for Multilevel Partnerships (CHAMP) for climate action, promoted by the COP28 presidency in Dubai and supported by more than 70 countries. This initiative seeks to strengthen cooperation between national and subnational governments to advance the implementation of NDCs and long-term climate strategies.

Together, these strategies consolidate the role of the Housing, Water and Basic Sanitation sectors as key allies of national and subnational climate action, contributing decisively to the fulfillment of NDC 3.0 and contributing to sustainable, just and resilient urban development for Colombia.

Collectively, the sectoral commitments consolidate the message that Colombia assumes a comprehensive, ambitious and transformative commitment in the face of the climate crisis, in coherence with its national planning instruments and with international transparency and climate action frameworks.

Based on these commitments, the chapter develops in a differentiated manner the national priorities in mitigation, adaptation and loss and damage, which constitute the three pillars of the NDC 3.0 and give shape to a coherent, inclusive country vision oriented toward the transformation of its development model.

3.1 Priorities from the Mitigation Component

Colombia reiterates its commitment to timely and decisive climate action, in coherence with the global mitigation target established in the Paris Agreement. The country maintains its 2030 target and, within the framework of this NDC 3.0, proposes a new 2035 target with a higher level of ambition, aligned with the trajectory toward carbon neutrality by 2050. The country acknowledges the existence of an implementation gap between what was established in the previous NDC and the results achieved; however, it reaffirms its political commitment to climate action and identifies the need for additional means of implementation, particularly climate finance and technology transfer, to advance toward the transformation required.

Indigenous Territories are indispensable for the fulfillment of mitigation priorities regarding Greenhouse Gases, Black Carbon and Deforestation, given that they conserve extensive forest areas, water sources and tissues, and biodiversity in balance. However, external threats are persistent and growing such as deforestation, land-use change, mining, hydrocarbon exploitation and agro-industry generate severe impacts on these territories.

Colombia also recognizes that overcoming institutional, technological, social and governance barriers requires international support, both in financing and technology transfer and capacity building. For this reason, it calls upon the international financial architecture to evolve to respond effectively to the needs of developing countries and enable progress toward diverse economies that protect ecosystems, reduce vulnerability, value biodiversity and ensure the rights of the most vulnerable peoples and communities.

In line with its commitments, Colombia is also advancing in the reduction of short-lived climate pollutants, particularly black carbon, recognizing that their mitigation contributes immediately both to public health and to the fulfillment of national and global climate targets. Likewise, the country strengthens its action against deforestation containment, understood not only as a pillar of mitigation, but also as a key strategy for biodiversity conservation and climate resilience.

Black carbon

Regarding the national black carbon (BC) reduction target, Colombia reaffirms its commitment to advancing in the mitigation of this pollutant, recognizing both its impact on climate change and on air quality and public health. The country established the objective of reducing black carbon emissions by 40% by the year 2030, taking 2014 levels as a reference. This is equivalent to a reduction of approximately 6,130 tonnes of BC, according to the baseline used for the formulation of the NDC 2.0.

According to BTR1 (2024), approximately 85% of national black carbon emissions originate from the energy sector, which evidences the magnitude of the challenge and the need to drive a set of diversified measures that allow comprehensive progress in its reduction.

Among the main actions undertaken, the following stand out:

  • The adoption of maximum permissible emission limits corresponding to the Euro IV (since 2015) and Euro VI (since 2023) standards for land mobile road sources equipped with compression ignition (diesel) engines, assembled, manufactured or imported to operate in the national territory.
  • The mandatory introduction of off-road land mobile sources (including machinery) that comply with maximum permissible emission limits corresponding to the Tier 4 Interim (Stage IIIB) standard, with national operating range.
  • The progressive reduction of agricultural burning in crops.

These measures contribute directly to the fulfillment of the black carbon emission reduction target and generate additional co-benefits, such as air quality improvement.

The detailed information to facilitate clarity, transparency and understanding (ICTU) of the mitigation target — including its type, parameters, methodological assumptions, coverage, accounting approach, coherence with the national inventory and monitoring method — is presented in Annex 2 of this NDC 3.0, in compliance with the provisions of Article 4 of the Paris Agreement and Decision 4/CMA.1.

3.1.2 Policies, Actions and Measures for Emission Reductions

Since 2020, Colombia has accompanied its Nationally Determined Contribution with a portfolio of mitigation measures that, from sectors and territories, contribute to greenhouse gas and short-lived climate pollutant emission reductions. These measures are the central instrument for fulfilling the national mitigation target by 2030 and projecting the path toward the new, more ambitious 2035 target.

In the process of updating and formulating NDC 3.0, the country has consolidated a portfolio that resumes and gives continuity to the measures presented in 2020, extends their implementation horizon and proposes new sectoral commitments in coherence with productive transformation, resilience and the just transition. In NDC 3.0, sectoral commitments are materialized in mitigation measures that guide Colombia's climate action and integrate the guiding principles and cross-cutting approaches of the NDC.

In general terms, sectors have proposed strategic transformations that will guide the fulfillment of the national target: productive and agricultural reconversion toward climate-smart and sustainable models; the consolidation of resilient cities and territories with more sustainable housing, sanitation and infrastructure; the progressive decarbonization of energy and transport; industrial transition toward efficiency and low-carbon competitiveness; and the strengthening of forests as a climate solution, integrating sustainable productive alternatives for the territories.

These transformations underpin the Mitigation Measures Portfolio presented in Annex 3 of NDC 3.0, which consolidates sectoral measures together with those proposed from territories and the private sector, all of them aimed at contributing, jointly, to the fulfillment of national mitigation targets. For each measure, the portfolio consolidates implementation responsibilities, support needs, implementation horizons and applicable cross-cutting approaches.

3.2 Priorities from the Adaptation Component

In compliance with Decision 9/CMA.1 and as part of the process of updating its Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC), Colombia presents in NDC 3.0 the information corresponding to its Second Adaptation Communication. In the National Circumstances chapter, the institutional arrangements and national legal frameworks that frame climate action are set out, as well as the main impacts, risks and vulnerabilities that the country faces.

This chapter brings together and organizes national adaptation priorities, and at the same time expresses the support needs the country requires to face the effects of the climate crisis.

3.2.1 National Adaptation Objective

The National Climate Change Adaptation Plan (PNACC, 2012) established as its long-term objective 'to reduce the socioeconomic risk and impacts associated with climate variability and climate change.' This purpose unfolds in at least three priority lines of action of the PNACC (DNP et al., 2016):

  • Manage knowledge about climate change and its potential impacts.
  • Incorporate climate change adaptation into environmental, territorial and sectoral planning.
  • Promote the transformation of development for climate change resilience.

This objective aligns with the Global Adaptation Goal (GGA) of the Paris Agreement, which seeks to increase resilience and reduce vulnerability within the framework of sustainable development. For Colombia, this alignment is not only technical, but also political: an opportunity to advance toward a development model that cares for life, respects territories and ensures more resilient, just and inclusive societies.

These objectives reflect not only the need to face the physical impacts of climate change, but also to transform the relationships between State, communities and territories, integrating local and ancestral knowledge, closing social gaps and recognizing the centrality of water, ecosystems and life in climate action.

3.2.2 National adaptation priorities

The conjunction of the SIIVRA dimensions (2025), the strategic axes of the PNACC (2012), the instrumental and strategic lines of the PNCC (2017), and the evidence synthesized in BTR1 (2024) and the TCNCC (2017), complemented by the Climate Change Scenarios (IDEAM, 2024)
