Complementarily, Colombia recognizes a series of strategic enablers that open opportunities to strengthen adaptation implementation and guide action in the medium and long term. These enablers are cross-cutting to the strategic objectives and seek to articulate scientific knowledge, political decisions, ancestral knowledge and territorial action:

Climate services and early warning systems. The role of IDEAM and regional networks will be strengthened to evolve from the provision of basic data toward comprehensive services that support decision-making, with clear and accessible protocols for both authorities and communities, within a framework of climate justice and equitable access to information.

Adaptive and resilient land-use planning. Climate foresight will be incorporated into Land Use Plans (POT),

  • the PIGCC and in territorial and sectoral guidelines, with the purpose of translating climate risk into land-use, public investment and infrastructure criteria, guaranteeing climate security and respect for ecosystems and communities.

Nature-based Solutions (NbS) and Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA). Restoration processes for páramos, wetlands and other strategic ecosystems will be scaled up, as well as agroecological practices that strengthen the adaptive capacity and resilience of ecosystems and communities, recognizing nature as a central subject of resilience.

Social and financial protection against loss and damage. Reliable registries, climate insurance and adaptive social protection mechanisms will be promoted to reduce response times and safeguard the livelihoods of the most vulnerable populations, especially those historically marginalized.

Consolidation of SIIVRA as a tool for effectiveness and transparency. This system will ensure that the national adaptation priorities have clear outcome and impact indicators, comparable data across territories and traceability mechanisms over time. In this way, SIIVRA not only guides the definition of priorities, but also ensures the measurement, collective learning and transparency of progress in their implementation.

Indigenous Knowledge Systems: indigenous peoples strengthen their well-being through traditional medicine practices that form part of the Indigenous Own and Intercultural Health System – SISPI, an experience that constitutes a valuable reference for the comprehensive understanding of health and the relationship with nature.

3.2.3 Adaptation Targets and Measures

The National Climate Change Policy establishes that national targets for climate-resilient development must be agreed upon with a medium and long-term vision, in order to evaluate the effectiveness of adaptation against the adverse effects of climate change. These targets are framed within SISCLIMA — which articulates the Intersectoral Climate Change Commission and the Regional Nodes —, the National Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) System for Adaptation, and the National Climate Change Adaptation Plan and the NDC.

In this NDC 3.0, national adaptation priorities are presented that will serve as a fundamental basis for sectors and territories to define, in future planning cycles, specific, robust and measurable targets and measures, in coherence with national strategic objectives. This approach seeks to strengthen ownership, viability and the effectiveness of future actions, ensuring they are solidly anchored in the operational reality and capacities of the responsible actors.

In the development of NDC 3.0, the contribution of the postulates of the Indigenous Movement on Own Economies and Indigenous Food Systems is recognized, which constitute manifestations of knowledge, balance and sustainability in the territories, in harmony with the principles of climate resilience and biodiversity protection.

NDC 3.0 thus establishes a progressive and participatory reference framework to build an increasingly concrete, implementable and financeable adaptation ambition. One of the main lessons learned in the implementation of NDC 2.0 was the difficulty of monitoring adaptation targets, due to the use of an aggregate vulnerability index based on municipal dimensions. In response, NDC 3.0 aligns adaptation efforts with the SIIVRA dimensions as the main tool for monitoring its climate resilience.

In this sense, every adaptation target or measure must be explicitly formulated from a priority SIIVRA dimension, within the Adaptation M&E System. This implies identifying at least one indicator within SIIVRA, defining a baseline (for example, the TCNCC 2017), establishing a periodicity and a monitoring responsible party. With this, Colombia advances toward measuring the effectiveness of adaptation in the medium and long term, strengthening comparability between territories and sectors, the attribution of changes to interventions, the prioritization of resources, and compliance with the Enhanced Transparency Framework of the Paris Agreement.

The Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development has provided technical support to sectors and territories through the development of methodological guidelines for the formulation of adaptation targets, strategies and measures, within the framework of national priorities. These guidelines define minimum technical criteria to guide resilience results, establish reference baselines and structure concrete actions.

This process recognizes sectoral and territorial leadership in climate action, in coherence with their productive, social and environmental dynamics, and integrates learning from planning processes dating back to 2012 or earlier, which have matured within the SISCLIMA framework.

The results of this process are consolidated in the Adaptation Targets and Measures Portfolio, presented as Annex 4 of this NDC 3.0. This portfolio brings together the targets and measures proposed by sectors, territories and other key actors, and will serve as the central instrument to guide the implementation, monitoring and financing of adaptive action in the country.

3.2.4 Support Needs for Adaptation

In 2025, Colombia has a stronger knowledge base, policies and instruments for adaptation to climate change, but also recognizes the challenges that persist in making resilience a reality in its territories and communities. The national balance, captured in BTR1, evidenced important progress, but also obstacles that limit the full implementation of adaptation targets. These limitations translate into support needs in terms of financing, technology transfer, capacity building and strengthening of planning and governance, whose attention is essential to close the implementation gap and accelerate the country's adaptive action.

Needs for adaptation financing: The lack of financial resources is one of the most critical gaps. The continuity of NDC 2.0 adaptation measures requires significant investments. The national, territorial and international financing mobilized to date is far below what is required. The gap estimated at 0.13% of annual GDP (approx. 2 trillion pesos of 2020 each year) (DNP, 2020) needs to be closed to reach the 2030 adaptation targets. It has been estimated that only 76% of the financial needs for adaptation, in terms of national contributions, is covered by annually available resources (Fedesarrollo, 2022); the scenario for adaptation actions under other climate change planning and management instruments could correspond to a much larger gap.

Needs for technology transfer and development: Despite progress achieved in three Technology Needs Assessments (TNAs), including the third currently underway, the adoption and implementation of technologies remains lagging. The country requires stable financing mechanisms and technical assistance with greater scope to ensure the adoption, scaling and sustainability of prioritized technologies, in a way that effectively contributes to the national adaptation objectives.

Needs for capacity building and installation: There remains limited technical and operational capacity at different levels of government to translate targets into effective measures. Although the formulation of Comprehensive Territorial Climate Change Management Plans (PIGCCT), required by Law 1931 of 2018, has advanced, it has not done so homogeneously in quality or implementation. It is necessary to strengthen all climate change management actors so they have the capacity to effectively participate throughout the entire cycle of adaptation management: planning, implementation, monitoring and evaluation.

Needs for planning, governance and other enablers: Action for adaptation requires a robust scaffolding of planning, governance and institutional enablers to guarantee its effectiveness. It is a priority to consolidate SIIVRA as the national monitoring system, strengthening its articulation with sectoral and territorial plans, as well as its contribution to the development of an operational and multilevel Adaptation M&E System (national, sectoral and territorial). Likewise, there is demand for a more effective multilevel governance that ensures coherence between national policies and regional and local action, as well as the meaningful participation of Indigenous Peoples; Black, Afro-Colombian, Raizal and Palenquera communities; the peasantry; and local communities. Added to this are other instrumental lines of the National Climate Change Policy that require momentum, such as the strengthening of applied climate information and science, advancing in basic and applied research, public education and awareness, inter-sectoral articulation, and the incorporation of differential and rights-based approaches.

These needs have been confirmed and enriched by two recent processes:

  • The evaluation of the PNACC (2012) carried out in 2025, which concluded that, while this pioneering instrument allowed knowledge generation and support for the formulation of sectoral and territorial plans, its practical impact has been limited. Progress has been concentrated in diagnoses and pilot projects, without yet achieving structural transformations. Gaps persist in sectoral-territorial articulation, in the incorporation of differential approaches and in the definition of clear and sustainable guidelines. For these reasons, Colombia requires technical and financial support to update the PNACC as an instrument of the PNCC (2017) and the guiding framework for adaptation, so that it can articulate sectors and territories, ensure coherence with other policy instruments (PIGCC, NDC, E2050) and generate verifiable targets at the national, sectoral and territorial level.
  • A national survey (Minambiente [DCCGR], 2025) applied to more than 200 territorial, sectoral and community actors, which confirmed the validity of these gaps and provides new perspectives for their understanding. The survey identified financing as the most urgent need, followed by technical and institutional strengthening and the availability of integrated climate information for decision-making. It also highlighted the importance of guaranteeing the continuity of policies and programs beyond changes in public administration, overcoming cultural and social barriers that limit community appropriation and advancing in the incorporation of differential approaches — especially gender and ethnic — and intersectional ones (human rights, intergenerationality, peace-building).

Faced with these gaps, Colombia recognizes the need for complementary international support in three critical areas: capacity strengthening, climate finance and technology transfer. This support is indispensable to consolidate the integration of adaptation into sectoral and territorial planning, close adaptation financing gaps, accelerate the consolidation of SIIVRA and its evolution toward the national adaptation monitoring and evaluation system, and to strengthen a more inclusive and effective climate governance.

In a complementary manner, the identification and formulation of these needs must be framed within national policies. In particular, the instrumental lines of the PNCC (2017) guide and facilitate the planning, implementation and monitoring of the targets and measures, complementing the means of implementation of the UNFCCC and the Paris Agreement. In turn, the strategic lines of the PNCC (2017), developed as part of its Action Plan together with institutional arrangements and monitoring, tracking, evaluation and reporting systems, constitute the basis for the responsible actors of the targets and measures to precisely identify their support and assistance needs.

The role of decision-making scenarios within the structures of Indigenous Peoples' own governance and the spaces of the Colombian Indigenous Movement, such as the Permanent Concertation Table (MPC) and the Regional Amazonian Table (MRA), in promoting intercultural dialogue and the collective construction of the Climate Action Policy, is recognized.

3.2.5 Implementation of Adaptation Measures and Plans

The main advances in planning, design and implementation of climate change adaptation actions were consolidated in BTR1 (2024), which reports the monitoring of NDC 2.0 adaptation targets. Of the 30 targets included, 28 present some degree of progress and 2 are still in the process of information consolidation.

The report shows that the country has strengthened its technical and institutional capacities and has generated relevant inputs to guide adaptation. However, the balance shows that the vast majority of commitments have remained in phases of studies, diagnoses or pilots of limited scope, without yet translating into concrete adaptation results at the sectoral-territorial scale. The coverage of actions remains registered and the available information does not allow demonstrating with certainty reductions in vulnerability or increases in resilience. The experience confirms that fully implementing the measures and, even more, reporting results in a traceable manner, has been a major challenge. This reflects the urgency of consolidating robust monitoring and evaluation systems such as SIIVRA, which allow moving from planning and diagnosis to the demonstration of effective results. The key learning is that Colombia needs to guarantee continuity, scalability and traceability in implementation, so that the next international reports can show verifiable impacts consistent with national priorities and the Global Adaptation Goal.

3.3 Priorities from Loss and Damage

Colombia recognizes that the loss and damage associated with climate change constitute an economic, social, cultural and ecological reality that must be explicitly integrated into its climate policy. The inclusion of this component in NDC 3.0 reflects the country's commitment to transformative action that addresses both avoidable impacts and those that are already irreversible. This approach transcends the repair of infrastructure, housing or livelihoods, and also incorporates the recognition of non-economic losses, such as the degradation of ecosystems, the loss of cultural heritage and climate-induced human mobility.

The integration of human mobility into the NDC is coherent with already existing national policy frameworks, such as the Long-Term Climate Strategy - E2050, which establishes the governance of human mobility as a key strategy to manage the impacts of climate change, and the National Climate Change Adaptation Plan (PNACC). These instruments provide an orienting framework for articulating inter-institutional and sectoral responses.

In the context of loss and damage, it is fundamental to recognize the value of Indigenous Knowledge Systems for the governance of the Territories, as well as the cultural and spiritual dimensions that guide their understanding of climate impacts. These approaches contribute to interculturality, legitimacy and the comprehensiveness of loss and damage assessment methodologies.

Addressing this issue implies recognizing that there are impacts that cannot be avoided through adaptation and that disproportionately affect vulnerable communities and strategic ecosystems. For this reason, Colombia's strategy is oriented toward strengthening national capacity to anticipate and recover from crises, integrating risk reduction, climate change adaptation and resilient development in all sectors. This effort is sustained by local and territorial actions and social protection mechanisms that promote inclusion and equity.

At the institutional and financial level, the country has two central instruments that lay the foundations to advance toward a national loss and damage framework:

The National Strategy for Resilient and Climate Change-Adapted Recovery with Community Focus, led by the UNGRD and DNP, which establishes standardized damage and loss assessment methodologies (EDAN, EDANPRI), strengthens the Unified Registry of Victims (RUD) and promotes community participation in recovery processes.

The National Financial Protection Strategy against Disaster, Epidemic and Pandemic Risk (ENPFRDEP), led by the Ministry of Finance, which develops risk transfer instruments such as catastrophe bonds and parametric insurance, and immediate liquidity mechanisms to protect macrofiscal stability.

On this basis, Colombia proposes the construction of a National Loss and Damage Framework, which articulates sectoral, territorial and financial actions with the aim of reducing vulnerability, strengthening adaptation and guaranteeing inclusive and resilient recovery processes. As a preliminary step, some sectors have identified priority interventions:

Agricultural sector: consolidate a National Damage and Loss Assessment System for the Agricultural Sector, integrating technological tools, community methodologies and drought surveillance systems. This system will generate relevant and contextual information enabling decision-makers and citizens to define and influence decisions for the sector, strengthening the productive resilience of the sector against recurring climate events

Environment sector: systematically implement the Post-Disaster Environmental Damage and Needs

  • Assessment (EDANA), with special emphasis on protected areas and strategic ecosystems (mangroves, páramos, wetlands, seasonally flooded savannas, tropical dry forests). The measure will include the assessment of damages to livelihoods, ecosystem services, food security, cultural identity and biodiversity, articulating environmental management with disaster risk management.

The technical expansion of these measures is presented in the Adaptation and Loss and Damage Targets and Measures Portfolio, included as Annex 4 of this NDC 3.0. This portfolio consolidates the proposed actions, their implementation responsibilities and support needs.

Colombia underscores that the challenge of addressing loss and damage exceeds national capacities. For this reason, it emphasizes the need for international cooperation and support, both financial and technical, to consolidate management mechanisms that recognize the country's social, economic and environmental particularities. In this framework, the Fund for Responding to Loss and Damage (FRLD), created at COP28, as a multilateral support mechanism for developing countries, acquires special relevance.

With this, the country reaffirms before the international community the need to advance in:

The development of periodic loss and damage reporting by the different countries with the technical and economic support of developed countries. The elaboration of guides on loss and damage response plans by existing international mechanisms. Greater international technical and economic support.

  • Calls to expedite work to fulfill the GST mandate to develop
  • guidelines for loss and damage reporting in the BTRs.

Finally, it is key to emphasize that the approach to loss and damage — including climate human mobility — must be a determining factor in guaranteeing climate action that is just, ambitious and aligned with the global commitments of the Paris Agreement.

As a closing of this chapter, and in coherence with the country's biocultural vision and with the need to articulate climate action with other global and national agendas, this NDC 3.0 presents Annex 6 - Synergies between Biodiversity and Climate Change. This annex develops a strategic perspective on how the climate and biodiversity agendas converge in the territory, in public policy instruments and in productive sectors, highlighting approaches, areas of articulation and opportunities

4. Means of Implementation

Means of implementation are a pillar of NDC 3.0, as they enable Colombia's climate action through the mobilization of financing, technology transfer, capacity strengthening and compliance with transparency commitments. The country advances in the consolidation of enabling conditions, innovative instruments and strategic alliances that direct resources toward climate action, in coherence with Articles 9, 10, 11 and 13 of the Paris Agreement.

This chapter presents Colombia's strategic priorities in each of the areas of financing, technology, capacity strengthening and climate transparency, while Annex 5 – Means of Implementation Measures details the specific portfolio of enabling mechanisms formulated by the responsible entities to support the execution of NDC 3.0. Both the strategic priorities and the portfolio are conceived as a continuous process that monitors what was established in NDC 2.0 and projects toward the long-term vision of E2050.

The country recognizes that, while national efforts have been significant, they are not sufficient given the magnitude of the investments, institutional capacities and transformations required. For this reason, the full implementation of NDC 3.0 demands a more ambitious and sustained international cooperation, which mobilizes resources on fair and concessional terms, facilitates the transfer and adoption of technologies and strengthens national and territorial capacities, including processes of education, training and just transition.

4.1 Climate Finance

Climate finance constitutes an essential pillar for the effective implementation of the mitigation and adaptation targets set in the country's NDC. Given the macroeconomic, political and social conditions, Colombia with its own financial resources does not have the capacity to assume without complementary support the monetary resources required to achieve its climate action objectives.

In this sense, and under the principles of common but differentiated responsibilities, Colombia advocates for concessional financing, in the form of grants from countries with greater responsibilities in emissions, considering that the polluter pays principle must be one of the pillars underpinning climate finance decisions at the global scale. Sources such as those from the private sector are complementary in nature, but in no case can they constitute substitutes for the obligations that the parties have in the transfer of resources from developed countries to developing countries.

Since the NDC update in 2020, Colombia has recognized that the costs of climate action far exceed the internal public financing capacity and that, consequently, a concerted effort is required that does not increase the country's debt burden. Under the New Collective Quantified Goal on Climate Finance, set by the parties during COP29, the need for Colombia, together with other developing countries, to be prioritized in the allocation of international cooperation resources, concessional and grant-based, with the complementary support of the private sector, is evidenced.

Facing the challenges of climate action financing, indigenous peoples raise the importance of exploring mechanisms that enable more direct and equitable access to resources, in harmony with their own governance structures and their priorities in mitigation and adaptation. These perspectives contribute to enriching discussions on NDC 3.0 and just financing approaches for climate action.

Voluntary cooperation under Article 6 of the PA

Colombia, voluntarily and based on its institutional, technical and operational capacities, as well as on the convenience for the fulfillment of its climate action objectives, will establish the institutional, legal and regulatory frameworks, so that, under Article 6.1 of the Paris Agreement, the mechanisms of Article 6 (6.2, 6.4 and 6.8) constitute a potential instrument for the implementation of the country's NDC, in pursuit of accelerating its fulfillment and generating a greater global net ambition, insofar as it is appropriate for the country and its objectives. In this sense, Colombia will develop the necessary regulations to implement in a comprehensive and transparent manner its participation in said mechanisms, guaranteeing environmental integrity, traceability in the corresponding adjustments, interoperability of national registries and compliance with the sustainable development criteria established at the international level.

4.1.1 National Climate Finance Strategy (ENFC)

The National Climate Finance Strategy (ENFC), published in 2017, forms an integral part of the instrumental planning line for climate change management of the PNCC (2017). In 2022 it was reformulated (Fedesarrollo et al., 2022) to align with the targets of the NDC updated in 2020 and considering the evolution of the climate finance environment in the country.

The objective of the ENFC (2022) is to efficiently, sustainably, scalably and transparently mobilize resources to finance mitigation and adaptation initiatives throughout their entire cycle, achieving national climate change targets with equity and justice.

The ENFC constitutes the country's roadmap on climate finance, oriented toward creating enabling conditions, mobilizing sources and connecting projects with resources. This strategic framework seeks to consolidate a more transparent and reliable financial ecosystem, broaden access to international and national sources, and generate mechanisms that effectively link the public and private sectors with climate action. In this way, Colombia advances toward a climate finance system aligned with the commitments of the Paris Agreement and with the effective implementation of its NDC.

The ENFC constitutes one of the main enablers included in the means of implementation portfolio, as detailed in Annex 5.

4.1.2 Instrumentos financieros innovadores

Colombia advances in the exploration and consolidation of innovative instruments that expand the available portfolio of options, such as the expansion of the Green Taxonomy toward biodiversity and adaptation topics, and the development of the Just Socio-ecological and Energy Transition Portfolio. These mechanisms, together with already existing instruments such as the carbon tax, environmental funds and carbon market schemes, reinforce the national strategy to mobilize resources at the required scale.

Debt-for-nature/climate swaps

Colombia faces a triple crisis of debt, nature and climate, where growing fiscal sustainability pressures seriously limit the country's capacity to invest in climate action. According to the first report of the Debt, Nature and Climate expert review, many emerging and developing economies (EMDCs), (also called low and middle income countries), are trapped in a vicious cycle in which climate and environmental disasters generate greater financing needs, increase public debt and reduce fiscal space to address precisely those crises. (Expert Review on Debt, Nature and Climate, 2024).

The Amazon and the Colombian Pacific, territories with a high concentration of biodiversity and cultural wealth, are inhabited by indigenous, Afro-descendant and peasant communities that have historically been the guardians of these ecosystems. These communities face growing threats due to the expansion of the agricultural frontier, state abandonment and the effects of climate change. The social and ecological debt with these territories demands structural responses. Specifically, the Colombian Amazon, which represents approximately 40% of the national territory, is threatened by deforestation, environmental degradation and pressure on the indigenous peoples who inhabit it.

At the same time, the debt burden limits fiscal space to invest in conservation and a transition toward sustainability. In this sense, the debt-for-nature swap is presented as an innovative solution that can free resources for climate action and biodiversity protection, while improving fiscal sustainability at the national level, and at the same time ensuring the survival of the ways and means of living of the communities that inhabit these ancestral territories.

In this sense, implementing a debt-for-nature swap mechanism that allows channeling sustained payments for the conservation of strategic areas of the Amazon and the Colombian Pacific would allow a significant reduction of the payment of interest on external debt — the debt service — of Colombia (focused on bilateral and multilateral creditors) in exchange for binding commitments for the conservation and restoration of the Amazon, accompanied by transparency mechanisms and community participation.

What is a debt-for-nature swap?

A debt-for-nature swap is a financial instrument that allows reducing the amount or service of a country's external debt in exchange for commitments and direct investments in ecosystem conservation, environmental restoration and climate actions. These agreements can involve partial debt cancellation, its repurchase on preferential terms or the redirection of payments toward national environmental funds.8

This mechanism does not imply a commodification of nature since the debt-for-nature swap can be designed to recognize the intrinsic value of ecosystems, not as tradable assets, but as essential common goods for life and human well-being. In this way, instead of converting biodiversity into a commodity (or its ecosystem services, as in the case of carbon markets), this mechanism ensures the permanence and functionality of nature through sustained and verifiable financial commitments. Furthermore, when structured with the participation of local communities, this instrument can strengthen inclusive and just environmental governance models, in response to the costs to which these communities are exposed in the framework of climate resilience.

Therefore, more than an economic transaction exercise, the swap is an act of ecological reparation and environmental justice, in which the countries that have most contributed to the global environmental crisis, (within the framework of unequal ecological exchange), assume part of the costs of its solution, supporting biodiverse countries like Colombia in their efforts to conserve what is ultimately a heritage of all humanity.

Over the last decades, several countries have implemented debt-for-nature swaps with results in terms of conservation as well as the strengthening of their institutional capacities; see the examples of Seychelles, Belize and Ecuador.9

Fund for Life and Biodiversity

The Fund for Life and Biodiversity is a financial instrument created in 2023 by the Government of Colombia with the objective of channeling national and international resources toward the conservation of strategic ecosystems and the strengthening of the country's environmental management with a long-term vision. This mechanism responds to the need to have a stable and sustainable source of financing that complements the efforts of the public budget and international cooperation, supporting national targets in biodiversity, climate action and sustainable development.

Its design under a patrimonial logic, administered by a trust company, gives the Fund the capacity to invest in a programmed and staggered manner, generating financial certainty for projects that require continuity beyond governmental cycles, such as ecological restoration, comprehensive water resource management or the recovery of degraded soils.

Complementarily, the Fund allows leveraging investments that integrate nature-based solutions with physical infrastructure, such as sustainable urban drainage systems, green corridors or adaptation projects in cities and rural territories. It also acts as a convergence point for strategic alliances with international partners, the private sector, local communities and civil society organizations, strengthening technical and financial cooperation.

The projects financed through the Fund offer a natural synergy between biodiversity and climate change, given that conservation, restoration and sustainable ecosystem use actions simultaneously contribute to emission reductions, carbon capture and the adaptation of vulnerable communities. In this way, it is positioned as a strategic instrument to advance in the integrated fulfillment of the NDC and global biodiversity targets, maximizing resource efficiency and enhancing the environmental, social and economic benefits of investments in climate action.

It should be clarified that resources destined for biodiversity and those oriented toward climate action are not substitutes for each other, as they respond to different international commitments even though they fulfill complementary functions. In the monitoring of financial flows, a clear differentiation must be maintained that allows reporting specific advances against each Convention and avoids the dilution of obligations. Nevertheless, in the realm of project investment it is pertinent to recognize and strengthen the synergies between both agendas, so that actions simultaneously contribute to conservation and climate action targets, in coherence with the principle of common but differentiated responsibilities.

One of the most relevant advances in climate finance in Colombia has been the development of the Green Taxonomy, an instrument that guides the financial system toward projects and investments aligned with the objectives of carbon neutrality and climate resilience. This tool constitutes a common language to classify and evaluate economic activities that contribute to environmental sustainability, offering certainty to both investors and public and private entities in the mobilization of resources.

The consolidation of the green taxonomy implies two strategic dimensions. First, its technical deepening to ensure that eligibility criteria reflect international standards, while recognizing the particularities of the Colombian context in social, territorial and environmental matters. Second, its expanded use within the financial system, not only as a guide for the design of credit products or thematic bonds, but also as a reference framework for budget planning and the definition of sectoral public policies.

The evolution of the taxonomy demands expansion toward critical themes such as biodiversity, climate change adaptation and comprehensive sustainability. This expansion responds to the need to recognize that economic and social resilience depends to a great extent on ecosystem integrity, climate risk management and the just transition. In this sense, the inclusion of criteria associated with biodiversity protection and ecosystem services strengthens the link between finance and natural capital, transcending the reductionist vision of sustainability centered solely on carbon emissions.

The incorporation of adaptation into the green taxonomy is equally a priority. Colombia, as a country highly vulnerable to climate change impacts, requires financial instruments that channel resources toward risk management measures, resilient infrastructure, sustainable agrifood systems and nature-based solutions. A financial system guided by these criteria not only leverages the reduction of vulnerabilities, but also contributes to reducing the fiscal costs associated with disasters and extreme climate events.

Finally, the consolidation of the green taxonomy as a comprehensive and complex mechanism implies recognizing that sustainability is not limited to environmental indicators, but must incorporate social, economic and cultural dimensions. This requires inclusive governance mechanisms, robust measurement, reporting and verification systems, and articulation with international standards that strengthen the country's credibility before global investors. In this way, the taxonomy becomes a bridge between climate policy, the protection of natural capital and economic development, expanding the portfolio of available financial options and positioning Colombia as a regional benchmark in green finance.

Portfolio for the Just Socio-ecological and Energy Transition

The Portfolio for the Just Socio-ecological and Energy Transition (hereinafter Socio-ecological Transition Portfolio) is a set of projects that guides Colombia's transformation toward a low-carbon, resilient and biodiverse economy. The portfolio has the objective of: supporting the development of the roadmap for the just energy and socio-ecological transition, which, in addition to promoting the deployment of new installed electrical generation capacity from non-conventional renewable energy sources, seeks to generate alternatives and capacities for the transformation of the productive matrix, and develop a long-term strategy to reduce economic dependence on fossil fuels. The portfolio is also known as the country platform which is an integral and innovative roadmap that denotes the national efforts to fulfill the climate and biodiversity commitments of the country, such as the NDC, the Biodiversity Action Plan (PAB), as well as national policy instruments (such as the comprehensive deforestation containment plan 2023-2026).

The socio-ecological transition portfolio contains a battery of projects grouped on the country platform, in turn framed in 5 pillars, intimately connected by the enabling conditions axis (which energize the necessary conditions for the country to implement the entire portfolio). This portfolio brings together a total of 120 projects, for an approximate value of USD $38,962 million, distributed as follows: I) Climate Change Adaptation (8 projects, USD $26 million); II) Systems Productive Sustainable Systems and of the Biodiversity Economy (20 projects, USD$ 687 million); III) Just Energy Transition and Reindustrialization (34 projects, USD$ 20,596 million); IV) Conservation and Restoration of Ecosystems (42 projects, USD$ 852 million), and V) Enabling Conditions and Mechanisms for a Just Transition (16 projects, USD$ 16,801 million). As of the date, funding of 10.6% has been secured, with resources primarily from the State for the entire portfolio.

For this reason, the country recognizes the Socio-ecological Transition Portfolio as one of the important economic instruments for climate action. While it does not represent the country's total financing need, it does constitute an innovation that denotes the importance for the country of having clear amounts to finance climate needs.

Climate finance - Strategic priorities

NDC 3.0 projects as Colombia's strategic priorities: consolidate the National Climate Finance Strategy, explore and consolidate innovative financial instruments that expand the portfolio of available options, and mobilize international and national financial resources — public and private — to close the resource gap without increasing the country's debt burden.

4.2 Technology Development and Transfer

Colombia reaffirms that the transfer and development of climate technologies are an essential means of implementation to fulfill the objectives of NDC 3.0 and advance toward carbon neutrality by 2050. In this framework, the country has undertaken the process of developing its third Technology Needs Assessment (TNA) and the construction of four Technological Action Plans (TAP), for each of the 4 sectors prioritized within the project, financed by the Green Climate Fund. In this case, the prioritized sectors are understood as the grouping of themes that require with greater urgency the effective technology transfer in Colombia, according to the country's climate policy. The foregoing is carried out in order to identify, prioritize and mobilize projects that effectively respond to the mitigation and adaptation challenges.

The sectors prioritized in the TNA-TAP process are:

  • Water-energy-food nexus, sanitary sovereignty and social well-being
  • Nature-based Solutions (NbS) for the AFOLU Sector
  • Resilient Infrastructure and Buildings for Smart Biodiversity
  • Sustainable Energy Systems and Electromobility

The Colombia TNA-TAP 2025 process is in the process of updating the country's technological priorities, consolidating a portfolio of climate projects that guides the achievement of resources through international cooperation and investment. This effort, led by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (Designated National Entity of the CTCN), the National Planning Department (Designated National Authority) and Fondo Acción (executing entity), within the framework of the SISCLIMA, integrates a broad spectrum of actors: public institutions, academia, research centers, the private sector, international cooperation and civil society, generating a more robust and participatory climate innovation ecosystem. Likewise, the project seeks to institutionalize this process so that effective monitoring of the implementation of the TAPs can be guaranteed.

This effort allows prioritizing critical technologies for mitigation and adaptation, while integrating the results into sectoral and territorial planning and into the set of technological measures included in the means of implementation portfolio (see Annex 5).

On the other hand, sector ministries have identified other key themes to be addressed at the country level in order to facilitate the implementation of climate targets. This has translated into six priority areas for technological action to support mitigation and adaptation targets:

  • Renewable energy and energy efficiency, with emphasis on clean generation, storage and efficient management of demand and industrial processes.
  • Sustainable transport and electromobility, as a central axis of decarbonization of the transport sector and the reduction of local pollutants.
  • Climate-smart agriculture, with water efficiency practices, precision irrigation and sustainable management of soils and crops.
  • Environmental monitoring, modeling and information systems, fundamental for climate risk management, decision-making and the strengthening of the National Climate Change Information System.
  • Resilient infrastructure and sustainable buildings, oriented toward the reduction of urban and rural vulnerabilities to extreme climate events, incorporating energy efficiency and water management solutions.
  • Nature-based solutions (NbS) and ecosystem restoration, as comprehensive adaptation and mitigation strategies that strengthen biodiversity and ecosystem services.

These areas represent the convergence between the priorities of NDC 3.0 and the 2050 Strategy, ensuring coherence between Colombia's national needs and international commitments on climate technology.

International cooperation commitment

Colombia reaffirms that closing the technological gap requires more ambitious and sustained international cooperation, which facilitates access to climate technologies, financing for their adoption and the creation of national capacities. The country is committed to continuing to strengthen joint work with the Climate Technology Center and Network (CTCN), bilateral and multilateral cooperation, and international funds, to accelerate the implementation of Colombia's TNAs, as well as the transfer and development of technology.

All this to ensure that its sector ministries and territories have the necessary tools to face climate change.

Technology Development and Transfer – Strategic Priorities

NDC 3.0 projects as Colombia's strategic priorities: consolidate the TNA-TAP as a periodic technological prioritization process, strengthen the transfer and adoption of technologies in key sectors, and articulate these efforts with the 2050 Strategy and the country's international commitments. Colombia will strengthen the Climate Change Technical Scientific Information Committee so that it drives climate change decisions from a national climate innovation ecosystem that integrates science, the private sector, international cooperation and local knowledge, in order to ensure that the technologies transferred and developed are pertinent, inclusive and sustainable.

4.3 Capacity Building

Colombia recognizes that capacity strengthening is a cross-cutting pillar for the implementation of NDC 3.0, in accordance with what is established in Article 11 of the Paris Agreement. This component encompasses the development of institutional, scientific, technical, educational, community and labor capacities, necessary for climate action to be carried out effectively, in an informed and sustainable manner. Colombia recognizes that these capacities must be strengthened continuously, progressively and over the long term, integrating science, local and ancestral knowledge, climate education, labor training for the just transition and institutional consolidation in sectors and territories.

4.3.1 Climate and Environmental Education

The Ministry of Education, in coordination with the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development, leads the update of the National Environmental Education Policy (PNEA), a participatory process that incorporates human rights, gender, intergenerationality, cultural diversity and territoriality approaches. With this, the aim is to consolidate cross-cutting climate education at all levels of the educational system, from early childhood to higher education and for work and human development.

NDC 3.0 reaffirms that climate training and education must respond to the sociocultural realities of the country, integrating scientific knowledge with local and ancestral knowledge to enrich educational processes and guarantee a just and equitable transition.

National Strategy for Education, Training and Public Awareness on Climate Change (ENEFSPCC)

For Colombia, education, awareness and public training on climate change is a key element in the process of generating awareness and responsibility about society's actions.

In coherence with Article 6 of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change, the ENEFSPCC guides actions aimed at promoting a climate culture that fosters co-responsibility, informed understanding of the phenomenon and the transformation of attitudes and behaviors toward climate risks and opportunities.

The strategy seeks to expand public access to clear, timely and relevant information about the causes, impacts and responses to climate change, as well as to strengthen individual and collective awareness through education, communication and awareness-raising processes. To this end, it promotes the development of scientific, technical and social capacities, and supports research and the production of relevant knowledge for decision-making and territorial climate change management.

Likewise, the ENEFSPCC promotes public participation in the planning, implementation and monitoring of programs and projects related to climate change, in harmony with the principles of the Escazú Agreement. This approach strengthens community processes and contributes to the appreciation of local and traditional knowledge, essential for adaptation and climate risk management.

Finally, the strategy promotes articulation and complementarity among the various existing environmental, climate and scientific education initiatives in the country, with the aim of improving their scope, effectiveness and impact. This articulation allows directing efforts toward common objectives, facilitating the monitoring and evaluation of interventions and generating inputs for the continuous strengthening of capacities at the local, regional and national levels.

4.3.2 Scientific Training and Knowledge Generation

Strengthening human capital for climate action includes the training of climate change scientists, with advanced competencies in research, modeling, impact assessment and the development of technological and nature-based solutions. Likewise, NDC 3.0 recognizes the importance of aligning science, technology and innovation production in climate change with periodic scientometric and traditional knowledge diagnoses, which allow identifying current knowledge areas, research gaps and priorities for national and territorial institutions. The integration of these diagnoses with the Climate Change Technical Scientific Information Committee of SISCLIMA, the four-year environmental research plans and the National Climate Change Communication will ensure technical, scientific and traditional knowledge inputs for the construction of the NDC, the PIGCCTs, and other instruments such as the ECDBC, the PNACC and the PNCC.

The integration of this knowledge will strengthen the methodologies, collection, storage, analysis and dissemination of information required by the National Climate Change Information System and its subsystems, contributing to improving the quality of evidence available for climate action at the national and territorial levels. This strategic priority seeks to consolidate national capacity for evidence-based decision-making, reduce dependence on external inputs and promote scientific innovation as a support for public policies and the implementation of the NDC.

4.3.3 Institutional and Territorial Strengthening

Beyond capacity building, institutional and territorial strengthening is conceived as a pillar of climate governance in Colombia and a means of implementation to ensure that the NDC 3.0 targets translate into effective, continuous and articulated actions throughout the country. It is oriented toward consolidating an institutional environment capable of planning, coordinating, implementing and monitoring climate action with a multilevel, multi-sectoral and territorial approach.

Colombia advances in a process of strengthening the National Climate Change System (SISCLIMA), including the updating of its institutional architecture. This effort seeks to ensure that climate action planning, execution and monitoring processes have sufficient and stable technical, administrative and financial capacities.

Institutional strengthening also implies ensuring that national, territorial and sectoral entities integrate climate change into their management instruments — development plans, budgets, sectoral policies, spending frameworks, environmental instruments, land-use plans — in a manner consistent with NDC 3.0.

In the territories, institutional strengthening is expressed in the capacity of local governments, environmental authorities, ethnic communities and social organizations to implement mitigation and adaptation actions, as well as to participate in SISCLIMA governance processes. This strengthening implies guaranteeing stable resources, continuous technical assistance and tools that facilitate the integration of climate change into local management and territorial decision-making.

Finally, NDC 3.0 recognizes that the consolidation of a modern, interoperable, inclusive and evidence-based SISCLIMA is indispensable for the country's climate ambition.

4.3.4 Just Workforce Transition Strategy

Colombia's Just Workforce Transition Strategy is incorporated in NDC 3.0 as a cross-cutting, articulating and enabling instrument for the fulfillment of mitigation and adaptation targets, as well as for the effective implementation of sectoral productive transformation and sustainability agendas. Its scope is national and multi-sectoral, oriented toward the entire national workforce, recognizing that transition processes require robust social protection mechanisms, labor reconversion and capacity building that guarantee that no one is left behind.

With this purpose, the strategy is conceived as a cross-cutting axis to all economic sectors and territories of the country, ensuring that decarbonization, technological innovation and the energy transition are accompanied by active employment policies, comprehensive training, labor guidance and insertion and reinsertion actions that promote the generation of decent and sustainable work. Its implementation is based on a multilevel and multi-sectoral approach that calls upon the State, the productive sector, workers and their organizations, academia and international cooperation to anticipate labor impacts and take advantage of green and sustainable employment opportunities.

The strategy ensures that each sector integrates a robust labor component into its transition plans and that measures are adapted to the needs and realities of the regions through social dialogue and the participation of diverse actors, strengthening just transition governance.

The implementation horizon of the Just Workforce Transition Strategy is projected to 2030, in coherence with the commitments established in NDC 3.0. This timeframe allows structuring and consolidating progressive labor reconversion mechanisms, strengthening workforce training, promoting the creation of green jobs and ensuring articulation with territorial and sectoral agendas. In turn, it aligns with the Long-Term Climate Strategy - E2050, acting as an operational pillar for achieving carbon neutrality through the strengthening of capacities in strategic sectors and the anticipation of labor impacts.

Finally, the strategy is based on the principles of decent work and the protection of labor rights. This implies preparing the workforce for the jobs of the future, protecting their incomes and career paths, and guaranteeing dignity in transformation processes. In this way, the Just Workforce Transition Strategy ensures that NDC 3.0 advances not only toward climate ambition, but also toward an equitable, inclusive and sustainable development model.

Capacity building - Strategic priorities

NDC 3.0 projects as Colombia's strategic priorities: consolidate cross-cutting climate education at all levels; strengthen the just transition through the progressive implementation of the Just Workforce Transition Strategy, guaranteeing training for green employment and labor reconversion; train climate change scientists and strengthen the generation of scientific, technical and ancestral knowledge; and consolidate institutional and territorial strengthening processes with stable resources that allow integrating climate change into sectoral and local management. Likewise, the country will promote the articulation between science, research and local knowledge to improve the quality of available climate information — including that generated by the SNICC systems — and to strengthen the education, training and awareness processes of the different SISCLIMA actors, ensuring informed, inclusive and evidence-based climate action.

4.4 Climate Transparency

Transparency constitutes an enabling means of NDC 3.0, insofar as it ensures that climate information is used strategically for decision-making, the monitoring of international commitments, the mobilization of financing and sectoral and territorial planning. In this framework, Colombia has strengthened its institutional architecture through the creation of the National Climate Change Information System (SNICC), regulated by Resolution 1383 of 2023 and integrated into the Environmental Information System of Colombia (SIAC). The SNICC integrates three specialized systems: i) the mitigation MRV system, ii) the Monitoring and Evaluation system for adaptation, and iii) the climate finance MRV system. This national architecture responds to what is established in the National Climate Change Policy, Law 1931 of 2018 and Law 2169 of 2021, and consolidates an official and harmonized framework for climate information management in the country.

Additionally, Colombia has the +Climate platform, developed by the National Planning Department in compliance with paragraph 1 of Article 24 of Law 2169 of 2021. This platform constitutes the official tool of the Monitoring and Reporting Scheme for the targets and measures established in that Law and in the NDC, including its 3.0 update. +Climate consolidates the information reported by the responsible entities and organizes sectoral and territorial advances in mitigation, adaptation and means of implementation. Its operation complements national climate information systems and provides necessary inputs for the traceability and periodic monitoring of the country's commitments under the Paris Agreement.

These information systems generate inputs to comply with the Enhanced Transparency Framework (ETF) of the Paris Agreement. The presentation of the First Biennial Transparency Report (BTR1, 2024) represented a significant advance in the consolidation of methodologies, the generation of consistent reports and the monitoring of mitigation, adaptation and climate finance actions. At the same time, relevant challenges persist. The country needs to consolidate the periodic functioning of the National System of GHG Emission and Absorption Inventories (SINGEI), fully operate the National Registry of GHG Emission Reductions (RENARE), develop the GHG Reduction and Removal Accounting System (SCRR-GEI), and strengthen the articulation with territories in the application of the methodologies and results of the Integrated Information System on Vulnerability, Risk and Adaptation (SIIVRA).

Likewise, Colombia will need to advance in the development of methodologies to map and monitor capacity strengthening actions, technology transfers and climate finance, in order to identify progress, gaps and emerging needs aligned with NDC implementation. This will require clarity in institutional roles, interoperability between sectoral and territorial systems, and technical capacities sustained over time.

Beyond international reporting, climate transparency fulfills an enabling function at the national level. The availability of reliable, consistent and comparable information strengthens accountability to citizens, provides credibility before investors and cooperating parties, and provides fundamental inputs for territorial, sectoral and budgetary planning. For this reason, the country recognizes that a robust climate transparency system requires not only technological platforms and regulatory frameworks, but also institutional sustainability, specialized human talent and articulation between science, technical knowledge and local knowledge.

In coherence with the foregoing, various strategic actions for the strengthening of climate change information systems in the country are detailed as specific measures in the Means of Implementation portfolio, presented in Annex 5. Their implementation will allow advancing comprehensively in the requirements of the Enhanced Transparency Framework, improving the traceability of mitigation and adaptation commitments, and strengthening international confidence in Colombia's climate action.

Finally, Colombia recognizes the need to integrate the monitoring schemes and targets linked to climate transparency with platforms associated with biodiversity, disaster risk management, Sustainable Development Goals and other international commitments. This systemic approach will enable more consistent, periodic and effective reporting, and will contribute to the consolidation of an interoperable, scalable and inclusive national climate information ecosystem that underpins the country's trajectory toward resilience and carbon neutrality by 2050.

Annex 1. Participatory Pathway in the Update of the

NDC 3.0

The update of Colombia's NDC — that is, the climate commitments the country presents to the world — is not just an international formality. It is, above all, an opportunity to decide together how we want to face climate change and protect our future. Although these targets are announced at the national level, their true strength lies in what happens in the territories. Mayor's offices, governorships, Indigenous peoples, Black, Afro-Colombian, Raizal and Palenquero communities, peasant communities, and organizations

Inviting local actors to participate is opening a space where all voices count. It is a collective process that allows us to listen to each other, recognize our differences and build together a shared vision of the climate and the territory. This dialogue not only adds ideas: it generates trust, commitment and a sense of co-responsibility that strengthens local governance. Furthermore, it seeks to ensure that the country's targets do not remain on paper, but are appropriated by communities and translated into action.

Territorial dialogues also help identify what opportunities exist to advance toward more sustainable development and what social, economic or institutional obstacles might appear along the way. And, perhaps most importantly, they open space for those who have historically been least heard to speak: indigenous peoples, Black, Afro-Colombian, Raizal and Palenquero communities, peasant farmers, rural women and other groups who directly live and feel the impacts of climate. Incorporating

For Colombia to truly advance in climate action, it is essential to understand what is happening in the territories: what opportunities already exist to drive low-carbon and resilient development, and what are the social, economic or institutional barriers that could slow that path. The best way to discover this is by listening directly to those who live and work in those territories. That is why social dialogues become valuable: since they open

With this purpose, the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development, through the Directorate of Climate Change and Risk Management, organized a series of workshops designed to bring together different actors: institutions, academia, social organizations, the private sector and communities. The idea is simple but powerful: sit down to talk, share experiences, combine knowledge and build proposals that help update the NDC with feet firmly in the territory. In this way,

Furthermore, these dialogues allowed recognizing what was learned in the previous update (NDC 2.0 of 2020) and collecting new perspectives and approaches that reflect how the territories and their needs have changed. It is not only about talking about risks or threats, but also about identifying local capacities, the opportunities that already exist and the proposals that emerge from the communities. All this helps drive climate action that not only responds to climate change, but transforms

This exercise in citizen participation, where different actors and levels of government come together, not only enriches the conversation about climate: it also strengthens and lends legitimacy to the commitments Colombia assumes before the world. Both the Escazú Agreement and the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) recognize that listening to people is key to making fairer and more effective decisions, especially in territories living with socio-environmental conflicts.

Therefore, in the update of NDC 3.0, Colombia decided to incorporate a clear pathway for citizen participation. This pathway facilitated access to information, opened spaces for communities to participate in decision-making and promoted environmental justice. The idea is to move from a model where policies come from above, to one where territories not only receive instructions, but also create, propose and help shape climate solutions. This is how governance is built

In this third version of the NDC, Colombia defines its national priorities in three broad areas: mitigation, adaptation and means of implementation. These three pillars structure a more coherent, inclusive vision for the country, oriented toward transforming our development model. A vision that recognizes that climate action is only effective when it is born of dialogue, recognition of differences and the real participation of those who live and care for the territories every day.

SCOPE

This annex corresponds to the description of the NDC 3.0 update pathway. Its scope accounts for the development of territorial workshops; climate risk analyses, and territorial proposals in mitigation, adaptation and means of implementation. Through thematic stations, printed matrices, digital surveys and deliberative plenary sessions; within the framework of mitigation, adaptation and means of implementation actions, necessary to advance toward effective, just and transformative climate action.

GENERAL OBJECTIVE

To promote broad and diverse participation by territorial actors in the NDC update, integrating local and institutional knowledge and differential approaches to guide the definition of climate targets and measures. This intention is inspired by the principles of climate justice, the human rights approach, intergenerational equity, differential approaches and the dialogue of knowledge that recognizes and values the different ways of understanding and inhabiting the territory.

SPECIFIC OBJECTIVES

  • To favor broad territorial and population-level participation, promoting institutions, ethnic communities, social organizations, academia and the productive sector to contribute from their diversity, with representativeness criteria and differential approaches.
  • To value and collect technical, community and symbolic contributions, creating spaces where the dialogue of knowledge around climate risks, vulnerabilities and local capacities allows integrating technical, ancestral and experiential knowledge into the NDC.
  • To foster joint review of NDC progress and barriers, inviting collective analysis of the commitments made in version 2.0, to identify achievements, gaps and challenges from the perspective of the territories.
  • To promote transparency and the return of contributions, ensuring that the inputs collected are systematized in clear formats and accessible digital repositories.

PARTICIPATING STAKEHOLDERS

Citizen participation in territorial dialogues is key for Colombia to advance genuinely and fairly in its commitments in the face of climate change. Both the UNFCCC and the Escazú Agreement emphasize that the most important decisions must be built by listening to people, and this is even more true in a country as diverse as Colombia. Therefore, the Territorial Participatory Pathway for the NDC 3.0 update was developed with the participation of institutional actors,

Therefore, different actors were convened to guarantee territorial representativeness, climatic diversity, methodological viability and political impact. This is not a random selection, but a decision based on the following reasons:

  • Ecological and climatic representativeness
  • Vulnerability and climate risk
  • Sociocultural and ethnic diversity
  • Institutional capacity and territorial articulation
  • Logistical viability and regional coverage

Various actors were convened with the objective of guaranteeing a multi-actor, inclusive dialogue representative of Colombia's territorial realities. The actors were the following:

  • The 17 departments (see map 1) and their public institutions such as Mayor's offices,

Governorships, environment, planning, health, agriculture, risk management secretariats, acting as territorial hosts for the participatory workshops:

  • The regional climate change nodes:
  • Antioquia Node and Pacific North Node (Antioquia, Chocó),
  • Caribbean and Insular Node (La Guajira, Bolívar, San Andrés, Providencia and Santa Catalina,

  • Nodo Pacífico Sur (Valle, Cauca, Nariño)

  • Nodo Centro Oriente Andino (Boyacá, Cundinamarca, Tolima, Huila, Bogotá

(Distrito))

  • Civil society and community organizations, which make visible differentiated impacts, promote equity and strengthen the legitimacy of the process from the social base, such as women's networks, youth collectives, victims' associations and community action councils.
  • Ethnic peoples and local communities, which represent ancestral knowledge, territorial visions and their own adaptation experiences.
  • Academia and research centers, which provide scientific evidence, risk analysis, and technical proposals for adaptation and mitigation, such as universities, technical institutes, regional study centers.
  • Cooperation bodies and NGOs, which support with technical and methodological resources, and promote sustainability, human rights and climate justice approaches, such as UNDP, GIZ, WWF, TNC, local environmental organizations.
  • The productive and business sector, as key actors in the implementation of mitigation measures and productive transition.

Map 1. Departments Participating in Territorial Dialogues

Some limitations are recognized such as unequal participation between regions, absence of some nodes, differences in technical capacity between departments and restrictions specific to territorial connectivity. However, these are aspects that enrich the process and contribute valuable lessons learned for subsequent update cycles.

PARTICIPATORY MECHANISM ADOPTED

The territorial participatory pathway is, in essence, the path Colombia has chosen to ensure that the NDC 3.0 update is built by listening to the territories. This mechanism seeks broad, multi-actor and territorially representative participation, without replacing prior consultation. It is grounded in Law 2169 of 2021 and the Escazú Agreement, under principles of effective participation, differential approach, multilevel governance, climate justice, transparency, access to information and dialogue.

Furthermore, this vision is aligned with polycentric climate governance, which understands that territories live different realities and that each has its own capacities, knowledge and challenges. Therefore, guaranteeing a differential and intergenerational approach is key so that those who have historically been marginalized — rural women, ethnic communities, youth, community organizations — can influence decisions that affect their territory.

This pathway was organized in three phases — pre-preparation, methodological deployment and systematization — which help coordinate moments, tools and responsibilities with the national process of updating the NDC 3.0 and strengthen climate governance.

Ultimately, the participatory pathway is the bridge that connects territorial voices with national decisions about climate. It is the path for the country's targets to respond not only to international commitments, but also to the reality, wisdom and hope of those who inhabit and care for the territories every day.

CITIZEN PARTICIPATION METHODOLOGY ADOPTED

The methodology applied for the development of the Territorial Participatory Pathway for the NDC update was designed by the Directorate of Climate Change of the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development and executed with the support of a specialized technical team, territorial facilitators and sectoral experts. With this, informative, consultative and deliberative participation was guaranteed, without replacing mechanisms of prior consultation or other formal participation instruments defined by law.

The methodology is grounded in the principles of effective participation, differential approach, climate justice, human rights, traceability, feedback and transparency, in coherence with Law 2169 of 2021 and the Escazú Agreement. It recognizes that climate change affects territories differently, so the construction of inputs for the NDC must integrate local knowledge, technical expertise and contextualized proposals.

As mentioned above, the methodology was developed in three phases: pre-preparation, methodological deployment and systematization. In the first, the mapping and convening of key actors is carried out, technical inputs are prepared and logistical conditions are organized. In the second, participatory territorial workshops are held with a sequence of nine moments that include symbolic, technical and deliberative activities, such as risk analysis, evaluation of NDC 2.0 targets,

METHODOLOGICAL DEPLOYMENT OF WORKSHOPS

As a result of the methodological deployment phase of the Territorial Participatory Pathway, developed in the 17 departments, inputs were consolidated on climate risks and proposals for territorial targets, built from multi-actor dialogue and the contextual reading of each territory. These results correspond to participatory contributions of an orienting and non-binding nature, which technically inform the NDC update.

7.1. Analysis of Risks and Threats Derived

The gathering of information on risks in the territorial workshops had as its central objective to identify the specific realities and vulnerabilities of each department. The Risk Station allowed identifying, from the territorial perspective, the main climate threats, affected ecosystems, pressured ecosystem services, vulnerable populations and impacted rights. This activity sought to identify threats derived from climate change through the interpretation of maps
