Table 2. Prevalent Findings from the Analysis — Dimension of Analysis

Dimension of Analysis

National Synthesis of Findings

1. Climate hazards
  • Floods (Caribbean, Andean, Amazon).
  • Seasonal droughts (Caribbean, Orinoquía, part of the Andes).
  • Heat waves (Amazonia, Orinoquía, Caribbean).
  • Mass movements and landslides (Andes, Coffee-Growing Region).
  • Extreme rainfall associated with La Niña.
  • Coastal erosion in departments with coastlines (Atlántico, Bolívar, Sucre, La Guajira).
  • Deforestation as an anthropogenic threat exacerbated by climate (Caquetá, Guaviare, Guainía).

2. Most affected ecosystems
  • Tropical humid forests (Amazonia and Pacific).
  • Páramos and high mountains (Cundinamarca, Boyacá, Caldas, Tolima).
  • Coastal systems (mangroves, beaches).
  • Transformed agricultural areas that increase vulnerability.
  • Water surfaces affected by sedimentation, temperature increase and variability.

3. Ecosystem services
  • Water regulation (páramos, forests).
  • Water supply for consumption.
  • Climate regulation (forest loss, extreme events).
  • Cultural and identity services affected by erosion, displacement or degradation.

4. Vulnerable populations
  • Children and adolescents, due to health, nutrition and education.
  • Elderly people (reduced mobility, health).
  • Ethnic populations (indigenous, Afro-descendants, Raizal, ROM).
  • Persons with disabilities.
  • Women (caregiving, housing, livelihoods).
  • LGBTIQ+ population on issues of participation, housing and security.

5. Rights at risk
  • Life and health from thermal extremes, floods, vectors and pollution.
  • Housing, due to floods, landslides and coastal erosion.
  • Work and productivity, especially rural.
  • Nutrition and food security due to droughts and crop loss.
  • Education due to interruptions and infrastructure damage.

6. Cross-cutting patterns
  • Water is the central axis of vulnerability (excess or scarcity).
  • Limited institutional capacities for monitoring, follow-up and implementation.
  • High dependence on strategic ecosystems (páramos, forests, watersheds).
  • Weak articulation between land-use planning and climate change.
  • Insufficient local and community climate information.

7. Factores

Deforestation, wildfires and agricultural expansion — Unplanned urbanization in risk zones

Deforestation, wildfires and agricultural expansion — Unplanned urbanization in risk zones — Insufficient or weak infrastructure (drainage, water systems, roads)

Unplanned urbanization in risk zones — Insufficient or weak infrastructure (drainage, water systems, roads) — Poverty and inequality gaps that increase vulnerability

Insufficient or weak infrastructure (drainage, water systems, roads) — Poverty and inequality gaps that increase vulnerability — Local economies dependent on climate (agriculture, fishing, tourism)

The risk and threat analysis showed that climate planning instruments must be directly connected to the reduction of these prioritized threats, integrating actions that strengthen community resilience, restore ecosystems, guarantee water and food security, stabilize the most exposed territories and reduce socio-environmental inequality. In fact, the coincidence between departments demonstrates that a set of national priorities can emerge from these realities.

  • The country faces a dual climate pressure related to hydrometeorological risks: water excess (floods, landslides) and water deficit (droughts).
  • The climate crisis in Colombia is multi-systemic and differentiated: Each department presents unique risks with highly differentiated territorial approaches, associated regionally as follows: - Caribbean: coastal erosion, droughts, extreme heat, floods - Amazonia/Orinoquía: heat waves, drought and wildfires, deforestation - Andean: mass movements, extreme rainfall, urban impacts - Coffee-Growing Region: landslides, intense rainfall and water stress
  • Climate risks intensify pre-existing social inequalities, as greater exposure of ethnic communities is revealed, vulnerability linked to multidimensional poverty, urban risks concentrated in informal expansion zones, which concentrate the impacts and have less response capacity.
  • The country's strategic ecosystems are under simultaneous stress: Forests, wetlands, páramos and coasts appear at risk.

Taken together, the collection of risks and threats constitutes a base contributed by the territories for the formulation of their planning instruments, showing that adaptation and mitigation cannot be conceived in isolation, but as transformative interventions that act on structural vulnerabilities and guarantee rights in a highly diverse, highly exposed and unequal country. This national conclusion synthesizes the territorial voice and guides the need for targets.

7.2. Analysis of Target Formulation

In a process as broad as the NDC update, where departments, productive sectors, ethnic communities, local organizations and national entities participate, it is natural that proposals arrive in many formats, styles and levels of detail. Each territory speaks from its experience and reality, and this makes the contributions valuable, but also diverse. In order to better understand them — and for them to genuinely serve decision-making at the national level — it is necessary to organize them.

Classifying the proposals helps convert that great diversity into a clear and manageable system. It allows identifying patterns, recurring themes, gaps that appear and shared priorities. Each territory formulates targets and measures from its own productive, geographic and sociocultural context, but the country needs to be able to see that panorama as a whole. Without categorization, all that information would remain fragmented and it would be very difficult to understand what the territories are asking for, what themes

Furthermore, this organization allows connecting what the territories say with the national and international commitments that Colombia must fulfill. The categories help align local proposals with the major chapters of NDC 3.0: mitigation, adaptation, loss and damage, means of implementation and climate justice. This way it is possible to see what each territory contributes, where there are coincidences, where ideas complement each other and what might need adjustments. Although each target retains its identity and its link with the department that formulated it, the categorization allows integrating it into a broader system that facilitates spatial analyses, heat maps, alignment matrices and other tools that help understand the general panorama.

Finally, classifying the proposals is not just a technical exercise. It is a way of making decisions with better information: it allows prioritizing investments, directing resources, designing programs and building policies that genuinely respond to what the territories are asking for. It also facilitates monitoring and transparency, because by organizing targets and measures by theme it is simpler to create indicators, roadmaps and monitoring systems.

The categories used to organize the targets formulated by the departments are presented in Table 3.

Five approaches are highlighted where department targets are concentrated. First, it is consistent across almost all departments the intention to raise the level of climate and environmental awareness through education, community training and awareness-raising processes, both in urban and rural contexts. The territories understand that climate change cannot be addressed solely through infrastructure or technology, but through sustained cultural transformations.

Second, climate governance, understood not only as regulatory or institutional, but as the capacity to make decisions from the territory, with the participation of local actors, ethnic authorities, municipal governments and community organizations. Many targets point to the need to update policies, incorporate environmental criteria in land-use plans and generate inter-institutional coordination bodies that allow coherence in climate actions.

In parallel, departments with strategic ecosystems — especially the Amazon, Pacific, Orinoquía and humid Caribbean — emphasize targets associated with forests and deforestation. The concern for the loss of forest cover, degradation of strategic ecosystems and the disorderly expansion of the agricultural frontier is evident in their proposals. These targets refer not only to control, but to community management of the forest, the promotion of legitimate economic incentives

Another important group of targets corresponds to adaptation to climate change, particularly in departments with high water vulnerability, flood risk, landslides, coastal erosion or droughts. Targets emerge that seek to improve the resilience of communities and productive systems, strengthen risk management, implement nature-based solutions, improve early warning systems and ensure water access.

And fifth, GHG mitigation, although not as predominant as education or governance, appears consistently in targets related to energy efficiency, clean energy, sustainable mobility, carbon neutrality and emission reductions in productive sectors. The lower frequency of these targets does not imply lower importance, but reflects that many territories consider that, to achieve these transformations, capacity gaps must first be resolved.

Finally, more localized but strategic groups of targets emerge, such as food security, marine-coastal ecosystems, climate justice, sustainable production and ecological restoration. These targets are an expression of specific territorial realities: the coastal vulnerability of the archipelago, agricultural pressure on Andean slopes, the need to restore caños, mangroves or tropical dry forests, or the urgency of integrating differential approaches for historically excluded populations.

Taken together, the targets project a country that conceives climate change as a cross-cutting phenomenon of social, economic and ecological life. Departments do not propose isolated targets, but structural transformations that seek to align territorial development and climate action. These are targets that reflect not only technical needs, but collective aspirations for change. Finally, all departments converge on a key element: without robust means of implementation, no target is viable.

From the social approach, targets that seek to protect vulnerable populations, guarantee fundamental rights and strengthen community capacities recur in most workshops. The territories insist that climate action must focus on people, with special emphasis on ethnic peoples, women, youth, elderly people and rural communities exposed to growing risks. This highlights the need for NDC 3.0 to incorporate differential approaches explicitly.

In sum, the most frequent targets at the national level show a country that recognizes both the magnitude of the climate challenge and the need to direct its action toward protecting ecosystems, water security, productive transformation, critical emission reductions and the strengthening of local governance and capacities. This territorial convergence constitutes a strategic input to define a more coherent, more territorialized NDC 3.0 aligned with real needs.

CONCLUSIONS OF THE PARTICIPATORY PATHWAY

The territorial participatory pathway for the update of the Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) 3.0 constitutes one of the most challenging and deliberative exercises carried out in the country for the NDC update. Its implementation allowed collecting inputs from institutional, community, ethnic, academic, productive, social and youth actors in 17 departments of the country. This process was developed under an informative, consultative and deliberative approach, without replacing mechanisms

Among the main limitations of the process the following are recognized: unequal participation between regions, differences in technical capacities between departments, absence of some regional nodes and logistical restrictions characteristic of territories with low connectivity.

The targets formulated by the territories reflect a national convergence around environmental education, climate governance, ecosystem protection, restoration, community and ecosystem-based adaptation, sustainable productive transition and emission mitigation. These targets constitute orienting territorial inputs that inform the technical process of constructing NDC 3.0, without constituting official commitments.

The analysis shows that national priorities were not imposed from the central level, but emerged from territorial voices. The centrality of environmental education, participatory governance, climate justice, bioeconomy, ecological restoration and forest protection — pillars of NDC 3.0 — directly coincides with the most frequent and robust proposals formulated by local actors. Likewise, the process allowed identifying that climate governance, governance

The participatory pathway as a participation mechanism through multi-actor territorial dialogues demonstrates that Colombia is advancing toward polycentric climate governance, where the national level articulates with the local. This process not only strengthens the legitimacy of NDC 3.0, but lays the groundwork for a climate transition model in which territories are protagonists of transformation and not mere executors.

The target proposals arising from this participatory pathway consolidate the most important message: Colombia can and must build its climate action from the territories, recognizing the ecological, cultural and social diversity of the country. NDC 3.0 is thus presented as a collective commitment, where science, ancestral knowledge, community experiences, institutional capacities and local aspirations are integrated into a common national vision: a just, resilient Colombia.

In this sense, the participatory pathway not only informs the NDC update; it becomes a methodological precedent for the country's future climate governance, demonstrating that broad, inclusive, transparent and traceable processes are not only possible, but essential to guarantee legitimate, effective and socially supported climate decisions. NDC 3.0 is thus the reflection of a country that recognizes that climate transformation is only viable when it is born from the territory.

4. Planning processes

  • Information on planning processes undertaken by the Party to prepare its nationally determined contribution and, if available, on the Party's implementation plans, including, as applicable: This element is developed in the subsequent sub-paragraphs: a(i) and a(ii). In accordance with section 2.2 of NDC 3.0, the institutional arrangements that support the implementation and monitoring of the contribution are grounded in the National Climate Change System (SISCLIMA), elevated to legal status by Law 1931 of 2018. This system articulates national coordination through the Inter-sectoral Commission on Climate Change (CICC), regional coordination through the Regional Climate Change Nodes (NRCC), and presidential coordination through the Inter-sectoral Cabinet Commission for Climate Action (CIGPAC). These bodies, together with the National Climate Change Policy, the Long-Term Climate Strategy E2050, and the Comprehensive Climate Change Management Plans (PIGCC), provide the foundation for climate governance in the country.

The NDC 3.0 update was, moreover, a broadly participatory, inclusive and representative process, deployed throughout the national territory between 2024 and 2025:

  • Territorial spaces: regional workshops were held within the framework of the Regional Climate Change Nodes (NRCC) and department-level deployments, with the participation of governorships, mayor's offices, environmental authorities, social organizations, academia, businesses, trade associations and local communities. Just Transition Dialogues were also held in regions highly dependent on fossil fuel economies, such as Valledupar and Barrancabermeja, which allowed integrating social and labor justice into the NDC.
  • Sectoral spaces: more than 30 workshops and technical working groups were organized with sectors such as energy, mining, transport, housing, industry, tourism, health, agriculture, education and finance. These spaces served to identify technically and economically viable measures, review progress on commitments from the previous NDC and align new targets with the capacities of each sector. The process strengthened sectoral co-responsibility and allowed consensus on specific actions in electric mobility, energy efficiency, waste management, transportation modernization, hydrocarbons, resilient agriculture, and water management.
  • Ethnic and differential approach spaces: workshops and meetings were held with indigenous peoples, Black, Afro-Colombian, Raizal and Palenquero communities, and the Peasantry, incorporating their visions of territory, biodiversity and collective rights. Specific spaces were also developed with women from rural communities and indigenous peoples, as well as with children, integrating differential and gender perspectives into NDC priorities. These contributions ensure that the update responded to the cultural and social diversity of the country, in line with the Escazú Agreement and the principles of climate justice and intergenerational equity.

Taken together, this territorial, sectoral and ethnic deployment transformed NDC 3.0 into a collective construction exercise that strengthens its social and political legitimacy, and ensures that climate targets reflect the realities and priorities of the country's different territories, sectors and communities.

a(ii) Contextual matters. This element is developed in the subsequent sub-paragraphs: a(ii)a, a(ii)b and a(ii)c, including, among others, as applicable.

According to section 2.1 of NDC 3.0, Colombia is a country highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change due to its geographic location, strategic biodiversity and levels of socioeconomic inequality. Recent impacts include phenomena such as flooding, drought, landslides and rising sea levels, which disproportionately affect rural, coastal and urban vulnerable communities. The economy maintains a high dependence on climate-sensitive sectors (agriculture, livestock, water resources and energy), which represents a structural challenge to achieve sustainable development and the eradication of poverty.

Colombia is also one of the countries with the greatest biological and cultural diversity in the world, which reinforces both its commitment to protecting ecosystems and the need to guarantee the participation of indigenous peoples, Black, Afro-Colombian, Raizal and Palenquero communities, the Peasantry and Local Communities in climate action. These national circumstances explain the orientation of the NDC toward a just, inclusive and resilient transition, aligned with the Long-Term Climate Strategy E2050 and with the Sustainable Development Goals agenda.

a(ii)b. Best practices and experiences related to the preparation of the nationally determined contribution;

Furthermore, the importance of having an inter-sectoral dialogue mechanism — the Inter-sectoral Commission on Climate Change (CICC) — for decision-making on climate change is highlighted, which has allowed reaching different levels of decision-making and technical advisory support in the entities responsible for climate action. The above, combined with the development of technical working groups with sectors, trade associations, experts and territories, allowed feeding back both the commitments at different scales of implementation and to identify enabling means and opportunities for action in the face of climate change. Finally, starting from a previously consolidated and well-received exercise, this facilitated dialogue with those responsible for mitigation, combined with the involvement of technical teams from different entities.

5. Assumptions and methodological approaches, including those used to estimate and to account for anthropogenic greenhouse gas emissions and, in their case, anthropogenic removals

NDC 3.0 Since the conception of the indicative NDC presented in 2015, the country has developed an accounting approach for its emissions based on the projection of a reference scenario and a scenario with mitigation measures, which allows it to establish a fixed-level target, which only varies in terms of increasing ambition. Both the iNDC, the NDC updated in 2020 and the present NDC 3.0 employed the same parameters and assumptions, as well as the projection of scenarios for the different

  • The methodological assumptions and approaches of Annex II of Decision 4/CMA.1 of BTR1 used to account for the anthropogenic emissions and removals of greenhouse gases corresponding to the nationally determined contribution. Colombia's NDC 3.0, presented in 2025 to the UNFCCC, was based on the most recent available information in terms of data and methodologies from the GHG Inventory communicated within the framework of the BTR1 (series up to 2021), which follows the IPCC Guidelines

It is worth highlighting that Colombia's NDC does not focus on identifying the secondary mitigation benefits derived from adaptation measures or economic diversification plans.

The accounting approach proposed for monitoring the national mitigation target of the NDC, through 'Indicator 1: Mitigation required for the target', takes as reference the emissions estimated in the last available year of the inventory

NDC 3.0 national GHG inventory most recent and compares it against the target established for a target year.

Consistent with BTR1, Indicator 2 monitors the deforested area in a year, in response to the emission reduction target from deforestation, which forms part of the national mitigation target. This monitoring is based on the information provided by the SMByC, which is governed by the methodological guidelines of the 'Protocol for digital image processing for the quantification of deforestation in Colombia. V 2.0 – IDEAM' (Galindo G., Espejo, Rubiano, Vergara and Cabre

The national GHG inventory is subject to quality controls and external reviews that independently verify the data, in line with IPCC guidelines, the Manual for national review of greenhouse gas inventories, and the joint EMEP/EEA 2019 Technical Guide for the development of air pollutant emissions inventories. This ensures that the reported information is unique in each category and avoids double counting. Colombia, through policies and instruments

In this way, the country has advanced in the establishment of a methodological framework for the monitoring of policies, actions and measures, not only through regulatory instruments, but also through the generation of accounting rules that allow compliance with the Enhanced Transparency Framework referred to in Article 13 of the Paris Agreement and ensure consistency in the establishment of baselines.

For the estimation of GHG reductions in policies, actions and measures, the country continues to strengthen its methodological framework with the purpose of conducting detailed monitoring of the results of each mitigation measure.

National-level emissions projections should not be used to evaluate progress in the implementation of the NDC, nor should they be used to monitor progress achieved in the implementation of measures, unless the country decides to do so and communicates it.

The implementation of the three mechanisms of Article 6 will be carried out based on national regulations determined by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development, to guarantee environmental integrity, implement strategies to maximize mitigation results, compliance with the NDC and achievement of the required sustainable and climate-resilient development.

NDC 3.0 is territorially differentiated, which includes: a human rights, gender and intergenerational approach. A peace with nature approach, which links climate action with the transformation of territories historically affected by armed conflict and environmental degradation. This implies that, in accordance with the national circumstances of the country, it is recognized that Colombia is a country highly vulnerable to climate change, despite having relatively low emissions in terms of global totals.

It is reaffirmed that climate action cannot be separated from the fight against social inequality, poverty, biodiversity loss and the need to consolidate peace. It also implies guaranteeing that climate commitments must be backed by clear means of implementation (finance, technology and capacities), without which ambition becomes rhetoric. In technical terms, NDC 3.0 has a commitment to accelerate the decarbonization of the electricity system through a reduction

This update process seeks to close implementation gaps; therefore, ambition is present as a basic aspect for the achievement of the nation's commitments.

6. How the Party considers that its nationally determined contribution is fair and ambitious in the light of its national circumstances

b) Equity considerations, including a reflection on equity;

The NDC incorporates an explicit equity reflection: (i) territorial participation, with the private sector and other stakeholders and inclusion 'leaving no one behind' (Indigenous Peoples, Black, Afro-Colombian, Raizal and Palenquero Communities, the Peasantry, Local Communities, children, adolescents, youth, women, formal and informal workers, trade unions/labor federations); (ii) social protection and decent work in transitioning sectors; (iii) respect for human rights, and gender and intergenerational approaches, as recognized in the Preamble of the Paris Agreement, and a peace approach adapted to the national conditions and circumstances of conflict in Colombia. Colombia shows progression and reflects the highest possible ambition (art. 4.3) by strengthening mitigation targets/scope compared to the previous NDC and socially enabling them through Just Transition (skills training and certification, productive reconversion and social dialogue). This social architecture minimizes distributive risks and reinforces the feasibility of meeting more ambitious targets over time.

c) How the Party has addressed Article 4, paragraph 3, of the Paris Agreement;

See equity reflection above. Colombia has shown progression and reflected the highest possible ambition (art. 4.3) by strengthening its mitigation target compared to the previous NDC, moving from a 20% reduction target in 2015 to 51% in 2020, and now to an absolute emissions ceiling for 2030 and 2035 aligned with its national carbon budget.

(skills training and certification, productive reconversion and social dialogue). This social architecture minimizes distributive risks and reinforces the feasibility of meeting more ambitious targets over time. Colombia has shown progression and reflected the highest possible ambition (art. 4.3) by strengthening its mitigation target compared to the previous NDC. It moved from a 20% reduction target in 2015 to 51% in 2020.

With respect to adaptation, this NDC seeks to go further by aligning national adaptation priorities with the dimensions of the Integrated Vulnerability Information System (SIIVRA) and with the thematic targets of the Global Adaptation Goal (GGA).

This update incorporates principles of just transition and cross-cutting approaches such as human rights, gender and peace with nature, which deepen the transformative character of climate action. This social architecture minimizes distributive risks and reinforces the feasibility of meeting more ambitious targets over time. In line with art. 4.4, Colombia, as a developing country, advances toward coverage targets of different economic sectors (public and private sectors) - (whole of economy approach), over time, strengthening sectoral consistency and accounting assumptions, while implementing Just Transition measures to ensure that the deepening of ambition occurs with decent work, social protection and participation. Furthermore, Colombia has presented emission reduction targets higher than its historical contribution to climate change, reaffirming an ambitious commitment despite being a highly vulnerable country with lower historical responsibility in global emissions. It has integrated mitigation with national agendas of sustainable development, biodiversity, water security and peace-building, showing coherence between climate action and development priorities.

7. How the nationally determined contribution contributes to the achievement of the objective of the Convention, as stated in its Article 2

To meet the long-term objective, Colombia in the climate action law (Law 2169 of 2021) in its Article 5 defined as a target 'achieving carbon neutrality by 2050'.

  • How the nationally determined contribution contributes to the achievement of the Convention objective, as stated in its Article 2; The NDC target for 2030 of reaching a maximum GHG emissions of 169.44 million tonnes of CO2 equivalent, and the NDC target for 2035 of emitting at most between 155 and 161 MtCO2eq, are aligned with the carbon neutrality trajectory for 2050. Therefore, the NDC implies a stabilization of emissions in this decade. The results of the national greenhouse gas inventory reported in the BTR1 show that net GHG emissions in 2021 were 280.1 MtCO2eq, while in 2017 they were 279.8 MtCO2eq. Although these emissions have not been reduced, they have not grown either, and although the dynamics in each subcategory are unique, in aggregate, current emissions could be expected to remain at the same level.
  • How the nationally determined contribution contributes to the implementation of Article 2, paragraph 1(a), and Article 4, paragraph 1, of the Paris Agreement. The NDC targets are consistent with the long-term objective of achieving carbon neutrality by 2050. current levels remain at the same level. In consideration of the above, Colombia's NDC 3.0 contributes to Article 4, paragraph 1 and Article 2 paragraph 1(a) of the Paris Agreement, as they aim at the stabilization of GHG emissions 'as soon as possible' and reduce them to achieve neutrality in 2050, as described therein.

Annex 3. Mitigation Measures

Introduction

This Annex consolidates the portfolio of mitigation measures that accompanies Colombia's Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC 3.0). This portfolio includes 39 sectoral mitigation measures, complemented by territorial measures and measures driven by the private sector. These measures constitute the operational instruments through which the sectors, territories and private sector contribute to the achievement of the national mitigation targets established for 2030 and 2035.

The portfolio integrates sectoral and territorial measures, as well as measures driven by the private sector, in accordance with their contributions to achieving emission reductions and the national decarbonization process. Each measure includes information on the responsible entity, sectors and actors involved, implementation horizon, support needs (climate finance, capacity strengthening, technology, governance and planning), and applicable cross-cutting approaches.

It is important to note that the measures included in this Annex do not represent additional legal commitments for the State, but rather guide and enable the sectoral and territorial implementation of NDC 3.0, consolidating an operational framework to accelerate climate action. Their construction is grounded in sectoral and territorial planning processes, inter-institutional dialogue, participation of non-state actors and technical review carried out during the formulation of NDC 3.0.

The portfolio has a dynamic character and may be updated as NDC implementation advances, financing instruments are strengthened, technical information availability increases and new capacities are developed in the territories. In this framework, territorial measures and those of private origin have been nested within the sector to which they contribute, in order to guarantee coherence, traceability and consistency with the climate planning architecture of SISCLIMA. The number of sectoral measures reported corresponds to the portfolio consolidated at the date of submission of NDC 3.0 and may be adjusted in future updates, without affecting the national mitigation targets.

Finally, some measures included serve an enabling role for other mitigation actions — for example, through technical guidelines, regulatory instruments, information systems and institutional strengthening. When these enabling measures are cross-cutting in nature and fulfill functions of climate finance, technology, capacities or transparency, they are incorporated in Annex 5 - Means of Implementation. However, this portfolio incorporates sectoral or specific enablers that, although they do not generate direct emission reductions, are indispensable for the effective execution of mitigation measures. This approach allows comprehensively reflecting the diversity of instruments that contribute to fulfilling NDC 3.0 and advancing toward carbon neutrality by 2050.

AGRICULTURAL SECTOR

Sectoral Measures - Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development

SUSTAINABLE CATTLE RANCHING NAMA — The measure seeks to transform cattle production toward a more efficient, low-emission model, combining productivity improvements with conservation and restoration actions on cattle farms. Its implementation focuses on promoting silvopastoral systems (intensive and non-intensive), sustainable management of pastures and proper management of cattle waste to reduce methane and harness its energy.

Likewise, the measure articulates environmental and productive land-use management practices at the regional level, including the release of areas for ecological restoration, the conservation of strategic ecosystems within farms and the optimization of logistical chains, especially in the marketing of raw milk.

Activities:

  • Sustainable intensification of cattle production through knowledge management and the establishment of intensive and non-intensive silvopastoral systems.
  • Restoration of natural areas within cattle farms for the conservation and/or restoration of natural ecosystems.
  • Manure management and utilization of methane gas generated by solid and liquid waste at cattle auctions and slaughterhouses in the country.

The number of farms to be intervened is 25,170 under the optimistic scenario, covering at least 3,628,959 ha with a 25.83% decrease in net emissions.

  • 900,000 cattle with improved diet.
  • 300,000 cattle with additives.
  • 590,000 ha under SSP (intensive and non-intensive).
  • 1,957 head with comprehensive manure management.
  • 68,675 ha restored. Mitigation potential: 12.2 MtCO₂eq

Observations: This value corresponds to the combined effect of the approaches included in the NAMA:

  • Improvement of cattle diet
  • Use of additives
  • Implementation of intensive and non-intensive silvopastoral systems
  • Comprehensive manure management

  • Training in ecological restoration and ecosystem conservation.
  • Training in technologies for manure management and biogas.

Cross-cutting approaches

In accordance with the Peace and Climate Action Methodological Matrix NDC 3.0, which integrates the five strategic Peace lines based on international metrics and aligned with the approaches of Just Transition, SDGs, Human Rights, Gender and Children, this measure corresponds to Alternative 2 – Implementation Enabling Mechanism, linked to the Peace lines: 4. Sustainable Development and Positive Peace, 3. Human Security and Community Resilience and 2. Human Rights and Social Empowerment.

  • Just Transition: Aligns with elements (b), (c) and (g) of the JTWP by promoting productive reconversion toward silvopastoral systems, strengthening rural capacities, improving the efficiency of the cattle ranching system and guaranteeing enabling conditions for the participation of small and medium producers, ensuring a low-carbon transition with social protection and ecosystem restoration.
  • Global SDGs: Integrates SDGs 2.4.1, 12.2.1, 13.2.2 and 15.3.1 by driving sustainable production, responsible soil management, agricultural emission reductions, ecological restoration and ecosystem conservation within cattle farms.
  • Human Rights: Guarantees better working conditions in the cattle sector, ensuring access to training, occupational safety and dignified conditions for rural workers. Furthermore, by reducing environmental degradation and promoting silvopastoral systems, it contributes to the constitutionally recognized right to a healthy environment and strengthens food security in vulnerable communities.
  • Gender: Promote the participation of women in the implementation of silvopastoral systems, living fences and pasture management, through training programs and access to green credits designed for women rural producers. This reduces historical gaps in access to land, technology and financing, and fosters female leadership in sustainable value chains, ensuring that women are direct beneficiaries of incentives and payment for environmental services mechanisms.

Analytical basis: The Sustainable Cattle Ranching NAMA drives a structural productive reconversion of the cattle sector through silvopastoral systems, restoration within farms, pasture efficiency and manure management, significantly reducing emissions and increasing removals. It strengthens livelihoods, generates rural employment, protects strategic ecosystems and contributes to food security. By articulating national and territorial actors, integrating green financing and improving rural technical capacities, the measure contributes to positive peace, community resilience and climate governance in areas with high dependence on cattle ranching.

GHG EMISSION REDUCTIONS IN RICE PRODUCTION THROUGH THE MASS ADOPTION OF TECHNOLOGY (AMTEC 2.0) IN COLOMBIA. The GHGs involved in the rice production system are methane gas (CH4), nitrous oxide (N2O) and carbon dioxide (CO2), whose concentrations at the national level tend to increase year by year, thereby contributing to deepening the problem of warming of the climate system. The AMTEC method is a technology transfer model based on the sustainability and social responsibility promoted by the organization, competitiveness and profitability of the producer, implementing integral massive technologies to increase yields and reduce production costs in rice cultivation. It includes weather forecasting - crop modeling, precision agriculture and the MIRI irrigation system.

Activities:

  • Volumetric water consumption management: Reduce the depth of the water layer in paddy fields with AWD (ALTERNATE WET-DRY): Through the alternate wet-drying irrigation technique (Alternate Wetting and Drying - AWD), the water layer in the paddy field is reduced, decreasing CH4 production.
  • Reduction in fertilizer use: By implementing a lower planting density, the use of nitrogen fertilizers per unit area is reduced, decreasing N2O emissions.
  • Harvest residue management: Decomposition of residues instead of physical burning including minimum tillage and utilization of straw (Less organic matter in water for CH4 production, increase of soil carbon).

2030

2035

2030

Approximately 108,976 ha of rice crops under AMTEC 2.0 technology between 2026 and 2030.

Mitigation potential: ≈0.08 MtCO₂eq

Observations: Mitigation potential:

2030

  • Escenario referencia: 0,61 MtCO₂eq
  • Escenario medida (AMTEC 2.0): 0,53 MtCO₂eq
  • Annual differential: ≈0.08 MtCO₂eq (emission reduction)

2035

  • Referencia: 0,65 MtCO₂eq
  • Medida: 0,56 MtCO₂eq
  • Diferencial anual: ≈0,09 MtCO₂eq

Cross-cutting approaches In accordance with the Peace and Climate Action Methodological Matrix NDC 3.0, which integrates the five strategic Peace lines based on international metrics and aligned with the approaches of Just Transition, SDGs, Human Rights, Gender and Children, this measure corresponds to Alternative 2 – Implementation Enabling Mechanism, linked to the Peace lines: 4. Sustainable Development and Positive Peace, 3. Human Security and Community Resilience and 1. Governance and Transparency.

  • Just Transition: Contributes to elements (b), (d) and (f) of the JTWP by driving the technological reconversion of the rice sector toward low-carbon practices (AWD, precision agriculture, reduction of fertilizers, residue management), accompanied by technical assistance, access to financing and organizational strengthening for small and medium producers. Ensures that productivity increases without generating territorial or socioeconomic inequalities.
  • Global SDGs: Contributes to SDGs 2.4.1, 6.4.1, 12.4.1, 13.2.2 and 15.3.1 by reducing CH₄ and N₂O emissions through efficient water management, decreasing nitrogen fertilization, improving soil health through no-burning of straw, promoting precision agriculture and strengthening climate resilience in rice-growing areas.
  • Human Rights: Guarantees the right to water and a healthy environment by decreasing water consumption, reducing contamination associated with residue burning and sustainable agricultural practices. Strengthens dignified working conditions, access to technical assistance and community appropriation of agricultural knowledge under the do-no-harm principle, guaranteeing equitable access to technologies and water.
  • Gender: Promote inclusion of women in training and access to credits. In accordance with the gender commitments of the agricultural sector in the PAGCC-CO. And in accordance with strategic lines 5.4 and 5.5 on gender and measure 4.2 to integrate the gender approach in sectoral climate change management. All this in coordination with the Rural Women's Directorate of the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development.
  • Children: The measure is articulated with MISNI indicators DI5, DI10, DI13, DI9, JE2, JE6 and JE9. The reduction of burning, proper water management and reduction of fertilizers protects the health and environment where girls, boys and adolescents live (DI5, DI13). The economic strengthening of small producers improves the standard of living of rural households (DI10). Technical training and territorial governance (DI9, JE2, JE6, JE9).

Analytical basis: AMTEC 2.0 reduces emissions through efficient water management, reduction of fertilizers, precision agriculture and elimination of burning, generating productivity increases and climate resilience. Its territorial and technological character strengthens local governance, improves rural livelihoods and contributes to positive peace and ecological sustainability in rice-growing areas of the country.

  • Human Rights: Ensures equitable access to clean technologies, water and productive resources; protects the right to health and a healthy environment, given the reduction of burning, efficient use of inputs and wastewater treatment. Furthermore, it strengthens dignified and safe working conditions for rural producers under the do-no-harm principle.

This reduces emissions, improves environmental health and strengthens rural livelihoods, enhancing community resilience and social cohesion in panela-producing territories highly vulnerable to climate change. It contributes to human security, sustainable productive development and positive peace by increasing the adaptive capacity of producers.

MEASURE NAME

ACTIONS TO INCREASE GHG ABSORPTIONS THROUGH THE PROMOTION OF AGRO-ENERGY CROPS AND BIOMASS UTILIZATION FOR THE PRODUCTION OF BIOFUELS AND BIOENERGY

Activities:

  • Development of technical and regulatory guidelines for agro-energy crops and biomass use.
  • Training and technical assistance programs for agricultural producers on bioenergy and biofuels.
  • Promotion of incentives and financial mechanisms for the adoption of sustainable practices.
  • Articulation with public and private entities to facilitate bioenergy value chains.
  • Monitoring and reporting of established areas and biomass utilized.

The measure will contribute to the net reduction of GHG emissions through two complementary mechanisms:

  • Carbon capture in plant biomass through the establishment of agro-energy crops in suitable areas, avoiding the conversion of natural ecosystems.
  • Partial substitution of fossil fuels with biofuels and bioenergy derived from the use of residual biomass, reducing emissions associated with transport and agro-industrial processes.

The scope is limited to promotion, training, technical guidelines and inter-institutional articulation actions, leaving installation and financing to other competent portfolios.

2030

2035

2030

Estimated reduction of 3% to 5% of agricultural sector emissions, equivalent to 1.5 to 2.5 million tonnes of CO₂eq, considering initial adoption in suitable areas.

2035

Estimated reduction of 6% to 8%, equivalent to 3.5 to 4.5 million tonnes of CO₂eq, with greater coverage and consolidation of bioenergy chains.

SUPPORT NEEDS

Financing / Technology

Capacities / Planning

1. Climate finance:

  • Green incentives and access to international funds for studies and technical assistance.
  • Carbon capture and emission reduction payment schemes.

3. Technology:

  • Technology transfer for biofuel and bioenergy production.
  • Digital monitoring systems for biomass and emissions.

2. Capacities:

  • Technical training in management of agro-energy crops and bioenergy technologies.
  • Training in MRV (Measurement, Reporting and Verification).

4. Planning, governance and others:

  • Inclusion in sectoral mitigation and bioeconomy plans.
  • Road map for agro-energy crops and bioenergy.
  • Articulación interinstitucional (Agricultura, Ambiente, Energía).
  • Standards and norms for sustainability and safety in bioenergy.

Cross-cutting approaches

In accordance with the Peace and Climate Action Methodological Matrix NDC 3.0, which integrates the five strategic Peace lines and the approaches of Just Transition, SDGs, Human Rights, Gender and Children, this measure corresponds to Alternative 2 – Implementation Enabling Mechanism, linked to the Peace lines: 2. Human Rights and Social Empowerment, 3. Human Security and Community Resilience and 4. Sustainable Development and Positive Peace. Based on these lines, the measure aligns with:

Just Transition: Contributes to element (a) by promoting structural changes toward bioenergy and low-emission agro-energy crops; to element (d) by strengthening technical and technological capacities in bioenergy; and to element (f) by fostering community participation and rural training in new value chains. Global SDGs: Aligns with SDGs 2.4.1, 7.2.1, 12.4.1, 12.5.1, 13.2.2 and 15.3.1, by driving sustainable production, clean energy, waste reduction, and climate coherence.

Analytical basis: The measure strengthens the transition toward agro-energy systems and bioenergy chains that capture carbon, substitute fossil fuels and reduce emissions from inadequate biomass management. It contributes to productive resilience, generates new rural economic opportunities and improves environmental sustainability, supporting human security, territorial well-being and positive peace.

MEASURE NAME

ACTIONS TO REDUCE GHG EMISSIONS IN AGRO-INDUSTRY, PROMOTING THE IMPLEMENTATION OF BIOGAS CAPTURE AND USE FROM BIOMASS DERIVED FROM RESIDUAL BIOMASS FROM AGRO-INDUSTRIAL PROCESSES

The measure consists of promoting the adoption of systems for the capture and use of biogas generated from residual biomass of agro-industrial processes, in order to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions in the sector. This action is oriented toward incentivizing the efficient use of organic waste as a source of renewable energy, reducing methane and carbon dioxide emissions, and contributing to the partial substitution of fossil fuels in agro-industrial operations.

Activities:

  • Development of technical and regulatory guidelines for the use of residual biomass in agro-industry.
  • Development of training and technical assistance programs for agro-industrial companies on biogas capture and use.
  • Articulation with other portfolios and entities to facilitate the implementation of biogas projects.
  • Monitoring and reporting of progress in the adoption of biogas systems in the agro-industrial sector.

This measure seeks to reduce GHG emissions in agro-industry, primarily methane, through energy recovery of organic waste generated in productive processes. It is oriented toward incentivizing the adoption of biogas technologies as a renewable source to substitute fossil fuels in agro-industrial operations, improve waste management and promote the circular economy. The scope is limited to promotion, training, technical guidelines and inter-institutional articulation actions.

2030

2035

2030

Estimated reduction of 2% to 3% of agro-industrial subsector emissions, equivalent to 0.8 to 1.2 million tonnes of CO₂eq, considering initial adoption in large processing plants.

2035

Estimated reduction of 4% to 6%, equivalent to 1.8 to 2.5 million tonnes of CO₂eq, with greater coverage in medium and large agro-industries.

SUPPORT NEEDS

Financing / Technology

Capacities / Planning

1. Climate finance:

  • Creation of green financial mechanisms (soft credits, tax incentives) for biogas projects in agro-industry.
  • Access to international climate funds (GCF, GEF) for studies, technical assistance and institutional strengthening.
  • Design of emission reduction payment schemes (voluntary and regulated carbon markets).

3. Technology:

  • Transfer of efficient and adapted technologies for biogas capture and use.
  • Implementation of digital monitoring systems to control emissions and biogas production.
  • Access to modular equipment and solutions for small and medium agro-industries.

2. Capacities:

  • Technical training programs for agro-industrial operators and managers in biogas capture and use.
  • Training in emission assessment and MRV (Measurement, Reporting and Verification).
  • Development of competencies in circular economy and bioenergy for the agro-industrial sector.

4. Planning, governance and others:

  • Strengthening of inter-institutional articulation between ministries (Agriculture, Environment, Energy).
  • Creation of public-private technical roundtables for biogas and bioenergy.
  • Establishment of standards and norms for the safe and efficient use of biogas.

Cross-cutting approaches

In accordance with the Peace and Climate Action Methodological Matrix NDC 3.0, which integrates the five strategic Peace lines based on international metrics and the approaches of Just Transition, SDGs, Human Rights, Gender and Children, this measure corresponds to Alternative 2 – Transition Enabling Mechanism, linked to the Peace lines: 2. Human Rights and Social Empowerment, 3. Human Security and Community Resilience and 4. Sustainable Development and Positive Peace. Based on these lines:

  • Just Transition: Aligns with elements (a) of reconversion toward clean energy in productive processes, (d) strengthening technical capacities in bioenergy, and (f) promoting participation of the agro-industrial sector in low-carbon technological changes.
  • Global SDGs: Contributes to SDGs 7.2.1, 9.4.1, 12.4.1, 12.5.1 and 13.2.2, by driving energy efficiency, waste reduction, circular economy and climate coherence in agro-industrial systems through biogas capture and use.
  • Human Rights: Respect and promotion of the right to a healthy environment and informed community participation in decision-making. It is ensured that promotion actions do not violate labor rights nor affect equitable access to resources, promoting transparency and consultation throughout the process.
  • Gender: Active inclusion of women in training processes, technical assistance and opportunities derived from bioenergy, ensuring equality in access to benefits and incentives. Indicators are incorporated to monitor female participation in technology adoption. In accordance with the gender commitments of the agricultural sector in the PAGCC-CO and with the differential approach of the PIGCCS of the agricultural sector, and in accordance with strategic lines 5.4 and 5.5 and measure 4.2 for sectoral gender.
  • Children: Contributes to MISNI indicators DI13, DI9, DI12, JE2, JE6 and JE9, by reducing local emissions and improving environmental quality for girls, boys and adolescents; promoting access to information and technical training for rural youth; and generating participation and empowerment opportunities in energy innovation, strengthening intergenerational equity.

Analytical basis: The measure drives bioenergy chains in agro-industry through biogas capture and use, reducing methane emissions and substituting fossil fuels. This strengthens productive efficiency, environmental sustainability and circular economy, while improving community well-being and territorial resilience, contributing to positive peace and sustainable productive transition.

REDUCTION OF AGRICULTURAL BURNING

For the foregoing reasons and in order to contribute to the national target for mitigation of Black Carbon emissions, the reduction of agricultural burning was adopted as a measure, since this activity represents a critical source of black carbon, classified as a short-lived climate pollutant.

2030

2035

2030

In compliance with this measure and according to the LEAP model, an emission mitigation potential for 2030 of 0.45 kt CN is estimated, with respect to the NDC 3.0 reference scenario.

Notas aclaratorias:

The reference scenario used by the model takes as its basis the information reported in BTR1 for burning only in sugarcane crops.

The model assumes, to estimate the mitigation potential, a 40% reduction in agricultural burning in sugarcane crops.

2035

For this measure, in the period 2031 to 2035, a mitigation potential of 0.5 kt CN is estimated with respect to the NDC 3.0 reference scenario.

Notas aclaratorias:

The reference scenario used by the model takes as its basis the information reported in the BTR1 for burning only in sugarcane crops.

The model assumes, to estimate the mitigation potential, a 40% reduction in agricultural burning in sugarcane crops.

SUPPORT NEEDS

Financing / Technology

Capacities / Planning

1. Climate finance:

  • Development of a monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV) mechanism for Black Carbon measures.
  • Inter-institutional management of technical and regulatory enablers, which allow strengthening the capacities of different sectors.
  • Research and Development: Invest in the adaptation of existing technologies to local conditions.

3. Technology:

  • Technology Transfer: Promote the adoption of alternative technologies to the use of fire.
  • Technical Assistance and Agricultural Extension: Strengthen rural extension programs and training for farmers in sustainable soil management techniques.

2. Capacities:

  • Effective adoption of alternatives that not only avoid burning, but improve soil health and productivity.
  • Remote Monitoring (MRV) Capacities.
  • Strengthening of baselines and monitoring of agricultural crops that practice burning.

4. Planning, governance and others:

  • Regulatory Framework: Update or create regulations that restrict or prohibit open-air burning, while establishing support mechanisms for the transition.
  • Inter-sectoral Coordination: Establish effective inter-institutional articulation mechanisms to establish policies, resources and financial instruments aligned with the NDC objective.

Cross-cutting approaches

In accordance with the Peace and Climate Action Methodological Matrix NDC 3.0, which integrates the five strategic Peace lines based on international metrics and aligned with the cross-cutting approaches of Just Transition, SDGs, Human Rights, Gender and Children, Alternative 2 – Implementation Enabling Mechanism is developed for this measure, linked to the Peace lines 1. Governance and Transparency and 4. Sustainable Development and Positive Peace. Based on these lines, the measure aligns with:

  • Just Transition: Element (b) of the UNFCCC Just Transition Work Programme, oriented toward equitable transitions with socioeconomic and labor dimensions, promoting sustainable livelihoods for farmers and reducing negative impacts associated with the progressive phase-out of fire use.
  • Global SDGs: 2.4.1, 12.4.1, 13.2.2 and 15.3.1, linked to sustainable agriculture, environmentally sound management, climate coherence in national policies and reduction of land degradation.
  • Human Rights: The reduction of black carbon and particulate matter emissions is directly linked to the protection of the right to health and a clean, healthy and sustainable environment; it also protects sustainable food security and the right to food. At the same time, reducing exposure to carbon promotes the principles of equity and non-discrimination, particularly under the protection of rights of Indigenous Peoples, Black, Afro-Colombian, Raizal and Palenquero Communities, and others.
  • Gender: Rural women play a crucial role in agriculture and are often the most affected by environmental degradation, so the measure improves the conditions of this group. In accordance with the differential approach of the PIGCCS of the agricultural sector, which establishes that climate change management takes into account that vulnerability reduction must be conceived from a gender perspective and in an intersectional manner, recognizing the need to advance in gender equity.
  • Children: The measure aligns with the following MISNI indicators: IM1, DI5, DI10, DI13, JE5 and JE6. By reducing and progressively eliminating agricultural burning, it decreases the exposure of girls, boys and adolescents to smoke and particulate matter, improving air quality and protecting their health in rural territories (IM1, DI5).

Analytical basis: This measure contributes to the reduction of short-lived climate pollutants and promotes sustainable agricultural practices that strengthen productive resilience, public health and the sustainability of rural livelihoods. Its implementation strengthens territorial governance, drives clean technologies, reduces socioeconomic vulnerabilities and promotes positive peace through the transition toward safer and more sustainable agro-productive systems.

In accordance with the Peace and Climate Action Methodological Matrix NDC 3.0, which integrates the five strategic Peace lines based on international metrics and aligned with the approaches of Just Transition, SDGs, Human Rights, Gender and Children, Alternative 2 – Implementation Enabling Mechanism is developed for this measure, linked to the Peace lines 4. It contributes to positive peace by promoting green jobs, technological modernization, reduction of air pollution and generation of healthier work and urban environments, in coherence with the country's sustainable re-industrialization.

  • Transfer and adaptation of low-Clinker technologies.
  • Certification of cement and concrete products with a lower carbon footprint.
  • Development of feasibility studies for the use of hydrogen and for carbon capture, storage and use in national territory.

2. Capacities:

  • Technical training in the operation and maintenance of new energy efficiency technologies and alternative cement manufacturing processes.
  • Generation of knowledge and feasibility studies for the scaling of cleaner technologies.

4. Planning, governance and others:

  • Inter-institutional management of technical and regulatory enablers.
  • Updating of regulatory frameworks identified as necessary for the development of the road map (Waste Tariff Framework, development of the carbon market and border adjustment mechanisms, updating of air emission standards).
  • Development of regulations for the implementation of new technologies such as carbon capture, storage and use.

MINES AND ENERGY SECTOR

Sectoral Measures – Ministry of Mines and Energy

COAL SUBSTITUTION AND FUGITIVE EMISSIONS REDUCTION

Cross-cutting approaches:

  • Just Transition: Support for workers affected by the energy transition, guaranteeing dignified and orderly labor pathways.
  • Global SDGs 7.2.1, 7.3.1, 9.4.1 and 13.2.2, which promote the increase of clean energy, energy efficiency, modernization of industrial processes to reduce emissions and the integration of climate action in regulatory and energy planning frameworks.
  • Human Rights: Primarily protects the right to a healthy and clean environment by reducing emissions that affect the respiratory health of communities near mines and plants. It also protects the right to decent work and social security. It is framed in the pillars of protection and respect for the rights of Indigenous Peoples and local communities.
  • Gender: Guide for the integration of the Gender Approach in Projects, Programs, Plans and Policies for climate change management. Mining-energy sector. National Development Plan 2018-2022 'Pact for Colombia, Pact for Equity'. Colombia's Gender and Climate Change Action Plan – Actions for gender equality, sustainability and climate justice. In accordance with the gender commitments of the Ministry of Mines and Energy in Colombia's Gender and Climate Change Action Plan.
  • Children: The measure aligns with the following MISNI indicators: DI1, DI5, DI6, DI10, JE3 and JE6, as it reduces pollutants and associated risks, protecting life, health and the comprehensive development of girls, boys and adolescents in mining-energy and urban territories (DI1, DI5, DI10).

Analytical basis: The measure contributes to a just energy transition through the progressive substitution of coal, institutional strengthening for the management of fugitive emissions and clean electrification. It contributes to positive peace by promoting an orderly economic reconversion, protecting communities dependent on coal, improving environmental quality and advancing toward a sustainable energy model based on rights, equity and informed participation.

Cross-cutting approaches

In accordance with the Peace and Climate Action Methodological Matrix NDC 3.0, which integrates the five strategic Peace lines based on international metrics and aligned with the approaches of Just Transition, SDGs, Human Rights, Gender and Children, Alternative 2 – Implementation Enabling Mechanism is developed for this measure, linked to the Peace lines 1. Governance and Transparency and 4. Sustainable Development and Positive Peace. Based on these lines, the measure aligns with:

  • Just Transition: elements (b) and (f) of the UNFCCC Just Transition Work Programme: (b) by progressively substituting fuels that generate health and well-being inequalities in vulnerable rural populations, and (f) by requiring inclusive processes for community adoption of clean energy sources in rural contexts.
  • Global SDGs 3.9.1, 7.1.2 and 13.2.2, directly related to access to clean cooking energy, reduction of indoor air pollution and climate coherence in energy policies.
  • Human Rights: The reduction of exposure to toxic smoke favors the everyday conditions that guarantee the right to health, dignified life and economic rights, as less effort is required for firewood collection. Participation and dialogue with communities must be guaranteed to ensure that no negative impacts are generated.
  • Gender: The actions of the PNSL help women to have opportunities for appropriation of time through new home energy alternatives, which impacts their well-being and quality of life as the gender most involved in cooking. This translates into greater attention to local economic development, both in the personal development of women and the elderly, in accordance with the gender commitments of the Ministry of Mines and Energy in the Gender and Climate Change Action Plan of Colombia, the gender guidelines of the mining-energy sector and the implementation of the PIGCCME to 2050 of the Mines and Energy sector.
  • Children: The measure is articulated with the following MISNI indicators: DI13, JE6 and JE9. By progressively substituting highly polluting fuels for cooking food in rural households, it directly reduces indoor pollution and improves air quality, guaranteeing girls, boys and adolescents a healthier and safer environment in their daily lives.

Analytical basis: The measure strengthens territorial energy governance through the progressive substitution of polluting fuels, reduces historical inequalities in health and care time, drives the economic sustainability of rural households, generates intergenerational benefits and contributes to positive peace through real improvements in well-being, quality of life and environmental co-responsibility.

Private Sector Measures

Emission reductions - ECOPETROL /

Ecopetrol committed in 2019 to reduce its emissions by 20% by 2030, aligned with the national target defined in 2015. The company expects to increase its ambition to contribute to addressing the effects of climate change.

For this purpose, the company established a Decarbonization Plan based on 4 lines of action: (i) continuous update of the GHG emissions inventory, (ii) implementation of emission reduction projects, (iii) compensation of emissions through nature-based solutions, and (iv) definition and implementation of a road map toward carbon-neutrality. Among these projects are:

  • Gas Flaring Utilization Project, to generate electrical energy for the Casabe Field.
  • First photovoltaic solar generation umbrella of Ecopetrol, covering part of the projects included in the company's proposed target of generating 400 MW of renewable energy for self-consumption by 2023 – Ecopetrol. Responsible entities: Ecopetrol S.A. Sectoral ally: Ministry of Mines and Energy.

Methane emission reductions in operations.

These reduction actions are aligned with the objectives established in the Sectoral Comprehensive Climate Change Management Plan (PIGCCS) of the Mining and Energy sector, specifically regarding methane emission reductions as part of the sectoral targets. Additionally, the initiatives are articulated with compliance with Resolution 40066 of 2022 and Resolution 40537 of 2024.

Photovoltaic solar energy sales contracts for large customers.

For the fulfillment of this commitment, the following milestones are proposed with activities related to the identification of potential customers for the solar energy offering, structuring of the proposal based on the customer's specificities, negotiation of the energy sales contract, and installation of the photovoltaic solar generation system.

The goal is to promote self-generation of energy through alternative sources to reach an installed capacity in photovoltaic solar generation systems for large customers, with projection to 2030.

TRANSPORT SECTOR

Sectoral Measures – Ministry of Transport

ACTIVE MOBILITY — Comprehensive implementation of the National Active Mobility Strategy with a gender and differential approach through the seven lines of action that promote active mobility in Colombian cities, and that increase the modal share of active modes (walking and cycling) by 5.5 percentage points. These actions will be developed in coordination with the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development, the Ministry of Housing, City and Territory.

Activities:

  • Organize events, meetings and gatherings aimed at the articulation, planning and implementation of ENMA measures, complemented by the implementation of the Territorial Strengthening Cycle as a governance strategy to consolidate institutional and territorial capacities. In a coordinated manner, the creation of an inter-sectoral active mobility coordination body and the update of the ENMA implementation plan are planned, strengthening governance mechanisms.
  • Implementation and monitoring of Information Management, Monitoring and Tracking of the ENMA comprises the collection, systematization and analysis of information associated with indicators, as well as the monitoring of the strategy's actions at both the national and territorial level. Complementarily, the structuring of the resource management system for the creation of SIMA is contemplated, and its subsequent commissioning, which includes the definition of the reporting flow, verification processes and the structuring of open data for information download.
  • Implementation and monitoring of the Technical Guidelines for Infrastructure, Mobility Management and Territorial Planning within the framework of the ENMA comprises the socialization of guides and guidelines oriented toward the development of active mobility projects, as well as the technical, legal and financial structuring of these initiatives, which include infrastructure components, shared mobility systems, promotion and demand management strategies. In a coordinated manner, the implementation of active mobility projects and the monitoring and evaluation of the impacts generated are envisaged, with the aim of measuring their scope, effectiveness and contribution to the strengthening of active mobility in Colombia.

Actions to increase the modal share of active transport by 5.5 percentage points through the comprehensive implementation of the National Active Mobility Strategy. These actions will be developed in coordination with the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development, the Ministry of Housing, City and Territory, the National Planning Department and local governments.

Cross-cutting approaches

In accordance with the Peace and Climate Action Methodological Matrix NDC 3.0, which integrates the five strategic Peace lines based on international metrics and aligned with the approaches of Just Transition, SDGs, Human Rights, Gender and Children, Alternative – Implementation Enabling Mechanism is developed for this measure linked to the Peace lines 4. Sustainable Development and Positive Peace and 5. Innovation and Cohesion for Capacity Building, given that active mobility depends on inclusive territorial planning, cultural changes, safe infrastructure and technical capacities to transform urban mobility. From these lines, the measure aligns with:

  • Just Transition: Element (c) of the JTWP by promoting equitable access to sustainable mobility systems, and element (f) by fostering participatory governance and territorial planning based on differentiated needs.
  • Global SDGs: 3.4.1, 9.1.1, 11.2.1, 13.2.2, oriented toward public health, sustainable infrastructure, safe mobility and climate coherence in urban policies.
  • Human Rights: Guarantees access to safe, clean and healthy mobility, reducing exposure to pollution, accidents and inequalities in the use of public space. It also favors the right to health, a healthy environment and equity.
  • Gender: Include the gender approach in the active mobility indicators set out in the ENMA, such as the percentages of women's trips in active modes, which serves to direct policies, programs and projects that promote in a sectored manner a safer active mobility, in accordance with the commitment of the transport sector set out in Colombia's Gender and Climate Change Action Plan (PAGCC-CO).
  • Children: The measure is articulated with the following MISNI indicators: DI5, DI10, DI13, JE6 and JE9. By increasing walking and cycling through safe infrastructure and inclusive territorial planning, it reduces congestion, emissions and road accidents, improving air quality, health and safe access for girls, boys and adolescents to essential services.

Analytical basis:

Active mobility reduces emissions, congestion and accidents, strengthening public health and territorial equity. It enables safe and sustainable access to essential services, drives inclusive urban transformation, fosters social cohesion and generates direct benefits for women, children and vulnerable populations. By being structured from territorial planning with a gender and differential approach, it consolidates institutional capacities and contributes to positive peace through more connected, healthy and safe cities.

FUND FOR THE PROMOTION OF TECHNOLOGICAL UPGRADE – FOPAT

Institutional and operational strengthening for the effective implementation of the Fund for Technological Upgrade of Transport (FOPAT). This measure contemplates the strengthening of the technical, administrative and operational capacities of the national and territorial entities involved in its execution. It includes the design and implementation of communication strategies aimed at beneficiaries and local authorities.

The following are the FOPAT sub-accounts:

- Individual public passenger transport – Taxis – Strategic, comprehensive and mass transit systems – Light cargo vehicles and dump trucks – Modernization of heavy cargo vehicles.

Activities:

Carry out the socialization and dissemination of FOPAT through the preparation of the socialization plan, the call to potential beneficiaries and the development of socialization sessions, directly contacting the identified actors to guarantee their understanding, participation and access to the Fund's benefits.

Carry out the operationalization of FOPAT in each of its sub-accounts through working sessions with the involved actors (manufacturers, suppliers, beneficiaries, trade associations, international cooperation, financial institutions and entities); the development of operational and technical guidelines that allow the issuance of the required administrative acts; and the generation of such administrative acts to enable the operation of each sub-account. Additionally,

Finally, coordination with territorial entities will be advanced to develop the guidelines for promoting zero- and low-emission vehicles within the framework of the Fund.

Governance and Transparency and 4. Sustainable Development and Positive Peace, given that rail rehabilitation requires institutional coordination

  • Global SDGs: 9.1.1, 9.4.1, 11.2.1, 13.2.2, oriented toward resilient infrastructure, productive modernization, sustainable transport and climate coherence in sectoral planning.
  • Human Rights: Reduces emissions, accidents and operational risks, protecting the right to a healthy environment, health and safe mobility of communities adjacent to the corridor. At the same time, it generates safe and decent working conditions. For its implementation, the participation and protection of rights of Indigenous Peoples, Black, Afro-Colombian, Raizal and Palenquero Communities, and peasant communities is promoted.
  • Gender: Improve the technical, operational, commercial viability and environmental and social sustainability conditions taking into account the gender analysis of labor roles, capacity building and the inclusion of women in their diversity, in accordance with the commitment of the transport sector set out in Colombia's Gender and Climate Change Action Plan (PAGCC-CO).
  • Children: The measure is articulated with the following MISNI indicators: DI13, DI10 and JE6. By rehabilitating the La Dorada, Chiriguaná, Santa Marta railway corridor and shifting cargo from road mode to a more efficient mode, it reduces emissions, noise and risks associated with fossil-fuel-intensive transport, improving the environment where girls, boys and adolescents live and move.

Analytical basis: The rehabilitation of the railway corridor reduces emissions by shifting road transport toward a more efficient mode, decreases road risks and improves air quality. It strengthens logistical competitiveness and territorial cohesion, drives green employment and contributes to positive peace through sustainable intermodal connectivity. Its structural character favors climate governance, long-term planning and economic development in corridors with vulnerable and riverside populations.

Cross-cutting approaches

In accordance with the Peace and Climate Action Methodological Matrix NDC 3.0, which integrates the five strategic Peace lines based on international metrics and aligned with the approaches of Just Transition, SDGs, Human Rights, Gender and Children, Alternative 2 – Implementation Enabling Mechanism is developed for this measure, linked especially to the Peace lines 1. Governance and Transparency and 4. Sustainable Development and Positive Peace. Based on these lines, the measure aligns with:

  • Just Transition: Element (c) of the JTWP, by promoting operational efficiency and technological modernization that reduces economic and environmental barriers in air transport, and element (f) for its orientation toward participatory processes to coordinate airlines, authorities and government.
  • Global SDGs: 9.4.1 (technological modernization with greater efficiency), 13.2.2 (climate integration in transport planning), 11.6.2 (reduction of urban atmospheric pollution associated with air routes), 7.3.1 (improved energy efficiency).
  • Human Rights: The reduction of GHG emissions and pollutants associated with air operations improves atmospheric conditions in areas near airports, guaranteeing the right to health and a healthy environment.
  • Gender: Foster the necessary conditions for taking advantage of RNAV (Area Navigation) and RNP (Required Navigation Performance) capacity, taking into account the needs, roles, skills and interests of all populations, including pathways for capacity building for women in their diversity, in accordance with the commitment of the transport sector set out in Colombia's Gender and Climate Change Action Plan (PAGCC-CO).
  • Children: The measure is articulated with the following MISNI indicators: DI5, DI13 and JE6. By implementing performance-based navigation (PBN) and optimizing routes, approaches and departures, it reduces fuel consumption and aviation emissions. At the same time, by making the air transport system more efficient and low-carbon, it generates benefits.

Analytical basis: Performance-based navigation (PBN) reduces inefficient trajectories, fuel consumed and emissions associated with air transport, strengthening the transition toward a more sustainable mobility system. The measure promotes transparent technical governance, improves national operational efficiency, drives technological modernization of the transport sector and generates climate, economic and public health benefits, contributing to positive peace and sustainable development.
