COMMERCE, INDUSTRY AND TOURISM SECTOR

Climate Commitment NDC3A35:

Title: Adaptation strategies for small, medium and large enterprises. Adaptation target: Implement by 2030, in at least ten percent (10%) of companies in the prioritized sectors with strategies, actions or climate change adaptation projects, covering small, medium and large enterprises according to their level of vulnerability. Responsible entities: Ministry of Commerce, Industry and Tourism.

Strategic allies: Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (DAASU and ONVS). National Administrative Department of Statistics (DANE). National Association of Industrialists (ANDI). National Planning Department (DNP). Ministry of Sciences, Technologies and Innovation (Minciencias). Business associations, companies and business support entities.

Support needs:

1. Climate finance:

  • Fondos de crédito verde y líneas preferenciales para la adaptación.
  • Cofinanciación público-privada para planes de adaptación empresarial.
  • Access to international resources for SMEs.
  • Concessional resources for enabling projects to scale business adaptation (e.g., platforms, baseline studies).

2. Capacities:

  • Training for businesspeople and other key actors in climate risk analysis, adaptation and nature-based solutions (NbS).
  • Institutional strengthening of MinCIT, its affiliated and linked entities and chambers of commerce.
  • Redes de aprendizaje empresarial sobre buenas prácticas de adaptación.
  • Strengthening of project structuring (templates) and knowledge exchange with other partners.

3. Technology:

  • Plataformas digitales de diagnóstico de vulnerabilidad empresarial vinculadas al SIIVRA.
  • Innovation in resilient technologies for supply chains.
  • Integration of Early Warning Systems (SAT) for tourism, commerce and manufacturing.
  • Electronic tool for exchanging and showcasing adaptation measures, including nature-based solutions (NbS).
  • Herramientas para identificar y gestionar los riesgos asociados al recurso hídrico, relacionados en las operaciones empresariales y de turismo.

4. Planning, governance and others:

  • Integration of adaptation criteria in sectoral policies or strategies.
  • Evaluation of results and impact of adaptation plans, programs and projects and NbS for continuous improvement and scaling.
  • Strengthening of governance and intersectoral and public-private cooperation mechanisms in adaptation.

Other SIIVRA dimensions: Food Security and Agricultural Production, Risk Management.

  • Human Rights: Reduces risks to decent work, livelihoods and working conditions in exposed sectors.
  • Gender: In accordance with the gender commitments of the commerce, industry and tourism sector in the Colombia Gender and Climate Change Action Plan and with Resolution 273 of 2021, through which the Committee on Gender Affairs and Women's Economic Empowerment of the Commerce, Industry and Tourism Sector is created, which recognizes the need to coordinate efforts on coordination and measures aimed at promoting gender equality, women's economic empowerment and
  • Children: Contributes to DI1, DI13 and JE5 by protecting family jobs, essential supply chains and reducing environmental risks that affect children and adolescents.

Analytical basis:

The measure positions business adaptation as an axis of economic and territorial stability, integrating incentives, information and tools to reduce vulnerabilities in key sectors, with a differential and territorial approach.

9. Energy Sovereignty and Democratization

Reduce the risk of the national energy system and local energy systems and the differentiated energy vulnerability of territories and economic sectors, through the development of adaptation actions aimed at strengthening the adaptive capacity of the sector's value chains to guarantee the right to energy and the provision of an essential service for inclusive human and economic development of the regions.

MINING AND ENERGY SECTOR

Climate Commitment NDC3A36:

Title: Monitoring system for the reduction of climate vulnerabilities and risks associated with energy. Adaptation measure: By 2035 implement the Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) System of the mining-energy sector that allows identifying, reporting and reducing climate vulnerabilities in value chains and in the territories, with public access to information under the Escazú Agreement and articulated with SIIVRA/SNICC.

Responsible entities: Ministry of Mines and Energy (MME)

Strategic allies: Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development. Institute of Hydrology, Meteorology and Environmental Studies (IDEAM). National Infrastructure Agency (ANI). National Roads Institute (INVIAS).

Support needs:

1. Climate finance:

  • Supplementary resources for the national energy sector budget to consolidate the system in synergy with international cooperation resources.
  • Access to climate transparency funds and international financing (e.g., Climate Transparency Partnership).
  • Cofinanciación con empresas privadas de energía y minería para asegurar sostenibilidad financiera.

2. Capacities:

  • Technical training in M&E methodologies for adaptation and climate risk.
  • Institutional strengthening in transparency and data access (aligned with the Escazú Agreement).
  • Creación de equipos especializados en MME y entidades reguladoras.

3. Technology:

  • Integration of climate information, sectoral risks and the energy dimension of territories in SIIVRA.
  • Development of interoperable platforms for sharing data between the Ministry of Mines and Energy, IDEAM, UPME and private companies.
  • Integration of climate risk information in value chains and in the energy dimension of territories in information platforms associated with sector planning.

4. Planning, governance and others:

  • Incorporación del sistema M&E en el PIGCCS de Energía.
  • Coordination with the CICC and regional nodes to territorialize monitoring.
  • Vinculación de comunidades locales y territorios étnicos en la definición de riesgos prioritarios
  • Incorporation of M&E system information in UPME and other sector-affiliated entities' planning processes and instruments.

Other SIIVRA dimensions: Todas.

  • Human Rights: Reinforces the exercise of the right to information and access to energy as an essential service.
  • Gender: In accordance with the gender commitments of the Ministry of Mines and

Energy in the Colombia Gender and Climate Change Action Plan; and with the gender guidelines of the mining-energy sector which establish that the Ministry of Mines and Energy, through the Office of Environmental and Social Affairs or whoever performs its functions, every 6 months will monitor progress in the implementation of activities in charge of the different departments of the Ministry and attached entities, companies and associations, based on the proposed indicators.

Children: Contributes to DI5, DI6, DI9, DI12, JE2, JE3 and JE9 by anticipating risks that compromise key basic services for children and adolescents (health, education, water, protection), and guarantees the participation of children, adolescents and youth and their right to access to information.

Analytical basis:

The M&E system allows preventive decisions on energy infrastructure and operations, articulating climate information, risk and energy justice, and strengthening public accountability.

  • Children: Contributes to DI9, DI12, JE2 and JE3 by protecting essential services for children and adolescents (education, health, water, protection) against climate events and ensuring their participation.

Climate Commitment NDC3A39:

Title: Strengthening of National and Regional Transmission capacity to reduce territorial energy vulnerability. Adaptation measure: By 2035, implement adaptation measures based on climate change risk analysis to increase the adaptive capacity of the National Transmission System (STN) and Regional Transmission Systems (STR) of the national interconnected system, as well as the distribution network, with intermediate implementation milestones by 2030 and alignment with the National Energy Plan.

Strategic allies: Ministry of Finance and Public Credit. Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (DAASU). National Planning Department (DNP). Mining-Energy Planning Unit (UPME). ISA Intercolombia (electricity transmission). National Dispatch Center (CND) – XM.

Support needs:

1. Climate finance:

  • National sector budget for energy transition and resilience in synergy with international cooperation resources.
  • Access to international funds (GCF, multilateral banks) for adaptive pathways.
  • Financial instruments from the national adaptation fund to reduce financial burdens on users or avoid passing costs on to end users.
  • Cofinanciación público-privada con empresas de energía.
  • Regalías (Habilitante para infraestructura energética).

2. Capacities:

  • Training in costing or financial quantification of adaptation of the country's transmission system.
  • Training in national and international instruments and funds to enable climate-energy financing.
  • Institutional strengthening for regulatory and administrative enablers for MME, UPME and CREG.
  • Development of capacities in public entities and private companies for formulating strategic projects aimed at renewing the transmission network.
  • Development of capacities in public entities and private companies for formulating strategic projects aimed at renewing the distribution network.
  • Development of territorial capacities in regions with extractive dependence.

3. Technology:

  • Renovación de infraestructura antigua y promoción de interconectividad y redundancia.
  • Innovación en infraestructura energética resiliente y diversificación tecnológica.
  • Physical and digital modernization with territorial planning of degraded distribution infrastructure.
  • Information and monitoring platform on the status of transmission networks at national and regional level.

Loss and Damage Goals and Measures

Colombia has taken a decisive step in strengthening its climate policy by recognizing that losses and damage arising from climate change can no longer be treated as a residual impact, but as a structural dimension of the environmental crisis facing the country. Far from limiting itself to repairing infrastructure or recovering livelihoods, this perspective incorporates profound and, in many cases, irreversible impacts: the degradation of ecosystems, the loss of cultural heritage and the This approach is based on the country's institutional advances, which have integrated human climate mobility and risk management as essential elements for adaptation. Based on frameworks such as the Long-Term Climate Strategy and the National Climate Change Adaptation Plan, Colombia proposes the construction of a National Loss and Damage Framework that articulates sectoral, territorial and financial capacities. With this, it seeks not only to anticipate inevitable impacts, but also to At this stage, the loss and damage portfolio is materialized in two initial commitments, conceived as a basis for the progressive development of the National Loss and Damage Framework.

ENVIRONMENT SECTOR

Climate Commitment NDC3PYD01

Loss and damage measure: By 2030 implement the methodology for Post-disaster Environmental Damage Assessment and Needs Analysis (EDANA), ensuring that 100% of disaster events registered in the National Natural Parks of Colombia system are assessed in an articulated manner with national disaster risk assessment processes and considering impacts on livelihoods, ecosystem services, cultural identity and biodiversity. Responsible entities: National Natural Parks.

Strategic allies: National Unit for Disaster Risk Management (UNGRD). Environmental Authorities. Scope: Although the country has made progress in the development of analyses of damages and economic losses, it is still necessary to evaluate non-economic damages and losses. For this reason, the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development has been developing the methodology for Post-disaster Environmental Damage Assessment and Needs Analysis (EDANA-C), whose main objective is to This tool was designed to be implemented by Environmental Authorities in their complementary and subsidiary role to the work of mayor's offices and governorships, providing a crucial technical input for post-disaster recovery planning with environmental sustainability criteria.

The adoption of EDANA-C was also influenced by the country's experience in dealing with extreme climate phenomena, such as those associated with 'La Niña'. These events highlighted the vulnerability of ecosystems and the need for mechanisms to quantify their impacts and define priority actions for their restoration.

Since its implementation, EDANA-C has become a fundamental instrument for guiding investments and efforts toward a recovery that not only restores pre-disaster conditions, but also fosters the resilience of territories and their ecosystems against future threats. Furthermore, in 2024 the methodology was updated to incorporate more indicators that allow the assessment of environmental damage.

Support needs:

1. Climate finance:

  • Territorialization of policies and incentives for resilient infrastructure.
  • Multi-year investment in actions and development of territorialized adaptive technologies in energy.
  • Access to international funds for growing energy access in ZNI and SIN for just transition, resilience and energy justice.
  • Co-financing with private sector companies for scaling actions aimed at growing energy access as territorialized adaptive measures.

2. Capacities:

  • Development of technical capacities in municipalities and governorships for implementing actions aimed at growing energy access, strengthening programs and projects for energy management.
  • Training of technical teams in public and private entities on energy solutions for territorial resilience.
  • Institutional strengthening in MME, CREG and UPME to lead and enable growing energy access actions.
  • Inclusion of local communities and social organizations in programs and projects for the social and community management of energy.
  • Strengthening of capacities and adaptive culture in territories around energy.

3. Technology:

  • Innovación en infraestructura energética resiliente y diversificación tecnológica.
  • Innovation in energy efficiency technologies.

4. Planning, governance and others:

  • Incorporation of reforms into regulatory and sectoral planning instruments and in the PIGCCS.
  • Coordinación interinstitucional a través de la CICC y nodos regionales.
  • Local community and ethnic territory participation in defining energy solution projects.

Other SIIVRA dimensions: Infraestructura, Gestión del Riesgo de Desastres, Hábitat Humano,.

Annex 5. Means of Implementation Measures

Introduction

The Implementation Means included were formulated by the responsible entities and are presented in a structured manner to reflect the priorities, gaps and institutional capacities required to implement the NDC. This portfolio is neither exhaustive nor closed; it is conceived as a dynamic and updatable instrument that can be expanded as new needs are identified, additional capacities are developed, or cooperation and climate finance opportunities become available.

It is important to note that the mitigation, adaptation and loss and damage measures included in NDC 3.0 detail, specifically, their support requirements in finance, technology, capacity strengthening, planning and governance, which feed and complement this Implementation Means portfolio. The articulation between both levels — the sectoral and the institutional enabling — allows for consolidation of a comprehensive vision of the needs for climate action.

The Annex is organized into four main components: climate finance; technology development and transfer; capacity building; and climate transparency. In this last case, transparency is addressed as a cross-cutting enabler that requires the development of technical and institutional capacities, climate information systems and monitoring mechanisms necessary to implement the NDC, in line with the Strengthened Transparency Framework of the Paris Agreement.

For each measure, the responsible entity, strategic allies and scope are identified, as well as their differentiated contributions to mitigation and adaptation where applicable. This structuring allows for a clear framework for planning, monitoring and articulating national and international efforts that support the implementation of NDC 3.0 and strengthen its progressive execution over time.

1. Climate Finance

National Planning Department – DNP

By 2030 implement one hundred percent (100%) of the National Climate Finance Strategy (ENFC). IMPLEMENTATION MEANS. Responsible Entity: National Planning Department. Strategic allies: Presidential Agency for International Cooperation of Colombia, Business Development Bank of Colombia – Bancóldex, National Administrative Department of Statistics, National Planning Department, Fund for the Financing of the Agricultural Sector, Institute of Hydrology, Meteorology and Environmental Studies.

By 2029, Colombia will have guidelines for the formulation, implementation and monitoring of Sectoral and Territorial Climate Finance Plans (PFC), aligned with the National Climate Finance Strategy, to contribute to the achievement of the country's climate targets. Responsible entities: National Planning Department. Strategic allies: Members of the Financial Management Committee. Scope: This measure seeks for sectors and territories to have tools to identify resources for Ministry of Finance and Public Credit. National Planning Department. Scope: By 2030, Colombia will have the National Tradable Emissions Allowance Program (PNCTE) designed, regulated and operational as one of the economic instruments created by Law 1931 of 2018 for the management of climate change. The PNCTE will operate as a carbon pricing mechanism based on tradable emission allowances, complementary to the carbon tax and other financial instruments provided for in Through the PNCTE, economic signals will be established that incentivize the cost-effective reduction of GHG emissions in covered sectors and promote technological innovation and transition toward low-carbon productive activities, in coherence with the National Climate Finance Strategy (ENFC).

Carbon pricing mechanisms: Carbon tax

IMPLEMENTATION MEANS Responsible entity

Implementation — Responsible entity Ministry of Finance and Public Credit Strategic partners: Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development, DIAN Descripción:

Ministry of Description: It is an instrument

Description: It is an instrument that seeks to discourage the use of fossil fuels and stimulates their more efficient use. Colombia has already been implementing the carbon tax since 2017,

framed under Law 1819 of 2016 and amended by Law 2277 of 2022. The carbon tax falls on the equivalent carbon content of fossil fuels that are used for combustion.

Likewise, it is established that the revenue has a specific earmarking since its creation, with uses such as conservation of strategic ecosystems; management of coastal erosion; conservation of water sources; Environmental Payment for Services schemes; promotion of the conservation and sustainable use of biodiversity; financing of the country's NDC targets and measures; and Substitution of Illicit Crops.

solid waste treatment

Reduction of food carbon footprint: Short production and Indigenous water management and soil conservation practices, consumption circuits, characteristic of Indigenous systems, minimize emissions associated with transport, packaging and refrigeration of food. Such as terraces and rotation systems, improve the capacity of agroecosystems to retain water and nutrients under changing climatic conditions.

Preservation of adaptive knowledge: The revitalization of Indigenous Knowledge Systems ensures the intergenerational transmission of adaptation strategies proven over time, strengthening community response capacity.

Mitigation

Adaptation

Protection and expansion of carbon sinks: Women, as direct managers of natural resources in their territories, can implement conservation and restoration actions that maintain and increase carbon reserves in forests and soils. Promotion of low-carbon practices: Women environmental caregivers transmit low-carbon practices within their communities and ancestral adaptation techniques that are a safeguard against climate change.

Strengthening of water and food security: Women, primarily responsible for the provision of water and food, acquire capacities to manage these resources more efficiently and resiliently in the face of climate variability. Reduction of the differential vulnerability of women: The intersectional approach addresses the specific burdens that climate change imposes on women and provides them with concrete tools to face them, building more equitable resilience.

Mitigation

Adaptation

Reduction of waste sector emissions: Green Business-certified operations promote the recycling and valorization of waste, avoiding emissions from the waste sector. Conservation of carbon sinks: Businesses based on conservation, restoration or agroforestry systems protect and expand the country's natural carbon sinks. Transformation of value chains: By strengthening green chains, the carbon intensity of products and services throughout their entire life cycle is reduced.

Water and food resilience: Certified activities incorporate conservation agriculture and efficient water use, which reduce vulnerability to climate variability. Conservation of key ecosystem services: Verified activities contribute to the protection of water sources, soils and biodiversity, which are the basis for ecosystem-based adaptation. Diversification of sustainable livelihoods: By promoting local green economies, less climate-vulnerable economic alternatives are created, especially for rural communities.

Mitigation

Adaptation

Effective protection of carbon sinks: Coordination with Indigenous governments, the main guardians of vast forested areas, allows the joint design and implementation of conservation strategies that avoid deforestation and degradation of these critical carbon reservoirs. Strengthening of forest governance: Institutional coordination facilitates the implementation of schemes such as Indigenous REDD+ with ancestral territorial management, ensuring that mitigation actions in Indigenous territories respect their autonomy. Valuation of low-carbon lifestyles: Integration of traditional knowledge can promote Indigenous production and territorial management models that are inherently sustainable and with a low carbon footprint. Conservation of agrobiodiversity as a climate safeguard: The coordination strengthens the strategies of Indigenous peoples to conserve resilient native crop varieties, which constitute a vital genetic bank for food security in the face of climate variability.

Integration of traditional knowledge in planning: Coordination allows the systematic incorporation of ancestral knowledge about natural signals, ecological cycles and resilient varieties in national and territorial adaptation plans. Strengthening of territorial resilience: Coordination facilitates the implementation of Nature-Based Solutions (NbS) that combine technical-scientific knowledge with a rights-based approach, creating more robust adaptation strategies. Reduction of differential vulnerability of Indigenous peoples: The measure ensures that adaptation policies and actions respond to the specific vulnerabilities and priorities of Indigenous peoples, who are disproportionately affected by climate change.

Responsible entities: Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development. Strategic allies: Indigenous Governments. Scope: The aim is to increase care and protection strategies consolidated as a climate justice and just transition action. The Indigenous Territories and Territorialities of Colombia will be recognized in national planning as strategic areas of climate management, through the issuance of a normative instrument that frames the mitigation and adaptation measures developed there in accordance with their own planning and land-use management instruments, with safeguards for the territorial rights of Indigenous peoples.

Mitigation

Adaptation

Protection of carbon sinks through community management: The territorial care strategies implemented by communities will help conserve forests, peatlands and other carbon-storing ecosystems. Strengthening of low-carbon local economies: Community strategies will promote sustainable livelihoods that do not depend on high-carbon footprint activities. Prevention of emissions from environmental degradation: Community protection of the territory will prevent the foregoing, in accordance with their own planning and land-use management instruments, with safeguards for the territorial rights of Indigenous peoples. Formalized protection of carbon sinks: Recognition of Indigenous territories as climate management areas ensures long-term conservation of forests, peatlands and other carbon-rich ecosystems under their custody. Institutionalized prevention of deforestation: The integration of their own land-use plans into national planning establishes formal barriers against extractive and land-use change activities that generate emissions in Indigenous territories.

Strengthening of community protection systems: The strategies developed will allow communities to collectively prevent and respond to extreme climate events. Specific protection of women and vulnerable groups: The strategies will include specific mechanisms to reduce the differential vulnerability of women to climate impacts. Conservation of water sources and food: Communities will have resilient systems to ensure access to water and food. Institutionalization of ecosystem-based adaptation: Formal recognition allows Indigenous territorial management practices, proven over centuries as adaptation strategies, to be integrated into national climate change policies. Strengthening of ecological connectivity for resilience: The climate management of Indigenous territories at landscape scale maintains the connectivity of ecological corridors essential for the adaptation of species and ecosystems.

Colombian Institute of Family Welfare

Strengthen community capacities, the recovery of ancestral knowledge and the implementation of sustainable and territorially adapted production, distribution and consumption strategies, enabling the integration of food sovereignty into care for early childhood, children, adolescents, youth, families and communities. Responsible entities: Colombian Institute of Family Welfare (ICBF). Strategic allies: National Family Welfare System.

The operational scope is structured around three pillars: 1) The promotion of autonomous and agroecological production through community and family gardens that prioritize native seeds and sustainable soil and water management; 2) The strengthening of traditional knowledge and food biodiversity through community seed banks and exchange networks that preserve biocultural heritage; and 3) Food and climate education through pedagogical processes that integrate knowledge.

Strategic allies: Ministry of Health and Social Protection, UNICEF, National Unit for Disaster Risk Management, Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development, Territorial Health Secretariats. Scope: This measure aims to strengthen psychosocial well-being and emotional coping capacities of early childhood, children, adolescents, youth, families and communities in priority territories with high incidence of climate emergencies such as La Guajira, Chocó.

  • The approach is based on intercultural dialogues that articulate community knowledge with the technical competencies of the health sector, to specifically address impacts such as eco-anxiety, post-disaster stress and ecological grief stemming from environmental displacement, loss of livelihoods and climate disasters.

The operational scope is realized through two main strategies: 1) The creation of safe and pedagogical spaces in ICBF settings where girls, boys, adolescents and communities can express and transform their emotions in the face of the climate crisis, fostering community resilience and mutual care; and 2) The joint development of institutional capacities, guides and specific psychosocial care pathways for climate emergencies, articulated with early warning systems.

Mitigation Strengthening of collective climate action

Adaptation Strengthening of psychosocial resilience:

Mitigation: Strengthening of collective climate action: Community mental health is a collective action: Community mental health is a requirement for effective social organization, which is the basis for local-level mitigation initiatives.

Adaptation Strengthening of psychosocial resilience: Emotional coping capacities allow people and communities to recover more quickly and effectively after an extreme climate event, which is one of the pillars of adaptation. Prevention of a cyclical process of vulnerability: Attention to climate mental health prevents trauma or chronic stress arising from a disaster from permanently weakening the capacity of communities to respond to future events. Building social capital for community transition: An emotionally resilient community is better prepared to take on the behavioral and technological changes that decarbonization requires, without falling into denial or resistance. Protection of the social fabric: By fostering mutual care and safe spaces, the measure preserves community support networks, which are the first line of defense and adaptation against any threat. Facilitation of adaptive decision-making: A person or community with preserved mental health can better assess risks, plan long-term and proactively participate in implementing adaptation measures, rather than reacting from panic or paralysis.

The training process will be based on playful, pedagogical and artistic approaches; green competencies, critical thinking and environmental awareness will be developed, avoiding the creation of new structures and strengthening existing participatory spaces. Ministry of Health and Social Protection

By 2030, calculate the greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions baseline for the health sector with a maximum prior period of three (3) years. Responsible entities: Ministry of Health and Social Protection. Strategic allies: Health Care Without Harm Organization. Description: Within the framework of Law 1931 of 2018, regarding climate change management, it was established that the country's sectors, including the health sector, must formulate and implement a Comprehensive Sectoral Climate Change Management Plan.

In this way, through the partnership that has existed since 2022 between the Ministry of Health and Social Protection and the international organization Health Care Without Harm, whose main objective is to estimate the carbon footprint of the Colombian health system, this measure aims to have a GHG emissions baseline for the health sector.

To achieve this objective, a first step has already been taken, using the tool developed by Health Care Without Harm for calculating the carbon footprint of health establishments, known as climate impact monitoring, obtaining the first estimate for 264 public and private hospitals in different regions of the country.

In order to continue measuring the carbon footprint, it is necessary to have a baseline of greenhouse gases (GHG) for the health sector, in order to plan and establish climate change mitigation actions that allow reducing emissions relative to the baseline. In this sense, this measure seeks to calculate the carbon footprint of the sector, using the climate impact monitoring tool with data from the period between 2027 and 2029.

  • A guide for measuring the carbon footprint of the health sector is developed and published, adapting the methodology used in the climate impact monitoring tool of the international organization Health Care Without Harm. (2027).
  • Identification and registration of health sector actors to be included in the representative sample of the health sector, including Public and Private Health Service Provider Institutions, national and subnational sector entities (2028).
  • Consolidated data from mandatory sources, corresponding to years 2027 and 2028, is available from health sector actors included in the representative sample (2029).
  • The greenhouse gas emissions baseline for the health sector and its preliminary analysis document are available (2030).

Regional Autonomous Corporation of the Negro and

Nare River Basins – CORNARE

By 2030, implement and consolidate in the CORNARE jurisdiction the Integrated Monitoring, Reporting, Verification, Evaluation and Learning System (MRV–MEA), articulated with the PIGCCTs, local disaster risk management plans, that enables monitoring of emissions, mitigation and adaptation measures, as well as generating learning and institutional strengthening processes in regional climate management. Responsible entity: Autonomous Regional Corporation of the.

The MRV system includes:

  • An Emission Reduction Potential (PRE) calculator, which estimates in real time the contribution of each implemented mitigation measure — for example, fuel substitution, energy efficiency, waste utilization or restoration — to the achievement of local, sectoral and national emission reduction targets.
  • A mitigation action monitoring module linked to the Comprehensive Territorial (PIGCCT) and Sectoral (PIGCCS) Climate Change Management Plans, enabling quantification of progress toward regional decarbonization.

In addition, the system integrates the Monitoring, Evaluation and Learning (MEA) Module on Adaptation, which evaluates the effectiveness of measures in terms of reducing climate vulnerability and strengthening adaptive capacity across seven territorial dimensions (biodiversity, human habitat, water resources, infrastructure, health, disaster risk and food sovereignty).

In this way, CORNARE provides a comprehensive and replicable solution that unites mitigation and adaptation under a single monitoring framework, strengthening regional climate governance and offering the country a pilot platform to consolidate the future National Climate Change Monitoring and Evaluation System (M&E). Climate action contribution. Mitigation Adaptation. Evaluation of adaptive effectiveness: The system measures whether the implemented actions tangibly reduce climate vulnerability.

Optimization of investment in resilience: By identifying which adaptation measures work and which do not, the system allows reorienting resources toward the most effective interventions to protect the population and productive systems.

Strengthening of governance for adaptation: Systematic monitoring across seven territorial dimensions requires intersectoral coordination and provides a common information base for adaptation planning.

Annex 6. Synergy Between the Climate Agenda and the

biodiversity agenda

Colombia recognizes that the climate crisis and biodiversity loss are interdependent dimensions of the same socio-ecological challenge. This understanding guides a national vision in which climate action and the protection of nature are addressed jointly, taking advantage of the mutual benefits that arise when both agendas converge.

This annex offers a strategic perspective on how this synergy is expressed in the territory, in public policy instruments and in various productive sectors. Its purpose is to make visible the interdependence between the climate change and biodiversity agendas, highlighting the approaches, areas of convergence and transformative synergies that strengthen the country's resilience and enhance the results of the Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) 3.0 and the Biodiversity Action Plan.

As a complementary non-binding document, this annex presents an integrated vision that guides technical and political dialogue toward more coherent, efficient and ecosystem-based action. With this, Colombia reaffirms its commitment to the biodiversity and climate change agendas, driving low-carbon, nature-positive and community well-being centered development.

INTEGRATION AND SYNERGY FRAMEWORK

Marco international

The international community recognizes that the climate crisis, biodiversity loss and land degradation are interdependent manifestations of the same planetary crisis, associated with unsustainable development models and production and consumption patterns that undermine people's well-being and the stability of ecosystems. In this context, policy coherence among the three Rio Conventions — the Convention

Framework of the United Nations on Climate Change (UNFCCC), the Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD) and the United Nations Convention to Combat Desertification (UNCCD) — recognize the importance of increasing efforts to achieve more effective governance and more coordinated, integrated and coherent multilateral action.

Within the framework of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), recent advances have strengthened convergence between the climate, biodiversity and land agendas. The cover decisions of COP25, COP26 and COP27 reinforced the essential role of nature and ecosystem-based approaches for mitigation, disaster risk management and adaptation to climate change, laying the foundations for a more integrated approach to climate action.

In recent years, both the Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD) and the Convention to Combat Desertification (UNCCD) have reinforced calls to deepen cooperation among the three Rio Conventions, recognizing the interdependence between biodiversity, land degradation and climate change, as well as the need to promote integrated actions, strengthen collaboration among focal points and prioritize nature-based solutions and ecosystem approaches.

At the political level, the Presidencies of the three Conventions have promoted the Rio Trio mechanism, aimed at aligning priorities and strategic messages, strengthening cooperation between presidential offices and promoting greater synchronization of COP processes, thus contributing to more coherent joint action within the framework of the Conventions of Rio. In addition, the Joint Declaration on Climate, Nature and People, launched during COP28, reaffirms that it is not possible to achieve the objectives of the Paris Agreement and the Global Biodiversity Framework without simultaneously and in an integrated manner addressing the three crises, and underlines the importance of holistic and evidence-based approaches, as well as broad participation of indigenous peoples, local communities, women, youth, the private sector and vulnerable groups.

Other multilateral forums, such as the United Nations Environment Assembly (UNEA), have consolidated a complementary framework for integration among environmental agendas, by explicitly recognizing the interdependencies between climate change, biodiversity loss, pollution and land degradation, and urging the expanded application of nature-based solutions in a manner coherent with multilateral environmental agreements.

Additionally, forums such as the Baku High-Level Dialogue on Adaptation and the Oceans Dialogue have promoted a common understanding of the importance of integrating biodiversity and ecosystem services into adaptation and resilience strategies.

In this context, Colombia prioritizes the synergy between the climate and biodiversity agendas, which is understood not only as a desirable technical option, but as a necessary condition for achieving the objectives of the Paris Agreement, the Kunming-Montreal Global Biodiversity Framework and the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development.

Marco national

Colombia recognizes that the challenges associated with climate change, biodiversity loss and land degradation are inseparable and must be addressed in an integrated manner. This vision is reflected in the National Development Plan 2022-2026 'Colombia, World Power of Life' which places climate action, biodiversity conservation and land management as structural axes of sustainable development. The plan promotes nature-based territorial planning, the strengthening.

The National Climate Change System (SISCLIMA) constitutes the ideal institutional framework for guiding climate policy coordination in the country; however, the country is working to introduce adjustments to the System's regulations that strengthen its articulation and improve its operability and impact. These adjustments would also consolidate its linkage with the objectives of the climate change and biodiversity agendas, including land management, promoting a coherent approach.

In the area of climate policy, the National Climate Change Policy integrates the principles of the Comprehensive Management of Biodiversity and Ecosystem Services as one of its strategic axes, recognizing that ecosystem conservation and restoration are fundamental to achieving carbon neutrality and strengthening climate resilience. The Long-Term Climate Strategy (E2050) promotes the protection of strategic ecosystems — forests, páramos, wetlands, mangroves, marine coastal ecosystems.

Colombia has progressively incorporated Ecosystem-based Adaptation, nature-based solutions and sustainable territorial management. Similarly, instruments such as the CONPES of 2020 and the 2017 Strategy for Deforestation Control and Forest Management, which addressed the country's main source of emissions, and the National Water Policy, which also suggest the incorporation of strategic ecosystems in territorial planning and the reduction of climate impacts.

The temporal convergence between the update of NDC 3.0 and PAB 2030 represents a strategic opportunity to advance toward greater coherence between their visions, objectives and instruments, respecting the specificity of each agenda and fostering spaces for progressive articulation. However, this process involves challenges inherent to the integration of complex agendas, related to the need to strengthen inter-institutional coordination, generation and management of.

Climate-Biodiversity Integration Framework

Colombia conceives the integration between biodiversity and climate action as a strategic axis to guide its transition toward a resilient, low-carbon and nature-positive development model. In coherence with the 'national vision' established in NDC 3.0, Colombia recognizes that climate stability and biological diversity are interdependent dimensions that underpin the well-being of communities, the integrity of ecosystems and the sustainability of national development.

This integrated framework allows addressing in an articulated manner the challenges associated with climate change and biodiversity loss, reinforcing the adaptive capacity of territories, reducing population vulnerability and enhancing nature's solutions for mitigation, adaptation and sustainable territorial management.

The present NDC 3.0 contributes to this integration by promoting a progressive articulation between the climate and biodiversity agendas, in coherence with the Biodiversity Action Plan 2030, the Kunming-Montreal Global Biodiversity Framework, and the guidance of the Global Stocktake and the Paris Agreement. From a climate justice and equity approach, Colombia advances toward a just, regenerative and territorially differentiated transition, supported by science, traditional knowledge and.

In this way, integration includes different levels; however, in the case of this annex, emphasis is placed on the nexus between biodiversity and climate, which is assumed as a fundamental element for guiding public policies toward a low-carbon, nature-positive and people-centered future, in line with the objectives of the Paris Agreement and the Global Biodiversity Framework.

STRATEGIC APPROACH TO SYNERGY

The integration between the climate change and biodiversity agendas starts from the recognition that natural ecosystems provide essential ecosystem services and sustain community well-being. Scientific evidence and national experience show that the impacts of climate change and biodiversity loss reinforce each other, and that climate and environmental action is more effective when this interdependence is understood.

In this context, the synergy can be interpreted through a set of integrating elements, which allow understanding how the agendas relate, reinforce and provide feedback to each other. These elements, represented in Figure 1, constitute an interpretive framework that allows visualizing how integration between both agendas is expressed in the country and guide the identification of areas of convergence and sectoral synergies.

Figure 1. Structuring elements on the synergy between Climate Change-Biodiversity

Source: Adapted from Gutiérrez, 2025

Element 1. Ecosystem scale as the basis of synergy

Biodiversity at its different levels and particularly Colombian continental and marine-coastal ecosystems are the natural meeting point between the climate and biodiversity agendas. Conservation, which according to the PNGIBSE involves knowledge, restoration, preservation and sustainable use of these ecosystems, generates multiple ecosystem services.

From this perspective, nature-based solutions (NbS) and ecosystem-based approaches highlight the multifunctional role of ecosystems in the territory.

Element 2. Science, knowledge and information

The synergy between the climate change and biodiversity agendas is supported by the existence of solid, comparable and accessible knowledge bases. National and international scientific evidence, the country's environmental information systems and territorial knowledge are fundamental for understanding the dynamics between ecosystems, climate and human well-being.

In this context, interoperability between systems such as the Forest and Carbon Monitoring System (SMByC), the Biodiversity Information System (SIB), the Vulnerability, Risk and Adaptation Information Integration System (SIIVRA) and other sectoral platforms enables the identification of pressures, trends, climate risks and changes in ecosystem status. The articulation between these systems facilitates the generation of common metrics and indicators, useful for interpreting.

This element also recognizes the role of local, community and traditional knowledge, and the importance of building information that dialogues with territorial realities, ancestral practices and the life systems of indigenous peoples, Afro-Colombian, Raizal, Palenquero, peasant communities and local communities.

The integration of science, knowledge and information covers, among others, the following aspects:

The strengthening of the scientific basis for understanding interdependencies between climate and biodiversity; The harmonization of data and methodologies to advance toward comparable indicators between agendas; The identification of common points between monitoring, reporting and verification processes associated with the three Rio Conventions; and the incorporation of human rights, gender equity and climate justice approaches in the production and use of information.

Likewise, the convergence between science, information and knowledge thus contributes to more coherent, evidence-based and territory-sensitive action.

Element 3. Integrating financial mechanisms and international coherence

Financing is one of the areas where the convergence between climate change and biodiversity becomes most evident. Technical literature, international experience and national learning show that investments that simultaneously consider climate and biodiversity objectives can generate joint benefits in mitigation, adaptation, ecosystem conservation and community well-being.

In this framework, financial synergy can be interpreted through several aspects:

Actions in strategic ecosystems — forests, páramos, wetlands, mangroves and marine-coastal ecosystems — can simultaneously contribute to carbon capture, water resilience, ecosystem-based adaptation and biodiversity conservation. Ecological restoration, sustainable land management and nature-based solutions can articulate resources from various climate and biodiversity finance sources. Existing financial instruments.

  • In addition, it is recognized that the integration between climate and biodiversity also depends on advancing toward a coherent international financial system that:

Supports interventions in ecosystems that generate climate and biodiversity benefits; Avoids double counting and ensures transparency and increases financial flows;

  • Recognizes the role of conservation, restoration and natural sinks in
  • climate action; and maintains the integrity and differentiation of associated international commitments
  • to both agendas.

This integrating element highlights that the synergy between climate and biodiversity finance does not imply new commitments, but rather a broader and more coherent interpretation of how different sources, instruments and mechanisms can contribute to climate, desertification and biodiversity outcomes.

Element 4. Integrated and systemic responses

The synergy becomes clearest when public policy, planning and territorial action responses are designed and implemented in an articulated manner. The structural causes of biodiversity loss and climate vulnerability (such as land use change, deforestation, ecosystem degradation and production and consumption patterns) are interrelated, requiring systemic approaches that simultaneously address these challenges.

This element refers to the capacity to connect planning instruments, sectoral policies and territorial decisions under a common vision, where NDC 3.0, PAB 2030, the National Adaptation Plan, the Long-Term Climate Strategy E2050 and the National Development Plan are interpreted as complementary frameworks that can mutually reinforce each other.

The integration of these agendas is realized in an inter-institutional and multi-level governance that articulates productive sectors, environmental entities, territorial governments and communities. This articulation allows addressing challenges systemically, simultaneously considering climate resilience, ecological integrity and sustainable development.

Integrated responses are not limited to aligning measures, but rather to recognizing the functional links between ecosystems, economic sectors and territorial dynamics, facilitating sustainable transitions that simultaneously contribute to climate objectives and biodiversity conservation.

Element 5. International coherence

The decisions adopted at the UNFCCC and the CBD (including the Global Stocktake and the Kunming-Montreal Global Biodiversity Framework) emphasize the need to continue integrating nature and climate in global environmental action. Likewise, mechanisms such as Rio Trio, which articulate the presidencies of the three Rio Conventions, have been strengthening and increasing calls for policy and technical convergence between the agendas.

This integrating element provides a reference framework that reinforces the relevance of advancing toward more integrated analyses and actions at the national level.

AREAS OF STRATEGIC CONVERGENCE

Strategic convergence areas represent fields of action in which the objectives of climate change and biodiversity converge naturally. This convergence occurs when both challenges share structural causes, depend on the same ecosystemic infrastructure or generate simultaneous benefits in terms of climate resilience, ecological integrity and community well-being, in coherence with what is established in national planning instruments, such as the Plan.

Deforestation

The reduction of deforestation constitutes a natural convergence point between climate action and biodiversity conservation. Both agendas recognize the need to advance toward greater coherence between targets and instruments that strengthen forest governance, sustainable forest management, territorial control and the prioritization of critical areas. This articulation allows guiding comprehensive interventions involving different actors, to simultaneously address.

Restoration

Restoration is one of the clearest areas of synergy between the two agendas. In the country, instruments such as the National Restoration Plan, the national strategy to 2026, Law 2169 of 2021, the Biodiversity Action Plan 2030 and the National Development Plan 2022-2026, as well as different instruments establishing measures toward ecosystem restoration, reinforce the ecological integrity of the territory and climate resilience. This convergence is also expressed in territorial actions.

Nature-Based Solutions (NbS) and Ecosystem-Based Adaptation

Ecosystems (AbE)

NbS and EbA are consolidated approaches for integrating both agendas by recognizing the interdependence between natural and human systems, and the role of ecosystems in emission mitigation, climate change adaptation and community well-being and disaster risk reduction based on ecosystems through Ecosystem-based Disaster Risk Reduction – EcoDRR. This is consistent with what is established in the different targets, especially targets 8 and 11 of the framework.

Water, water ecosystems and wastewater management

Water constitutes a natural axis of articulation between the climate and biodiversity agendas. The comprehensive management of water resources — including the protection of ecosystems such as wetlands, rivers and freshwater bodies, and wastewater treatment — allows simultaneously advancing in water quality, reduction of climate risks, water resilience, public health and biodiversity conservation. In this area, a single intervention can contribute to reducing associated emissions.

Sistemas agroalimentarios sustainables

Sustainable agri-food systems are a field of convergence between climate change, biodiversity, food security and rural development. This approach articulates practices such as sustainable production, restoration of productive landscapes and sustainable use of forest ecosystems, integrating climate and biodiversity actions at the cross-sectoral level.

The adoption of agroecological and regenerative practices, integrated water and soil management and the strengthening of capacities of small producers and rural communities contribute to reducing emissions, restoring degraded ecosystems, improving territorial resilience and advancing toward shared objectives of environmental sustainability and social well-being. This is consistent with the promotion of agroecology as a fundamental climate mitigation and adaptation strategy.

Ecosystem representativeness in SINAP

The strengthening of the National Protected Areas System (SINAP) constitutes a field of action where biodiversity and climate resilience priorities converge. Actions oriented toward expanding ecosystem representativeness, strengthening connectivity and consolidating different conservation strategies that benefit biodiversity maintenance, reduce the advance of deforestation and reduce disaster risk — including those developed in ethnic territories —

The integrity and connectivity of protected ecosystems contribute to sustaining essential ecological functions that support territorial resilience to the effects of climate change.

TRANSFORMATIVE SYNERGIES

The integration between the climate change and biodiversity agendas is materialized in a set of sectoral synergies that, taken together, contribute to the transformation of territories and productive models toward 2035. This section does not present a technical cross-referencing between measures nor introduce new commitments; rather, it offers a synthesis of shared contributions from a set of measures already existing in the NDC and PAB, selected for their strategic character.

The synergies are organized by sector, taking as reference the strategic convergence areas previously identified — deforestation, restoration, Nature-Based Solutions and Ecosystem-Based Adaptation, water and water ecosystems, sustainable agri-food systems and ecosystem representativeness in SINAP — and the double materiality approach: climate action that depends on biodiversity integrity, and biodiversity conservation that is strengthened when.

The selected synergies are illustrative and not exhaustive. They reflect those fields of action which, from accumulated technical experience, are recognized as most strategic and transformative for the country's trajectory toward 2035.

ENVIRONMENT SECTOR

In the environment sector, transformative synergies are structured around three main fronts: the reduction of deforestation, ecological restoration and the strengthening of SINAP and ecosystems and their ecosystem services, including blue carbon, particularly mangroves and marine-coastal ecosystems.

The strengthening of the ecological integrity of the territory and the sustainable management of ecosystems — reflected in actions aimed at increasing representativeness in SINAP and expanding ecological restoration processes — align directly with conservation targets for terrestrial and marine areas, as well as with the strengthening of scientific, social and community monitoring networks. Where the conservation and restoration of nature not only safeguards biodiversity.

Sector Agricultura

The agriculture sector advances toward sustainable agri-food systems that integrate mitigation, adaptation, biodiversity and food security. This sectoral transition is based on different approaches such as the updating of the agricultural frontier with a zero-deforestation approach, the declaration of Food Production Protection Areas (APPA) and productive reconversion processes, complemented by multifunctional restoration actions aimed at recovering.

COMMERCE, INDUSTRY AND TOURISM SECTOR

In this sector, the integration of climate change and biodiversity actions is oriented toward consolidating a low-carbon and nature-based economic model. The aim is the implementation of a bioeconomy based on Nature-Based Solutions (NbS), which facilitates the development of biodiversity-centered value chains through biological and circular base economies, adaptation and mitigation to climate change and climate risk management for strengthening.

The transition toward energy efficiency, material circularity and innovation in industrial processes is complemented by the creation of mechanisms to articulate economic sectors of the bioeconomy that develop NbS at the productive level. This articulation drives a new dynamic of sustainable economic development, where bio-based innovations that substitute fossil inputs, transform industrial processes and strengthen business decision-making oriented by.

MINING AND ENERGY SECTOR

In the mining and energy sector, transformative synergies focus on the incorporation of climate change in sectoral planning, on the consolidation of information on climate vulnerabilities in sectoral value chains and in the territories in which the sector is embedded, as well as in the design of territorialized adaptive pathways to promote the implementation of adaptation projects that address the reduction of climate vulnerabilities in territories and ecosystems.

Otros sectors

Housing, Water and Sanitation Sectors. The incorporation of climate adaptation in territorial instruments stands out, where it is articulated with the targets and the establishment of national pacts and alliances that promote criteria of ecosystem conservation, restoration and recovery in territorial planning. This link strengthens territorial planning with an ecological approach, generating more resilient urban and rural environments balanced with their natural surroundings. Additionally.

COUNTRY STRATEGIC MESSAGES

Colombia advances toward a low-carbon and nature-positive development model. The country recognizes that climate change and biodiversity loss are expressions of the same global crisis. Therefore, the integration between both agendas constitutes a national development axis toward a low-carbon, resilient and nature-positive model. This vision reinforces the ecological stability of territories, the sustainability of the economy and the well-being of communities.

The synergy between climate and biodiversity is positioned as a structural principle of the country's environmental policy. The convergence between NDC 3.0 and PAB 2030 strengthens coherence between planning, financing and monitoring instruments. This principle contributes to more efficient public action, avoids duplication and enhances shared results in mitigation, adaptation, conservation and maintenance of ecosystem services.

The country advances in the design and implementation of Nature-Based Solutions (NbS), leveraging the achievements and lessons learned from ecosystem-based approaches to adaptation, mitigation and risk reduction. In order to simultaneously address climate and biodiversity challenges, these approaches promote ecosystem and community resilience, generate social and environmental co-benefits, and improve sustainable development trajectories that integrate.

Integrated financing is a decisive element for enhancing synergy. Colombia promotes financial approaches that recognize the value of ecosystems as infrastructure for climate action and conservation, that avoid double counting and that guarantee the transparency of financial flows. Advancing toward coherent and articulated financing mechanisms allows multiplying the impacts of public and private investment, strengthening international confidence and improving.

The strengthening of science, knowledge and interoperability is fundamental for integrated action. Colombia promotes the harmonization of data, indicators and baselines between the climate and biodiversity agendas, as well as the integration of scientific, technical and ancestral knowledge, with a human rights, climate justice and territorial equity approach. Interoperability between environmental information systems strengthens the comparability and coherence of associated monitoring.

Territory is the operational space for synergy. Colombia advances in ecosystem-based management approaches, restoration, sustainable production and strengthening of productive landscapes, river basins and ecological corridors. This vision recognizes the country's biological and cultural diversity, and underlines the role of indigenous peoples, Afro-Colombian, Raizal and Palenquero communities, peasant communities and local communities as fundamental actors in environmental action.

The articulation between sectors drives productive and territorial transformations. The convergence between climate and biodiversity measures in the environmental, agricultural, industrial, mining-energy, housing and transport sectors reinforces development trajectories that integrate sustainability, competitiveness and resilience. This integration contributes to consolidating more secure, productive and ecologically stable territories toward 2035.

Colombia consolidates its regional leadership in the integration of global environmental agendas. By promoting a vision that articulates the Paris Agreement, the Global Biodiversity Framework and commitments on land degradation, the country demonstrates its commitment to policy coherence, multilateralism and environmental diplomacy. This vision projects Colombia as a reference in the design of integrated public policies, combining climate ambition, nature conservation and social.
